This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project to make the world's books discoverable online.

It has survived long enough for the **copyright** <term>copyright: the legal right that grants the creator of an original work exclusive rights to its use and distribution</term> to expire and the book to enter the **public domain**. A <term>public domain</term> book is one that was never subject to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary country to country. Public domain books are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture and knowledge that's often difficult to discover.

Marks, notations and other <term>marginalia</term> <note>original: "marginalia"; refers to notes, scribbles, or comments written in the margins of a book</note> present in the original volume will appear in this file - a reminder of this book's long journey from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

## Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying.

We also ask that you:

+ *Make non-commercial use of the files* We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for personal, non-commercial purposes.

+ *Refrain from automated querying* Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google's system: If you are conducting research on machine translation, optical character recognition <note>often abbreviated as OCR; the electronic conversion of images of typed, handwritten or printed text into machine-encoded text</note> or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.

+ *Maintain attribution* The Google "watermark" you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.

+ *Keep it legal* Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we can't offer guidance on whether any specific use of any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book's appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liability can be quite severe.

## About Google Book Search

Google's mission is to organize the world's information and to make it universally accessible and useful. Google Book Search helps readers discover the world's books while helping authors and publishers reach new audiences. <meta>The transcription contains a digitized watermark and marginal handwritten notations indicating focus areas for a previous reader. This page serves as a preface to an English translation of the Sanskrit epic, establishing the moral and symbolic framework of the narrative.</meta>

# THE RAMAYANA IN AN ENGLISH GARB.

---

The immortal Epic of <term>Valmiki<gloss>The legendary sage and "first poet" (Adi Kavi) credited with composing the Ramayana</gloss></term> is undoubtedly one of the gems of literature,—indeed, some considering it as the <note>original: "Kohinur"</note> <term>Koh-i-Noor<gloss>A famous large diamond originating in India, used here metaphorically to mean the "mountain of light" or the pinnacle of literary value</gloss></term> of the literary region, which has for centuries, and from a time reaching to the dim and far past been shedding unparalleled and undying halo upon the domain presided over by "the vision and the faculty divine." The burthen of the bard's song is the perpetual contest between good and evil, that is everywhere going on in this mysteriously-ordered world of ours, and which seemingly sometimes ending in the victory of the former, and at others in that of the latter, vitally and spiritually results in the utter overthrow and confusion of evil and in the triumph and final conquest of good. <term>Rama<gloss>The prince of Ayodhya and protagonist, regarded as an incarnation of the god Vishnu</gloss></term> sprung from the bright loins of the effulgent luminary of day <note>Referring to the Solar Dynasty or Suryavansha, the royal lineage descended from the Sun</note>, and bringing his life and being from a long and illustrious ancestry of sovereigns, Rama taking birth among the sons of men for chastising and repressing rampant Iniquity and Injustice, typifies the spirit of good that obtains in this world,—<term>Ravana<gloss>The primary antagonist and king of Lanka</gloss></term>, that grim and terrible Ten-headed one, a <term>Rakshasa<gloss>A powerful supernatural being or demon in Hindu mythology</gloss></term> by virtue of birth, and worthy to be the chief and foremost of <term>Rakshasas</term> by virtue of his many misdeeds and impieties, who challenges and keeps in awe the whole host of the <note>original: "celestials"</note> gods—"to whom the Sun did not shine too hot, and about whom the Wind did not dare to breathe," represents the spirit of unrighteousness and evil. <term>Lakshmana<gloss>Rama's loyal younger brother</gloss></term>, disregarding the pomp and splendours of princely life, to follow his beloved brother Rama into the forest, and cheerfully undergoing there a world of trials and privations, and daily and nightly keeping watch and ward over his brother and his spouse in their cottage,—and <term>Bharata<gloss>Another of Rama's brothers, who refused to take the throne meant for Rama</gloss></term>, stoutly and persistently declining, despite the exhortations of the elders and the spiritual guides, to govern the kingdom during Rama's absence in the forest, and holding the royal umbrella over his <margin>Handwritten bracket and arrow pointing to the description of Bharata’s devotion.</margin>

<summary>This preface introduces the Ramayana as a moral allegory representing the eternal struggle between good and evil, personified by the virtuous Rama and the demonic Ravana. It highlights the epic's historical significance and the selfless devotion of Rama's brothers, Lakshmana and Bharata.</summary>

<keywords>Ramayana, Valmiki, Rama, Ravana, Rakshasa, Lakshmana, Bharata, Indian Epic, Solar Dynasty, Moral Allegory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introduction of the Ramayana's main characters, likely following a description of Rama's brother Bharata (implied by the mention of the sandals). It focuses on the virtues of friendship, loyalty, and the pivotal moment of Rama's exile.</meta>

— brother's sandals, are personations of the *<term>ultimate peak<gloss>original: "ne plus ultra," a Latin phrase meaning the highest point or state of perfection</gloss></term>* of fraternal love, and consummate and perfect ideals of their kind. The righteous <term>Bibhishana<gloss>The younger brother of the antagonist Ravana; he joins Rama’s side, representing the choice of righteousness over family loyalty</gloss></term>, who for Rama's cause forsook his royal brother, and set small store by the splendours of royalty, who suffered no earthly considerations to interfere with his entire and absolute devotion to his friend, embodies in his person the sterling virtues going under the precious name of friendship. The ever-devoted <term>Hanuman<gloss>The monkey-deity and loyal general who helps Rama rescue Sita; he is the supreme symbol of selfless service</gloss></term> glorying in the appellation of Rama's servant,—ever-prompt at the beck and call of his master to lay down his life—is the grandest and loftiest conception of the faithful servant that is to be found in all literature. Shall we say aught of <term>Rama<gloss>The prince of Ayodhya and incarnation of the god Vishnu</gloss></term> and <term>Sita<gloss>Rama's wife, revered as the ideal of womanly virtue and devotion</gloss></term>, or keep silence over themes too sacred for babblement and profane mouthing? The kingdom is astir and alive with the jubilations of the populace at the prospect of Rama's coronation; pennons by thousands are streaming like meteors in the air at the tops of stately edifices; and drums and *<term>panavas<gloss>A type of ancient Indian percussion instrument or small drum</gloss></term>* and other musical instruments are sounding forth the auspicious announcement. The royal household swims in a sea of bliss surging and heaving on all sides. Delight and Joy move about and laugh and talk under the names of <term>Dasharatha<gloss>The King of Ayodhya and Rama's father</gloss></term> and <term>Kausalya<gloss>Rama's mother and the senior queen</gloss></term>. Anon a thunder-clap bursts in the midst of the merry-making, and converts delight into dole, the sounds of laughter and hilarity into loud wails and lamentations issuing from hearts knowing no consolation. All is lost! Rama is to be banished into the woods for fourteen years. He cheerfully makes up his mind and repairs to the forest in consonance with his father's promise. Sita steps forth—a divinity clad in flesh—Sita would follow the fortunes of her lord. She considers it as the height of undutifulness to remain behind, continuing to enjoy the pleasures of the palace, while her beloved Rama is leading a life of toils and privations in the remote woods. The daughter as well as the daughter-in-law of kings, brought up in the lap of luxury and amidst the soft ministrations of those pleasures that pertain to a royal household, Sita, the idol of every one's love and regard, boldly and with alacrity faces all the toils and terrors of a forest-life, in preference to remaining in Dasharatha's residence, bereft of the company of her sweet lord.

All these and various other characters that figure on the fascinating and enchanting boards of <term>Valmiki<gloss>The legendary sage and "first poet" (Adi Kavi) credited with composing the Ramayana</gloss></term>, have been developed

fully and elaborately, and with truth and perfect consistency of portraiture through the length of his gigantic poem of <term>Ramayana<gloss>The "Journey of Rama," an ancient Sanskrit epic attributed to the sage Valmiki</gloss></term>. <margin>A vertical bracket in the right margin highlights the description of the characters.</margin> <term>Ravana<gloss>The powerful, ten-headed demon-king of Lanka who serves as the epic's primary antagonist</gloss></term> standing before us in stupendous proportions as the personation of terror and wrong-doing, before whom the human spirit trembles as <term>Sita<gloss>The heroine of the epic and Rama's wife, revered as the ideal of womanly virtue</gloss></term> in the <term>Asoka wood<gloss>The grove where Sita was held captive by Ravana; named after the Ashoka tree, which symbolizes the removal of sorrow</gloss></term>; the lotus-eyed <term>Rama<gloss>The prince of Ayodhya and the seventh avatar of the god Vishnu, representing the perfect man</gloss></term> self-forgetful and heroic, and possessed of the highest perfections that can adorn humanity, and through the extremes of misery and misfortune ever abiding by righteousness and truth; Sita the best and fairest of her sex, the embodiment of all loveliness and grace physical and mental, she who rose from the sacrificial fire of inspiration—a goddess in all her manifold perfections and unsurpassed excellences, whose name carries in the very mention a world of pathos; the faithful <term>Lakshmana<gloss>Rama's younger brother, who chose exile to remain by Rama's side</gloss></term>, aye cleaving to his brother on the perilous edge of raging battle, and in the dreary forest leading a life lorn and desolate,—these and others whom we forbear reluctantly to name, have been pourtrayed to the life; they are quick with the *Promethean spark* <note>A reference to the Greek myth of Prometheus; here meaning the characters are imbued with a life-giving, divine creative energy</note> and occupy prominent positions in that ideal world brought into being by those mighty intellectual wizards—the poets; and are the perennial fountains of our joy and sorrow, never suffering the good and the beautiful to degenerate into cant and commonplace in our minds. Oh! the privilege of genius.

The influence exercised by the Ramayana upon the Hindus reaching down to the lowest strata of the society, is literally and in actual fact immense: Truly of the Ramayana it can be said in *Baconian language* <note>Referring to the style of the English philosopher Francis Bacon, suggesting the poem is deeply practical and relevant to daily life</note> that it has come home to the business and bosoms of all men. If there is one test which more than another distinguishes the true from the false in Art, it is the circumstances of a work influencing or not influencing life: a work that assimilates itself with the mental constitution of a nation, lending energy to impulse, contributing to clearness of thought, and ennobling and spiritualising the higher emotions and aspirations, must by the very reason of its doing so, be true; while that which fails in doing so, is not the real and genuine thing and can well be spared. The Ramayana has become a household-word in Hindu Society, and expressions embodying the memories of incidents celebrated in the epic, pass current amongst all ranks of the people, being mouhed alike by high and low, by prince and peasant, by the aristocracy

and the nobility of the land, by merchants and mechanics, by cultivators ploughing the field, and by shepherds keeping the flock, by princesses and high-born dames in towering edifices, and the women of the peasantry plying their daily tasks, by religionists and politicians and men of letters,—in short by the community universally. Such absolute and all-commanding and comprehensive sway and influence of literature is perhaps unknown in the West, with the single exception of the Bible. <term>Rama's regime<gloss>Known as 'Rama-Rajya,' this term refers to a golden age of justice and prosperity established during the reign of the god-king Rama</gloss></term> embodies the popular conception of administrative perfection—the ideal of a monarchy. <term>Ravana<gloss>The ten-headed king of Lanka and the powerful antagonist of the epic</gloss></term> is remembered not only in consequence of the prominent part he plays in the <term>Ramayana<gloss>The ancient Sanskrit epic detailing the life and adventures of Prince Rama, attributed to the sage Valmiki</gloss></term>, but also on account of his famous advice to Rama immediately before his death,—namely that the execution of evil projects should be deferred, but that good ones should be promptly executed,—a very sage counsel doubtless, answering partially to Macbeth's observation on hearing of Macduff's escape:

<note>The author quotes William Shakespeare's Macbeth (Act 4, Scene 1) to draw a parallel between the practical wisdom found in English literature and that of the Sanskrit epic.</note>

“The vow concerning the bow-breaking,” <note>original: "Dhanurbhanga," referring to the legendary contest where Rama broke the divine bow of Shiva to win the hand of Sita in marriage</note> applied sarcastically to a case of contumacy. “The war between <term>Rama<gloss>The hero of the epic and seventh avatar of Vishnu, representing the perfect man</gloss></term> and Ravana is matched by that war alone,” <note>original: "Ramaravanayoryuddham ramaravanayoriva"—a famous idiom suggesting that this conflict was so unique it can only be compared to itself</note> “Ravana's family,” “Ravana hath been ruined by domestic disclosure,” <note>A reference to Ravana's brother, Vibhishana, who defected to Rama's side and revealed the secrets of Lanka's defenses</note> “Lanka hath met with destruction in consequence of excess of pride,” “That won't render the Ramayana incorrect,”—these are some of the adages universally current in Hindu society, mixing constantly into common talk. Does not this unequivocally and unmistakably prove that the influence of <term>Valmiki<gloss>The legendary poet and "Adikavi" (first poet) credited with composing the Ramayana</gloss></term> has entered into the pith and marrow of the nation, and vitally sways its intellectual and emotional tendencies?

<term>Sita<gloss>The wife of Rama and heroine of the epic, revered as the ultimate model of womanly virtue</gloss></term> has become the grand exemplar to Hindu women as the embodiment of purity, chastity, and wifely fidelity. She has furnished Hindu ladies with the highest and noblest conception of their duties in their various and manifold relations in life. Her empire is both wide and deep over the hearts of her sex, performing for their eternal behoof spiritual services of incalculable worth. She should be looked upon as one of the greatest teachers of her kind—as a teacher in that highest and best sense in which <term>Christ<gloss>The central figure of Christianity</gloss></term> and <term>Chaitanya<gloss>A 15th-century Indian saint who popularized the Bhakti (devotional) movement</gloss></term>, <term>Nanak<gloss>Guru Nanak, the 15th-century founder of Sikhism</gloss></term> and <term>Socrates<gloss>The foundational philosopher of Western thought from ancient Greece</gloss></term> are called teachers. Ah, who can say how

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Ramayana's cultural impact, moving from its general social influence to its role as a foundational literary source for later poets and its immense value to historians of ancient Indian life.</meta>

v

How many women have turned away in the budding prime of youth from the "primrose path of dalliance," <note>An allusion to Shakespeare’s *Hamlet*, referring to a life of ease and pleasure-seeking.</note> and have in preference followed Virtue, who alone is truly fair—how many, stirred and influenced by the example of her matchless self-sacrifice <note>This likely refers to Sita, the heroine of the Ramayana, who is revered as the ultimate model of virtue and devotion.</note>, have firmly made up their minds to tread in her footsteps? In like manner it may be said of almost all the principal characters of the <term>Ramayana<gloss>The ancient Sanskrit epic detailing the life and adventures of Prince Rama, traditionally attributed to the sage Valmiki</gloss></term>, that they have more or less deeply influenced the thoughts and sentiments of the people.

Further, the Ramayana has been all along a reservoir upon which subsequent writers have drawn ceaselessly. Indeed, most of the succeeding poems owe their subjects to the <term>Mahabharata<gloss>The second of India’s two great Sanskrit epics, detailing the struggle between two groups of cousins, the Kauravas and the Pandavas</gloss></term> and the Ramayana. Not to mention writers of less note, even the great <term>Kalidasa<gloss>Widely considered the greatest poet and dramatist in the Sanskrit language, who lived around the 4th–5th century CE</gloss></term> himself has drunk deep of that fountain. <term>Bhavabhuti<gloss>An 8th-century playwright famous for his Sanskrit dramas; the author specifically refers to his work 'Uttara-rama-charita,' which focuses on the later, more tragic life of Rama</gloss></term>, who is no less celebrated, has composed a poem treating of the latter part of Rama’s life and saturated with a pathos which perhaps no other pen has surpassed.

To the antiquary and the student of oriental literature and manners, a knowledge of the Ramayana is simply indispensable. Together with the Mahabharata, with which it is joined in popular parlance, and with which it goes hand in hand in the scope and variety of information—but to which its superiority is pronounced in point of epic excellence and consistency of execution—the Ramayana constitutes the great repository of wisdom and learning, and the manners and customs of the ancient Hindus. Indeed, the adage current in our society with regard to the Mahabharata, “What is not in *Bharat* (<term>Mahabharat<unclear>a</unclear><gloss>The author uses 'Bharat' as a shorthand for the epic Mahabharata</gloss></term>), is not in *Bharat* (<term>India<gloss>The Sanskrit name for India is 'Bharat'</gloss></term>)” applies to the Ramayana as well. In it, cosmogony and theogony <note>Theories regarding the origin of the universe and the origins of the gods.</note>, the genealogies of kings and princes—of human and extra-human beings, of <term>Asuras<gloss>Powerful, sometimes demonic beings who often oppose the gods</gloss></term> and <term>Danavas<gloss>A race of mythological giants or demons</gloss></term>, of <term>Yakshas<gloss>Nature-spirits who guard hidden treasures</gloss></term> and <term>Gandharvas<gloss>Celestial musicians and singers</gloss></term>, and <term>Siddhas<gloss>Perfected, saint-like beings with supernatural powers</gloss></term> and <term>Charanas<gloss>Celestial bards or singers</gloss></term>; folklore, anecdotes, and legends, and stories half-mythical and half-historical; descriptions of cities existing at a period long before the age of <note>The author uses classical Western references to emphasize the antiquity of the Indian epics.</note> Troy and Memphis, and the chronicles of kings that reigned before Priam and Busiris—all these, with others too numerous to list, have been woven into the mighty web and woof of the magic drapery evolved by the so potent art of <term>Valmiki<gloss>The legendary sage and poet celebrated as the author of the Ramayana and the 'First Poet' (Adi Kavi) of Sanskrit literature</gloss></term>.

Nor is the poem less interesting from a political point of view. It

<meta>This page continues the argument for the Ramayana's cultural relevance, specifically targeting British administrators and scholars as the primary audience for this translation, emphasizing that understanding ancient literature is key to modern governance.</meta>

can hardly be questioned <insert>‘</insert>that all progress to be real and intrinsic
must be developed out of the inherent tendencies of a nation—the
feelings and sentiments and idiosyncracies into which it is born as
well as those which have been stamped on its life and mind by the
stress and exigencies of circumstances, social and political. For
a nation, therefore, to govern another with such an object as that
with which England has taken upon herself the Government of this
country—namely, the progress and advancement of the children
of the soil—a close and wide study of its laws, and institutions,
manners and customs, modes of thought and emotional proclivities,
becomes a thing of paramount interest. It is clear, hence, that to our
rulers an acquaintance with such works as the <term>Mahabharata<gloss>One of the two major Sanskrit epics of ancient India, detailing the struggle between the Pandava and Kaurava dynasties</gloss></term> and the <term>Ramayana<gloss>An ancient Sanskrit epic poem attributed to the sage Valmiki, following Prince Rama's quest to rescue his wife Sita</gloss></term> is most important for wise and beneficent administration.
Nor can it avail one to advance the seemingly unanswerable objection
that treating of as they do a state of society divided from the real
present by a huge and abysmal gulf of time, such works can by no
means serve as useful and faithful guides to the life and manners of
Hindu society existing at this day. “In India,” as <note>Sir Monier Monier-Williams (1819–1899) was a prominent British Indologist and the second Boden Professor of Sanskrit at Oxford University.</note> Professor Monier
Williams justly remarks, “the lapse of centuries is powerless in
effecting radical changes in the foundation and constitution of Hindu
society.” The conservative character of the Hindu nation is proverbial.
In India usages and observances, the rituals prescribed by the scrip-
tures and the customs sanctioned by hoary age, are clung to with a
tenacity that is proof against time and innovation ; and those who think
that England has materially swayed and influenced the social life of the
people, labour, we make bold to say, under a lamentable delusion.

Having regard alike to the surpassing and matchless excellence
of the poem itself both in its dramatic and lyric character, the
extreme interest it possesses for antiquaries and students of oriental
literature, and the importance with which its study is fraught politi-
cally to Englishmen, it is most desirable that the Ramayana should
be presented before the public in an English garb. In consequence
of its being composed originally in <term>Sanskrit<gloss>The primary liturgical and classical language of India, known for its complex grammar and role in sacred literature</gloss></term>, it literally remains a
sealed book <note>A metaphor meaning something that is completely unknown or impossible to understand without a key or translation.</note> to the majority of students. Few are the persons that
can devote their time and energies to master Sanskrit—a language
which of all languages existing on earth, is in consequence of its
highly complex and complicated grammar, as well as the indefiniteness

<meta>This page continues the introduction from page vi, moving from the political importance of Indian epics for British administrators to the linguistic and spiritual challenges of the translation itself. ->vii<-

...which characterizes it on account of its possessing countless synonyms, most difficult to master by a foreigner. Nay, we can perhaps safely go so far as to assert that very few amongst those Western scholars who have devoted their lives to the study of Sanskrit literature, have been able to enter into the spirit of that part of its vocabulary in which are couched those peculiarly Hindu ideas and sentiments that constitute the unique genius of the people. To translate, therefore, such a work as the <term>Ramayana<gloss>The "Journey of Rama," an ancient Sanskrit epic poem exploring themes of duty, love, and the struggle between good and evil.</gloss></term> from the dead and indefinite Sanskrit into the living and real English, is like unearthing a fossil and inspiring it with life; or rather like transferring a light from a bushel in which it has been hidden, to a mountain-top—so that men may behold it and the surrounding objects by help of its grateful rays. Surely, to render a work from a dead tongue into a living language—and especially such a language as English with all its resources—is literally taking it from its narrow and circumscribed sphere of influence, and placing it before the world at large; in fact, making it the common property and heritage of all mankind. The utility, therefore, we flatter ourselves, of this present literary undertaking will recommend itself to all thinking minds without any further elaboration on our part. Indeed, it would argue no common hardihood in him who, despite common sense and reason, would endeavor to maintain that the Epic of <term>Valmiki<gloss>The legendary sage and "first poet" (Adi Kavi) who is traditionally credited with composing the Ramayana.</gloss></term> published in an English garb (always provided that the execution do not fall far short of the requirements) would prove valueless as a contribution to the cause of literature and culture.

In translating the Ramayana into English, we are concerned with a work composed by an illustrious ascetic passing his days in a hermitage in devout contemplation and the practice of rigid austerities and self-denial. It behoves us, therefore, to approach the task in a becoming spirit, with minds duly prepared and fitted. Let us, accordingly, begin by invoking Him whose presence can convert the foulest and the most unclean spot pure and clean, “like the icicle that hangs on <note>A reference to Diana, the Roman goddess of the moon and chastity. This quote is from William Shakespeare's play 'Coriolanus,' used here to emphasize absolute purity.</note> Dian’s temple,” or the hearts and aspirations of the <note>In ancient Rome, these were priestesses of the goddess Vesta, bound by a vow of celibacy and dedicated to maintaining the sacred fire.</note> Vestal Virgins, or pious saints ever engaged in meditating on the Most High. May He in His infinite and eternal grace vouchsafe to purge our minds of all ignoble feelings and motives—may He enable us to find delight in duty and doing His will! May our energies...

never flag while carrying the burden we have taken on our shoulders! May He <note>This likely refers to a divine entity; translators of this era often invoked God or a specific deity like Ganesha to ensure the successful completion of a literary task.</note> enlighten our understanding to interpret aright and convey in clear and adequate language the great thoughts and sentiments of the sublime bard <note>Valmiki, the traditional author of the Ramayana and the "first poet" of Sanskrit literature.</note>,—so that the English <term>Ramayana<gloss>The "Journey of Rama," an ancient Sanskrit epic detailing the life of the prince Rama and his battle against the demon-king Ravana.</gloss></term> being read by all the subjects of a Monarch <note>A reference to Queen Victoria.</note> on whose dominions the sun never sets <note>A famous idiom describing the vast geographical extent of the British Empire at its height.</note>, it may contribute to their constant profit and delight.

# THE RĀMĀYANA <note>The "Journey of Rama," one of the two foundational epics of ancient India.</note>

---

## THE BOOK OF YOUTH <note>original: "BĀLAKĀNDAM"; the first volume of the epic, detailing the early life and trials of the hero Rama.</note>

---

### SECTION I

---

The ascetic Vālmiki asked that best of sages and foremost of those skilled in speech, who is ever engaged in spiritual austerities and <term>Vedic studies<gloss>original: "Vaidika"; referring to the Vedas, the oldest sacred scriptures of the Hindu tradition.</gloss></term>, the celestial messenger Nārada, saying:—“Who at present in this world is truly gifted with every high quality? Who is endowed with great prowess, knows his duty, is grateful, truthful, and firm in his vows? Who is distinguished by his character, and is always devoted to the well-being of all living beings? Who is learned, understands the ways of the world, and knows the art of ruling and pleasing his subjects? And who alone is always pleasant to behold? Who has mastered his own heart, controlled his anger, is blessed with grace, and is free from jealousy? And whom, when he is enraged in battle, do even the gods fear? I have a great desire to hear of such a person. You, O <term>Great Sage<gloss>original: "Maharshi"; a title meaning "Great Seer," reserved for saints of the highest spiritual order.</gloss></term>, can tell me of a man of this description.” 

Hearing Vālmiki's words, Nārada—who is knowledgeable about the three worlds—replied with great enthusiasm:—“Listen! Rare as these qualities you have listed are, I will describe to you a person who possesses them, after having carefully considered your request. There is a man born of the line of <term>Ikshwāku<gloss>A legendary solar dynasty of kings to which Rama belongs.</gloss></term>, known by the name of Rāma. He is of disciplined spirit and possesses extraordinary power—”

<summary>The epic opens with the sage Valmiki questioning the divine messenger Narada about the existence of a perfect man. Narada introduces Rama of the Ikshwaku dynasty as the living embodiment of all ideal virtues.</summary>
<keywords>Valmiki, Narada, Rama, Ikshwaku dynasty, virtues, Balakandam, Sanskrit epic, Vedic studies, Maharshi</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the speech of the sage Nārada, who is answering Vālmiki’s inquiry about the ideal man. It completes the list of the hero Rāma’s extraordinary physical and moral attributes before transitioning into the beginning of the plot involving King Daçaratha’s desire to name Rāma as his successor.</meta>

->### RĀMĀYANA <note>The "Journey of Rāma."</note><-

...matchless; effulgent; endowed with patience; having his senses under control; intelligent; learned in morality; eloquent; crowned with grace; and the slayer of his foes. He is broad-shouldered, possessed of mighty arms, a conch-shaped neck <note>A neck with three graceful lines, considered a sign of beauty and noble character in ancient India.</note>, fleshy jaws, and a broad chest. He is a powerful bowman, the repressor of his enemies, and has muscular shoulder blades. His arms reach down to his knees <note>original: "Ājānubāhu"; a physical characteristic attributed to great heroes and divine incarnations, signifying immense power and reach.</note>; he has a beautiful head and a graceful forehead, and is endowed with excellent strength. 

He possesses symmetrical limbs and a complexion of a cool hue <note>Often described as the color of a dark rain cloud or blue lotus.</note>. He is full of prowess and has a well-developed chest and expansive eyes. He is crowned with auspiciousness and favorable marks; he knows his duty, is firm in his promises, and is always engaged in the welfare of his subjects. Rāma is of accomplished renown, furnished with knowledge, pure in body and spirit, modest towards his superiors, and versed in self-knowledge. He is like <term>Prajāpati<gloss>The "Lord of Creatures," a creator deity in the ancient scriptures.</gloss></term> himself. 

He is blessed with prosperity, protecting all, the destroyer of enemies, and the supporter of all living beings. He is the guardian of the social order, practicing all the duties of his class <note>The warrior or kingly class (Kshatriya).</note> and protecting those who seek his refuge. He is versed in the deep meanings of the <term>Vedas<gloss>The most ancient and sacred scriptures of India.</gloss></term> and the <term>Vedāngas<gloss>Six auxiliary sciences—such as grammar and astronomy—needed to understand the Vedas.</gloss></term>. He is accomplished in archery, gifted with a good memory, and able to rapidly determine the truth of matters. 

He is the darling of all, beyond reproach, of an unvanquished spirit, and discerning. He is proficient in every branch of learning and is always sought out by the good, just as the rivers flow toward the ocean. He is worthy of being honored, regards everyone equally, and is capable of filling the heart with ever-new joy. Crowned with every grace, he increases the happiness of his mother, Kauçalyā. In his depth and gravity, he is like the sea, and in his patience, he is like the <term>Himavat<gloss>The deity of the Himalaya mountains.</gloss></term>. 

In his prowess, he is like the god <term>Vishnu<gloss>The Preserver of the universe.</gloss></term>, and he possesses the pleasing beauty of the Moon. In his anger, he resembles the fire that rages at the end of time; yet in his forgiveness, he is like the Earth. In his generosity, he is like <term>Kuvera<gloss>The god of wealth and the treasurer of the gods.</gloss></term>, and in his commitment to truth, he is like another <term>Dharma<gloss>The god of righteousness and justice.</gloss></term> himself.

“Desirous of doing that which would be acceptable to his subjects, King Daçaratha <note>Rāma's father and the ruler of Ayodhya.</note>, out of the fullness of his affection, wished to—

# THE BOOK OF YOUTH
<note>original: "BĀLAKĀNDAM"; the first section of the Rāmāyana epic, detailing the early life of Rāma.</note>

->[King Daçaratha intended to]<-

install as his associate in the kingdom his beloved and meritorious eldest son, Rāma—a man of infallible prowess, endowed with sterling virtues, and ever intent on the welfare of the people. Beholding the provisions for the installation, the king’s consort, Queen Kaikeyī, who had previously been promised two favors <note>original: "boons"; in ancient tradition, these were solemn, unbreakable promises granted by a king to be redeemed at a later time.</note>, asked for these: namely, the exile of Rāma and the installation of her own son, Bharata. Bound by the ties of duty in consequence of that promise of his, King Daçaratha banished his favorite son Rāma. In pursuance of his father’s promise, and with the view of fulfilling the pleasure of Kaikeyī, that heroic one, commanded by his father, journeyed into the forest.

And on the eve of his departure for the forest, his dear brother Lakshmana—that enhancer of Queen Sumitrā's joy <note>Lakshmana was the son of Sumitrā, the third wife of Daçaratha.</note>, a favorite of his brother, endowed with humility, and displaying great brotherly devotion—followed him out of pure affection. And as the star Rohinī <note>A star in Indian astronomy personified as the Moon's favorite wife.</note> followeth the Moon, Rāma's beloved spouse Sītā followed him. She was born of the line of King Janaka, appeared like an embodiment of Divine power, was as dear to Rāma as life itself, and was always engaged in good acts; furnished with every auspicious mark and the best of wives, she followed her husband.

Having been followed for a great distance by his father Daçaratha and the citizens, Rāma met with the virtuous and beloved king of the <term>Nishādas<gloss>A tribe of hunters and forest-dwellers; their king, Guha, was a devoted ally to Rāma.</gloss></term>. Then, in the company of Guha, Lakshmana, and Sītā, he dismissed his charioteer on the banks of the river Ganges at the town of Sringaverapura. 

Wending then from one woods to another and having crossed many broad rivers, they arrived at the Chitrakūta mountain in accordance with the directions of the sage Bharadwāja. Constructing a romantic abode, the three began to live there as they pleased. They spent their days in delight, even like gods and <term>Gandharvas<gloss>Celestial musicians and spirits in Hindu mythology known for their beauty and skill.</gloss></term>. 

And when Rāma had reached Chitrakūta, King Daçaratha, distressed on account of his son’s absence, passed away and went to heaven, bewailing his son. When Daçaratha had ascended to heaven, the mighty Bharata, although pressed by the <term>Brahmins<gloss>The priestly and scholarly class who served as advisors to the crown.</gloss></term> headed by the sage Vasistha to rule the kingdom, did not wish for dominion. Instead, that hero went after Rāma into the forest with the view of...

# RAMAYANA.

Honoring that venerable man, Bharata came to the high-souled Rāma—whose prowess was rooted in truth—and pleaded with his brother with the utmost respect. Rāma said to Bharata these words: “You also, as one who understands duty <note>original: "dharma-jña"; one who knows the sacred laws and moral obligations of a king.</note>, are now the king.” 

However, the exceedingly generous, illustrious, and mighty Rāma, maintaining a cheerful expression, did not wish to take the kingdom, out of respect for his father’s final command. To settle the matter, he gave his own sandals <note>original: "pādukā"; in Indian tradition, the footwear of a master or deity represents their authority and presence.</note> to Bharata to serve as his substitute on the throne. Despite Bharata's repeated pleas, Rāma firmly refused to return. Finding his desire to restore his brother to the throne unfulfilled, Bharata touched Rāma’s feet in reverence and began to rule from the town of Nandigrāma, where he waited for Rāma’s eventual return.

Once the auspicious Bharata—a man firm in his promises and of controlled senses—had departed, Rāma noticed an influx of citizens and others coming to visit him. To maintain his life as a hermit, he eagerly moved deeper into the Dandaka forest. Upon entering that great wilderness, the lotus-eyed Rāma slew the <term>Rākshasa</term> <note>A class of powerful, shape-shifting supernatural beings, often translated as "demons" or "ogres."</note> named Virādha. During his travels, he visited the forest sages Sarabhanga, Sutikshna, Agastya, and Agastya’s brother. 

Guided by the sage Agastya, Rāma joyfully took possession of several divine weapons: the bow of the god Indra, a set of inexhaustible arrows, a sword, and a quiver. While Rāma lived there among the forest-dwellers, the local sages gathered before him, seeking his protection against the <term>Asuras</term> <note>A broad category of powerful celestial beings or titans who often warred with the gods.</note> and Rākshasas. In the presence of those ascetics, who were as radiant as flaming fire, he promised to destroy those demons in battle. 

It was during his residence there that the demoness Surpanakhā, a resident of Janasthāna who could assume any form at will, was disfigured <note>According to the epic tradition, her nose and ears were cut off by Lakshmana after she attempted to attack Sītā.</note>. Driven by the provocations of Surpanakhā, the demon commanders Khara, Triçira, and Dushana, along with all their followers, attacked. Rāma slew them all in battle, and in that great conflict, fourteen thousand Rākshasas were killed. 

Upon learning of the destruction of his kinsmen, the demon king Rāvana was driven to a frenzy of anger. He sought the assistance of a fellow Rākshasa named Māricha. Even though Māricha strongly tried to dissuade him, saying—

# BALAKANDAM <note>The "Book of Youth," the first book of the Rāmāyana epic.</note>

"You ought not to enter into hostilities with that powerful one. Do you, therefore, O Rāvana, excuse me!" Yet, disregarding those words of his, Rāvana, urged on by Fate <note>original: "daiva"; the inescapable force of destiny or divine will.</note>, went into that asylum <note>A forest hermitage or "ashrama."</note> in company with Māricha. And that one (Māricha) commanding illusions, having drawn far the king's sons (Rāma and Lakshmana), he (Rāvana) carried away Rāma's wife, slaying the vulture Jatāyu. 

And beholding the vulture slain and learning of the carrying off of Mithilā's daughter <note>Sitā, who was the princess of the kingdom of Mithilā.</note>, the descendant of Raghu <note>A title for Rāma, referring to his royal lineage.</note>, deprived of sense, bewailed in grief. And having with unassuaged sorrow burnt the vulture Jatāyu <note>Rāma performed the funeral rites for the bird, treating him with the honor due to a father.</note>, as he was searching for Sitā in that wood, he fell in with a <term>Rākshasa</term> <note>A powerful, often malevolent supernatural being or demon.</note>, Kāvandha by name, of a dreadful and deformed shape. 

Having slain him, the mighty-armed one burnt his body—and thereupon he <note>Kāvandha, who had been a celestial being cursed into a monstrous form.</note> went to heaven. And the Rākshasa addressed Rāma, saying, "Do you, O descendant of Raghu, repair unto the female ascetic, Savari, conversant with all systems of morality." 

Repairing to Savari, that destroyer of foes, gifted with exceeding energy, Rāma, the son of Daçaratha, highly honored by Savari, met with Hanumān on the banks of the Pampā. Then, agreeably to Hanumān's advice, the exceedingly powerful Rāma saw Sugrīva and detailed unto him all, specially touching Sitā. 

Then the monkey Sugrīva, having heard all from Rāma, was well pleased with Rāma and in the presence of fire <note>A sacred witness in Indian tradition, making their alliance a holy and binding covenant.</note>, made friends with him. Then the king of monkeys, out of friendship, mournfully related unto him all about his hostilities with Vāli. And then Rāma vowed that he would slay Vāli. 

Thereupon the monkey described unto Rāghava the prowess of Vāli, and he feared lest Rāma should not prove a match for Vāli. And with the view of convincing Rāghava (as to Vāli's might), Sugrīva showed unto him the huge corpse of Dundubhi <note>A buffalo demon previously killed by Vāli.</note>, resembling a large hill. And looking at the skeleton, Rāma endued with exceeding prowess, smiling the while, with his toe cast it off at the distance of full ninety miles <note>original: "yojanas"; a traditional unit of distance often equal to about 8 or 9 miles.</note>. And with a single mighty shaft he pierced seven palmyra palms, a hill, and the sixth nether world, carrying...

# RAMAYANA <note>The "Great Journey of Rāma," one of the two major Sanskrit epics of ancient India.</note>

conviction into Sugrīva <note>The exiled monkey prince who becomes Rāma's primary ally.</note>. Thus convinced, the mighty monkey well pleased went with Rāma towards the cave called Kishkindhā <note>The capital city and stronghold of the monkey kingdom.</note>. And having arrived there, that best of monkeys, Sugrīva of a tawny and golden hue, set up loud roars. And at those mighty sounds, out came the lord of monkeys <note>Vāli, Sugrīva’s brother and rival.</note>; and having obtained Tārā’s <note>Vāli’s wise wife who had advised him not to fight.</note> consent, came before Sugrīva for battle. Then Rāghava <note>A title for Rāma, meaning "descendant of King Raghu."</note> killed Vāli on the spot with a single shaft. And, in compliance with Sugrīva’s request, having slain Vāli in battle, Rāghava conferred the kingdom on Sugrīva. 

Then that best of monkeys having summoned all the various monkeys, sent them in various directions in search of Janaka’s daughter <note>Sitā, the princess of Mithilā and wife of Rāma.</note>. Then at the suggestion of the vulture Sampāti <note>The brother of Jatāyu; he possessed the vision to see across the ocean.</note>, the mighty Hanumān crossed the salt sea extending for a hundred <term>yojanas</term> <note>An ancient measure of distance; one yojana is roughly 8 to 9 miles.</note>. And arriving at the city of Lankā, ruled by Rāvaṇa, he found Sitā in the midst of an Aśoka wood, absorbed in thought. And then having shown her the sign <note>Rāma's signet ring, given to Hanumān to prove his identity as a messenger.</note>, he related unto her all about the friendship between Rāma and Sugrīva, and having cheered Videha’s daughter <note>Another title for Sitā, referring to the kingdom of Videha.</note>, he smashed the gate of the palace. 

Then having slain five generals, and seven counsellors’ sons, and grinded the heroic Aksha <note>Aksha-kumara, the youngest son of the demon-king Rāvaṇa.</note>, he was bound fast by the arms of Indrajit <note>Rāvaṇa’s eldest son, whose name means "Conqueror of Indra."</note>. Then knowing that in virtue of the grand-sire’s boon <note>A reference to the god Brahma; Hanumān allowed himself to be captured because he knew the weapon used against him would only hold him temporarily due to a divine blessing.</note>, he was free, he forgave those <term>Rākshasas</term> <note>Powerful supernatural beings, often translated as demons or ogres.</note> that were leading him to Rāvaṇa. Then having burnt down the city of Lankā, with the exception of the place occupied by Mithilā’s daughter, the mighty one returned, with the intention of delivering the glad tidings unto Rāma. 

And that one of immeasurable soul having come before the high-souled Rāma, and circled him <note>A gesture of deep respect and worship known as "pradakshina."</note>, addressed him, saying, — “I have truly seen Sitā.” Then accompanied by Sugrīva, Rāma repaired to the shore of the mighty ocean, and with shafts resembling the sun, vexed the deep. Then that lord of rivers — the Ocean — showed himself. And agreeably to the advice of the Ocean, Nala <note>A monkey chieftain and skilled architect, son of the divine builder Vishvakarma.</note> constructed a bridge over the water. By that bridge Rāma went to the city of Lankā, — and slew Rāvaṇa in battle. And having recovered [Sitā...]

### BALAKANDAM <note>The "Book of Youth," the first book of the Ramayana, which introduces the lineage of Rāma and summarizes the entire epic.</note>

Upon seeing Sītā, Rāma experienced great shame <note>This refers to Rāma's concern over public perception, as Sītā had lived in the palace of her captor for a long duration.</note>, and used harsh language towards Sītā in the presence of all. Incapable of bearing this, the chaste Sītā entered flaming fire. Thereupon assured by Agni <note>The Vedic god of fire, who acts as a divine witness to Sītā's purity.</note> as to the sinlessness of Sītā, Rāma became exceedingly pleased, and was honored by all the deities. And at this great act of Rāma's, the three worlds <note>The celestial, earthly, and subterranean realms.</note> with all that was mobile and immobile in them, as well as the sages and gods, were well pleased with the mighty-souled Rāghava <note>A title for Rāma, meaning "descendant of King Raghu."</note>. 

Then installing that foremost of Rākshasas <note>A class of powerful, often shapeshifting beings; in this context, "demons" or "ogres."</note>, Bibhisana <note>Vibhishana, the righteous brother of Rāvana who defected to Rāma's side.</note>, on the throne of Lankā, Rāma was perfectly easy, and rejoiced exceedingly. Then Rāma, obtaining a boon from the celestials, revived the monkeys fallen in battle, and surrounded by friends, set out for Ayodhyā on the car Pushpaka <note>The *Pushpaka Vimāna*, a magnificent flying chariot originally belonging to the god of wealth, Kubera.</note>. And repairing to Bharadwāja's <note>A celebrated Vedic sage whose hermitage Rāma visited.</note> hermitage, Rāma, having truth for his prowess, despatched Hanumān to Bharata <note>Rāma's brother, who had been ruling as regent in Rāma’s absence.</note>. 

Then talking over past affairs, accompanied by Sugriva <note>The king of the monkeys and Rāma’s loyal ally.</note>, Rāma, mounted on the Pushpaka, departed for Nandigrāma <note>The village where Bharata lived as an ascetic while waiting for Rāma’s return.</note>. Having arrived at Nandigrāma, the sinless Rāma sheared himself of his matted locks <note>The long, unkempt hair worn by Rāma during his fourteen-year vow of exile.</note> along with his brothers, and having regained Sītā, got back his kingdom. 

And Daçaratha's <note>Dasaratha, the late King of Ayodhyā and Rāma’s father.</note> son, the auspicious Rāma, lord of Ayodhyā, hath been ruling those happy subjects of his, even like a father. During his reign, his subjects will enjoy happiness, and contentment, and become hale, and grow in righteousness, and be devoid of mental disquietude and disease, and free from the fear of famine. And no person is to witness his son's death, and women will be ever chaste, and never bear widowhood. And no fear of conflagration will trouble the people, nor creatures be drowned in water. And no danger will come from the wind, nor any suffer from fever. And no fear will come from hunger, or from thieves. And cities and provinces will be filled with corn and wealth. And all will live happily as in the Golden age <note>Reference to the *Satya Yuga*, the first and most perfect of the four world ages in Hindu cosmology, characterized by absolute virtue.</note>. And having performed with countless gold an hundred horse sacrifices <note>The *Ashvamedha* ritual, a complex Vedic ceremony performed by powerful kings to prove their imperial sovereignty.</note>, and bestowed with due rites—

<summary>This page concludes the narrative arc of the Ramayana's main plot, describing Sītā’s fire ordeal, Rāma’s triumphant return to Ayodhyā, and the establishment of his ideal, peaceful reign known as Rāma-rājya.</summary>
<keywords>Sītā, Rāma, Agni, fire ordeal, Vibhishana, Pushpaka Vimāna, Ayodhyā, Bharata, Rām-rājya, Golden Age, Ashvamedha</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the initial summary of the Rāmāyana’s plot and the spiritual rewards for its readers. It then transitions into the second section of the "Book of Youth" (Bālakāṇḍam), where the sage Valmiki prepares to bathe in the river Tamasā, marking the moment just before the epic's poetic composition begins.</meta>

->RĀMĀYANA.<-

...ten thousands and tens of millions <note>The original Sanskrit terms are *ayuta* (10,000) and *koti* (10,000,000).</note> of cattle to learned scholars, and countless wealth to famous Brāhmanas <term>Brāhmana: the priestly and scholarly social class</term>, Rāghava <term>Rāghava: a common title for Rāma, meaning "descendant of King Raghu"</term> will establish a hundred royal families and will ensure each of the four social classes <note>The *varna* system: Brāhmana (priests), Kshatriya (warriors/kings), Vaisya (merchants), and Sudra (laborers).</note> performs its own duties. And after reigning for eleven thousand years, Rāma will depart for the realms of Brahmā <note>The highest spiritual heaven.</note>.

Anyone who reads this sacred, sin-cleansing, and merit-bestowing history of Rāma—which is equal to the holy Vedas themselves—is purified of all sin. The person who reads this Rāmāyana, which grants a long life, is honored in heaven after death, along with their children, grandchildren, and relatives. If a Brāhmana reads it, they attain excellence in speech; if a Kshatriya <term>Kshatriya: the warrior and ruling class</term>, they acquire lordship over lands; if a Vaisya <term>Vaisya: the merchant and farmer class</term>, they gain an abundance of wealth in trade; and if a Sudra <term>Sudra: the service and laboring class</term>, they achieve greatness.

---

## ->SECTION II.<-

---

HEARING those words from Nārada <note>A divine messenger and sage who narrated the summary of Rāma's life to Valmiki.</note>, that man of virtuous soul and eloquent speech, together with his disciples, worshipped the mighty sage. Having received due honors, that celestial being asked for and received permission to depart, then ascended to the heavenly regions. 

When Nārada had left for the celestial realms, that holy person <note>Valmiki, the traditional author of the Rāmāyana.</note> went to the banks of the Tamasā River, which was close to the Jahnavi <term>Jahnavi: a name for the River Ganges, daughter of King Jahnu</term>. Upon arriving at the riverbank, the pious sage, observing a holy spot free of mud, spoke to his disciple standing nearby, saying: “O Bharadwāja, look at this holy place, free of mud. It is beautiful and the water is pleasant, even...”

---

<margin>An *ayuta* is ten thousand; and a *koti* is ten millions.</margin>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous section where the sage Vālmīki, inspired by the story of Rāma, goes to the riverbanks to perform his daily rituals. This page recounts the tragic event that sparked the creation of the first poetic verse (sloka) in history.</meta>

# BĀLAKĀNDAM <note>Sanskrit: "The Book of Youth," the first of the seven books of the Rāmāyana.</note>

->2<-

"...like the minds of good men. Child, put down your water-pitcher and give me my bark clothing <note>Ascetics in ancient India wore *valkala*, garments made from the inner bark of trees.</note>. I will bathe right here in the Tamasā, the most excellent of holy waters." Thus spoken to by the high-souled Vālmīki, Bharadwāja—ever devoted to serving his spiritual teacher—presented the sage with his bark garment. That sage of self-controlled senses, having received the garment from his disciple, began to wander about, gazing at the vast forest.

In the vicinity of the woods, the venerable sage spotted a pair of *Kraunchas* <term>Krauncha: a species of crane or curlew, often symbolizing fidelity in Indian literature</term>, calling out in melodious notes and flying about in perfect peace of mind. At that very moment, a wicked-minded fowler <note>A hunter of birds.</note>, without any cause for hostility, singled out the male bird and killed him in the very presence of the holy man. Seeing him struggling on the ground, soaked in blood, his mate began to wail in piteous cries at the sight of her separation from her red-crested, bird husband, who just moments before had been playing with his feathers spread.

Seeing the bird thus brought down by the hunter, the piety of that righteous-souled *Rishi* <term>Rishi: a divinely inspired poet or celestial sage</term> was deeply stirred. Considering it a sinful deed and with a heart moved by pity, that "twice-born" one <term>Twice-born: *Dwija*, a member of the priestly or warrior classes whose second birth is their spiritual initiation</term>, watching the weeping female crane, spoke these words:

"O hunter, since you have killed one of this pair of cranes while they were distracted by love, you shall never find prosperity!" <note>This is the famous *Mā niṣāda* verse, traditionally considered the first poetry ever composed.</note>

Having uttered this, he thought to himself: "What is this that I have said, overcome with grief for a bird?" Reflecting on this in his mind, that highly wise and greatest of sages addressed his disciple, saying:

"This speech that I have uttered is composed of equal feet and accents; it is capable of being chanted to the rhythm of a stringed instrument. Let it therefore be known as a *sloka* <term>Sloka: a specific rhythmic verse form; the word is also a pun on *shoka*, the Sanskrit word for "sorrow"</term> because it was born out of my sorrow!"

When the sage had spoken, his disciple was well pleased and agreed with his excellent words, which gratified the pious Vālmīki. Then, having performed his ritual washing at that holy spot, the reverend sage turned back toward his hermitage, still pondering the events surrounding the birth of that verse.

<summary>While bathing at the Tamasā River, the sage Vālmīki witnesses a hunter kill a crane, leading him to spontaneously compose a curse that becomes the first "sloka" or poetic verse in history.</summary>

<keywords>Vālmīki, Bharadwāja, Tamasā River, Krauncha birds, origin of poetry, Sloka, grief, fowler's curse</keywords>

# RAMAYANA

And his disciple also, accomplished in learning and of meek demeanour, followed Vālmīki, carrying on his back a pitcher filled with water.

Having entered the hermitage <term>Hermitage: An *ashrama*, a secluded dwelling where sages lived, taught, and practiced austerities.</term> along with his disciple, that one knowing duty, while apparently engaged in diverse kinds of talk, revolved in his mind the circumstances connected with the verses. And it came to pass that, desirous of seeing the best of sages, there arrived that lord and creator of all, the effulgent Brahmā, furnished with four countenances <note>Brahmā, the creator god in Hinduism, is traditionally depicted with four heads, representing the four Vedas and the four directions.</note>. Beholding him, Vālmīki rose up suddenly and, wondering greatly, humbly and silently stood before him with folded hands. And duly bending low in reverence, Vālmīki offered that deity water to wash his feet, and other things for reception.

And having sat down on a highly-honored seat, that worshipful one enquired after the welfare of that sage Vālmīki, who knows no deterioration; and then asked him to be seated. And having been seated in the presence of the Grand-sire of all <note>A traditional title for Brahmā, indicating his role as the progenitor of all living beings.</note>, Vālmīki, his mind running upon the self-same subject, became plunged in thought.

“What a sin hath been committed by that wicked-minded one, incited by hostile feeling, when he without cause slew that sweetly-singing Krauncha!” And thereupon, again lamenting that female Krauncha, he, in grief of heart, mentally recited those verses.

Then smiling, Brahmā spoke unto that excellent sage— “Those verses of thine which thou hast composed shall attain celebrity: no doubt need be entertained on this head. It is because I had intended so, that those verses had come out of thy lips. Do thou now, O best of saints, compose the entire history of Rāma. Do thou relate unto the world the history of the righteous-souled and intelligent Rāma, crowned with qualities. And do thou, as thou hast heard it from Nārada, relate all that is known, and all that is unknown to thee, O wise one, concerning Rāma, and Lakshmana, and Videha’s daughter <note>A title for Sītā, the wife of Rāma, referring to her father’s kingdom, Videha.</note>, and all the Rākshasas <term>Rākshasas: A race of powerful, often shapeshifting supernatural beings or demons in Hindu mythology.</term>. And even what is not known to Nārada, shall...

<summary>Vālmīki returns to his hermitage still troubled by the hunter's cruelty, where he is visited by the creator god Brahmā. Brahmā reveals that the sage's poetic outburst was divinely inspired and commands him to compose the complete epic of Rāma's life.</summary>
<keywords>Vālmīki, Brahmā, Rāma, poetic inspiration, shloka, Krauncha bird, Sita, Lakshmana, divine command</keywords>

<page-num>11</page-num>

->**THE BOOK OF CHILDHOOD**<-
<note>original: "BALAKANDAM"</note>

"...shall be unfolded unto thee. And no words of thine in this poem shall contain an untruth. Do thou, therefore, compose into verses this delightful story of Rāma. And as long as the mountains and the seas exist on earth, thy history of the Journey of Rāma <note>original: "Rāmāyana"</note> will spread among men. And as long as this story of the Journey of Rāma shall retain currency, thou shalt reside both in this world and in mine <note>Brahmā's celestial realm, Brahmaloka.</note>." Having said this, the worshipful Brahmā disappeared there. And thereat the sage and his disciples marvelled greatly. And his disciples sang those verses again and again; and, momentarily experiencing pleasure, said unto him with exceeding wonder,—"Those verses, composed of equal accents, and furnished with four parts, have been sung by the mighty saint, have, in virtue of frequent repetition, been associated with a world of pathos, and have attained the eminence of a *verse form* <term>Shloka: The rhythmic meter born from Vālmīki's grief (shoka). It became the standard verse form for Sanskrit epics.</term>. And now it is the intention of that illustrious and self-centered <note>In this context, "self-centered" implies one who is self-possessed or established in the soul.</note> sage to compose the entire Journey of Rāma in this metre."

The great ascetic Vālmīki of gracious appearance and unparalleled renown has composed hundreds of verses in melodious measure, couching the significance of the history of Rāma's line. Listen to the annals of the foremost of the lineage of Raghu <note>Raghu was a legendary king and great-grandfather of Rāma; the dynasty is often called the Raghuvamsha.</note>, and the destruction of the Ten-headed one <note>A reference to the demon-king Rāvaṇa.</note> composed by the ascetic, with **compounds** <term>Samasas: A grammatical feature where multiple words are joined into a single unit.</term>, **phonetic links** <term>Sandhis: The rules governing how sounds blend at the meeting of words or syllables.</term>, **primary forms** <term>Prakritas: Here referring to the underlying natural linguistic structures.</term>, and **suffixes** <term>Pratayas: The grammatical elements added to word roots.</term>; and lucid with sweet and equally-accented words.

### SECTION III.

---

Having heard the entire history of the intelligent Rāma, capable of conferring religious merit <note>Dharma</note> and the two other cognate objects <note>Artha (prosperity) and Kama (pleasure)</note>, as well as emancipation <note>Moksha</note>, Vālmīki again sought to get insight into it. And, seating himself facing the east on a cushion of **sacred grass** <term>Kusha grass: A sacred plant (Desmostachya bipinnata) used in Vedic rituals to create a purified seating area for meditation.</term>, and sipping water <note>A ritual purification known as achamana.</note>...

<meta>This page continues from the previous section where the creator god Brahmā instructed the sage Vālmīki to compose the story of Rāma, promising that his words would remain true and endure as long as the mountains and seas. <page-num>12</page-num>

->**RAMAYANA**<-

In accordance with sacred law, Vālmīki turned his mind to deep meditation <term>Yoga: A disciplined practice of spiritual and mental focus used here to gain prophetic insight.</term> upon the subject. Through the power of his meditation, he clearly saw before him Rāma, Lakshmana, and Sītā, as well as King Daçaratha and his wives in his kingdom—all laughing, talking, acting, and moving as if they were alive before him. He witnessed everything endured by Rāma, who remained firm in his promises, accompanied by his wife as his third companion <note>This refers to the trio of Rāma, Lakshmana, and Sītā in exile.</note>. Like a gooseberry <note>original: "emblic myrobalan" or "Amalaka." A traditional Indian metaphor for something held so clearly in the hand that every detail is visible.</note> resting on the palm of his hand, that righteous soul, by virtue of his meditation, perceived all that had happened in the past and all that would happen in the future.

Having truly seen everything through his contemplation, the noble-minded sage set about recording the beautiful history of Rāma. Agreeably to what had been related by the mighty-souled Nārada, the worshipful saint composed the history of the lineage of Raghu <note>The Raghuvamsha, the royal dynasty to which Rāma belongs.</note>. This work grants both wisdom and delight; it is filled with excellent qualities and, like the ocean, is full of riches and captivates both the ear and the mind.

He recorded:
*   Rāma's birth and his great heroism;
*   His kindness to all and his popularity;
*   His patience, goodness, and truthfulness;
*   The wonderful conversations he had with Viswāmitra;
*   The marriage of Jānaki <note>Another name for Sītā, meaning "daughter of King Janaka."</note>;
*   The snapping of the great bow;
*   The conflict between Rāma and the other Rāma <note>Pārasurāma, or "Rama with the axe," a legendary warrior-sage who challenged Rāma.</note>;
*   The noble qualities of Daçaratha's son and the preparations for Rāma's installation as heir;
*   The spite of Queen Kaikeyi and the obstacles placed in the way of his crowning;
*   The exile of Rāma;
*   The King's grief, his lamentations, and his passing to the other world;
*   The sorrow of the people and Rāma's decision to send them back to the city of Ajodhyā;
*   The meeting with the King of the Nishādas <note>Guha, the forest chieftain.</note>;
*   The return of the charioteer and the crossing of the river Ganges;
*   Rāma’s meeting with the sage Bharadwāja;
*   His arrival at the mountain Chitrakuta following Bharadwāja's advice;
*   Rāma's building of a home and his stay there;
*   The arrival of his brother Bharata and his attempt to persuade Rāma to return;
*   Rāma offering water to the spirits of his father <note>original: "manes." Ritual offerings made to deceased ancestors.</note>;
*   The crowning of the sandals <note>Bharata placed Rāma’s sandals on the throne to rule as his regent.</note>;
*   And Bharata’s residence at Nandigrāma.

### SECTION IV.

---

When Rāma had secured his kingdom, the venerable sage Vālmīki composed the entire history of that hero in beautiful verse and profound meaning. The saint composed twenty-four thousand verses <term>ślokas: The standard metrical unit of Sanskrit epic poetry.</term>; and it consists of five hundred sections, and is divided into six books <term>Kāṇḍas: The major "chapters" or volumes of the epic.</term> along with the Final Book <note>The *Uttara Kāṇḍa*, or the epilogue.</note>. And having composed it, including even those events which were to happen in the future, that lordly sage reflected on who should perform and spread this work before public assemblies. And as that great sage of purified soul was thus pondering, in came Kuśa... <note>The text cuts off as Rāma's son, Kuśa, enters; he and his brother Lava were the first to sing the epic.</note>

### THE BOOK OF CHILDHOOD
<note>original: "Bālakāṇḍam," the first of the seven books of the Rāmāyaṇa.</note>

...and Lava, in the guise of the sons of ascetics, and touched his feet. And he found those illustrious princes, the brothers Kusa <note>original: "Kusi"</note> and Lava, knowing morality, and living in a hermitage, and endowed with sweet voices—apt at taking in the meaning of poetry. And finding them of a retentive endowment, and initiated into Vedic <term>Vedas: The oldest and most sacred scriptures of Hinduism.</term> studies, that lord <note>Referring to the sage Vālmīki.</note> taught them how to interpret the Vedas, and that vow-observing one taught them the great Rāmāyaṇa in full, treating of Sītā’s life, and the destruction of the descendant of Pulastya <note>original: "Paulastya," a title for the demon-king Rāvaṇa, referring to his grandfather, the sage Pulastya.</note>.

And those sweet-voiced brothers, resembling celestial musicians <term>Gandharvas: Heavenly beings known for their mastery of music and dance.</term> in grace, accomplished in music and dancing, and cognizant of *vocal registers* <term>Sthana: The three positions of the voice—chest, throat, and head.</term> and *scales* <term>Murchhana: The melodic modulations or scales in traditional Indian music.</term>, began to chant this poem delightful in recitation and in singing, set in three measures, and seven notes, and sung according to time to the accompaniment of stringed instruments, and fraught with the sentiments of love, pathos, comedy, fury, terror, and heroism <note>These are the "Rasas" or aesthetic emotions that a performer evokes in the audience.</note>. 

And knowing the characteristics that go to make up the Drama, and gifted with mellifluous voices, those blameless princes, coming from Rāma’s body <note>A poetic way of stating they are his biological sons.</note>, and resembling him, even as the reflection of the solar or the lunar disc resembles that disc, got by heart that excellent and moral story in its entirety; and those princes versed in the Fine arts, with a concentrated mind chanted it as they had learnt it, in the assemblies of ascetics and Brahmins <term>Brahmins: The priestly and scholarly class of ancient India.</term> and good men.

Once upon a time, those high-souled and pious ones furnished with every auspicious mark, chanted this poem in an assembly of ascetics of purified souls. Having heard this music, all the ascetics were seized with surprise, and with eyes flooded with tears, exclaimed, “Well done! Well done!” And well pleased, those saints cherishing Duty, praised the praiseworthy Kusa and Lava as they sang, saying,—“Ah! what charming music! What sweetness of the verses! All this happened long ago, yet it seems as if we saw it before us.” And unified with the theme,

### RĀMĀYANA

Both of them singing together sweetly, and at a high pitch, by means of the *shadja* <term>Shadja: The first note of the Indian musical scale, from which the other six notes are said to be born.</term> and the other notes, they entranced the audience. And the two thus went on sweetly singing at a high pitch, praised by those mighty sages priding in their asceticism. Some one in the assembly, pleased with them, presented them with a water-pitcher; and some one of high fame, being delighted, made them a present of a bark garment; and some one gave them a dark deer skin;—and some a holy-thread,—and some, a *kamandalu* <term>Kamandalu: A simple water-pot made of earth or wood, traditionally carried by an ascetic.</term>, and some great saint conferred on them a girdle made of *munja* <term>Munja: A type of tough grass used to make sacred belts for young students.</term> grass; and some person granted them a *vrishi* <term>Vrishi: A mat or seat used by a person practicing spiritual disciplines.</term>, and some, a *kaupina* <term>Kaupina: A small loincloth, representing the simplicity and renunciation of worldly comforts.</term>. 

And then some ascetic, well-pleased, gave them an axe; and some, a red cloth; and some, a thread for tying up their matted locks; and some gladly gave a twine for binding faggots with,—and some ascetic presented them with a sacrificial pot; and some, a quantity of fire-wood; and some, a seat made of *udumbara* <term>Udumbara: The wood of the glomerous fig tree, which is considered sacred in Vedic rituals.</term>. And some exclaimed, “May it be well!” <note>original: "Swasti," a Sanskrit greeting or blessing signifying auspiciousness.</note>; and some joyfully cried,—“May ye be long-lived!” And all those ascetics of truthful speech conferred on them blessings. 

And the sages said,—“Wonderful is the story! And, O ye accomplished in all kinds of music! Beautifully have ye chanted and finished this poem, charming ear and heart, and conferring long life and prosperity,—which will afford themes to poets.” 

And admired everywhere, on one occasion those singers were seen by Bharata’s elder brother <note>Referring to King Rāma, who is the eldest of the four brothers.</note>, in a street of Ayodhyā <note>The capital city of the kingdom of Kosala and the setting for much of the epic.</note>, sparsely scattered with stalls. And having had the brothers Kusa and Lava brought under his roof, that destroyer of enemies, Rāma, accorded those ones worthy of honor, a respectful reception. And having seated himself on a throne of excellent gold, in the midst of his—

# BALAKANDAM

Together with his brothers and counsellors, that lord, Rāma, beholding both the brothers, handsome and of modest demeanour, spoke unto Lakshmana, Bharata and Satrughna, saying,—"Do ye listen to the story, fraught with excellent sense and composed in excellent measure, as sung by these ones endowed with the divine inspiration <note>The original uses the word "afflatus," meaning a divine creative impulse or communication from a deity.</note>." And then he ordered the singers to begin. 

Thereupon, causing the hair of the audience to stand on end <note>A traditional Indian literary trope indicating intense emotion or spiritual thrill.</note>, and ravishing their minds and hearts, they began to sing melodiously and distinctly and in as high a pitch as they could command, in strains rivalling the notes of a *Vinā* <term>Vinā: An ancient Indian stringed instrument, often associated with Saraswati, the goddess of music and learning.</term>. And that song of theirs enchanted the ear of that assembly. 

And Rāma said,—"Although these Kusa and Lava, who perform rigid penances, look like ascetics, yet they bear on their persons the signs of royalty. And, besides, the story leads to my own fame. Do ye, therefore, listen to that history fraught with great worth!" And then, commanded by Rāma, they began to chant according to the *Marga* <term>Marga: A formal, classical style of music, considered the "proper" or "refined" path of composition in ancient India.</term> mode; and Rāma, seated in the midst of his court, was drawn to the music, anxious for the perpetuation of his history.

### SECTION V.

This great story of the *Rāmāyana* <term>Rāmāyana: Literally "The Journey of Rāma," one of the two major Sanskrit epics of ancient India.</term> treats of those victorious kings commencing with Prajāpati <term>Prajāpati: A Vedic deity of creation and the protector of life, considered an ancestor of the solar dynasty.</term>, and having Ikshwāku <term>Ikshwāku: The legendary first king of the Ikshwāku dynasty, also known as the "Solar Dynasty."</term> for their founder, who ruled the entire earth as no other kings had done so before them, and in whose line Sagara was born—Sagara who dug the ocean <note>Refers to the myth of King Sagara's sons whose digging created the bed of the ocean.</note>, and whom, while out in progress, his sixty-thousand sons followed. We shall now chant the entire history of that dynasty from the beginning. Do ye, with minds free from ill will, listen to that story conferring merit, profit, and pleasure.

There is on the banks of the Sarayu <term>Sarayu: A sacred river in North India, on the banks of which the city of Ayodhya is located.</term> a great and flourishing country called Kosala <term>Kosala: The ancient Indian kingdom ruled by the Ikshwāku dynasty, centered around Ayodhya.</term> abounding in corn and wealth, in which the inhabitants passed their days pleasantly. ### RAMAYANA.

The capital of that country was Ayodhyā <note>Literally meaning "The Unconquerable City."</note>, famed among men, which was founded by Manu himself—that foremost of men <note>In Hindu tradition, Manu is the legendary first man and lawgiver of humanity.</note>. And that beautiful and mighty city was twelve *yojanas* <term>Yojana: An ancient Indian measure of distance, roughly equivalent to 8 or 9 miles.</term> in length and ten in breadth; and was intersected outside with spacious roads laid out orderly. And scattered with blown blossoms, and regularly sprinkled with water, the well-arranged broad highways looked beautiful. And that one bringing prosperity unto mighty kingdoms, King Daçaratha, lived in that city, like unto the lord of the deities <note>Indra, the king of the gods.</note> inhabiting the celestial regions. 

And the city was furnished with doors and gates, and well-arranged rows of shops. And it contained all kinds of instruments and arms, and was inhabited by all classes of artisans. And that graceful and matchlessly brilliant city abounded in eulogists and genealogists <note>Professional bards who maintained family records and sang the praises of the king's lineage.</note>. And it was crowned with stately edifices with flags, and guarded by hundreds of *Sataghnis* <note>original: "Sataghni," literally meaning "hundred-killer." These were defensive weapons, often described as massive stones or beams set with iron spikes to be dropped on invaders.</note>. 

And the mighty city contained theatres for females, and gardens, and mango-groves; and was enclosed by a wall. And encircled by a deep moat, the city was accessible neither to friend nor foe. And it abounded with elephants and horses, and cattle and camels and asses. And it was thronged with neighbouring kings come to pay tribute, and inhabited by merchants from various countries, and adorned with mountain-like palaces glittering with gems, and filled with sporting-places for females, and like unto Indra's Amarāvatī <term>Amarāvatī: The capital city of the gods in heaven, known for its incomparable splendor and eternal happiness.</term>. 

And the city was wonderful to behold, gleaming with gold-burnished ornaments, and inhabited by troops of courtezans, and abounding in all kinds of gems, and graced with royal places.
