### SECTION VI.
---

->And in that city of Ayodhya resided King Dasharatha<-

And in that city of Ayodhya resided King Dasharatha, versed in the **Vedas**, commanding all resources, far-sighted, of mighty prowess, dear to the inhabitants both rural and urban, a **Supreme Charioteer** <term>Athiratha: A warrior of even greater status than a Maharatha, capable of battling ten thousand Maharathas at once.</term> in the **Ikshwaku** <note>The solar dynasty of kings, legendary for their righteousness, to which Dasharatha and Rama belong.</note> line, performing sacrifices, engaged in the performance of duties, self-controlled like unto a **Great Sage** <term>Maharshi</term>, a royal saint famed in the three worlds, possessed of strength, the destroyer of foes, having friends, of subdued senses, comparable unto **Indra** <note>original: "Sakra," the King of the Gods and ruler of the heavens.</note> and **Kubera** <note>original: "Vaisravana," the god of wealth and treasurer of the deities.</note> by virtue of accumulated riches and other possessions, and protecting people even as the highly energetic **Manu** <note>The legendary first man and the foundational lawgiver in Hindu tradition.</note> protected them. And as Indra rules **Amaravati** <note>The capital city of the gods in the celestial realm.</note>, that one firm in promise, and following duty, profit, and pleasure, ruled that best of cities. And in that excellent city, the men were happy and righteous-souled, and widely-read, and each contented with his possessions, and devoid of covetousness, and speaking the truth. And in that prime of cities, there was none who had not at his command a plenteous supply of the good things in life.

# BOOK OF CHILDHOOD #
## (BALA KANDA) ##

The **warriors** <term>Kshatriyas: The social class of rulers, administrators, and protectors.</term> and the **laborers** <term>Sudras: The social class traditionally responsible for service and craft vocations.</term>, focused on their respective duties, served the three other social orders. That city was masterfully governed by that lord of **Ikshwaku's** <note>The solar dynasty to which Rama belongs.</note> line, just as the first and wisest of men, **Manu** <note>The legendary first king and lawgiver of humanity in Hindu tradition.</note>, had governed it in ages past. 

As a mountain cave is filled with lions, the city was filled with warriors who were like blazing fires—upright in their ways, brave, and highly educated in the arts of war and wisdom. The city abounded with magnificent horses from **Kamboja** <note>An ancient region in northern Afghanistan/Pakistan famous for its cavalry.</note>, **Balkh** <note>Vahlika: A region in modern-day Afghanistan.</note>, **Vanayu** <note>Likely referring to the regions of Persia or Arabia.</note>, and the banks of the **Indus** <note>Sindhu</note> River. These steeds were comparable to the divine horse of **Hari** <note>original: "Hari's charger"; refers to Uchchaihshravas, the celestial horse of Indra/Vishnu.</note>.

The city also teemed with fierce elephants from the **Vindhya** and **Himalayan** <note>Himavat</note> mountains. These creatures were massive as hills, possessed exceeding strength, and were often in a state of **musth** <note>original: "filled with juice"; referring to the hormonal state of high energy and aggression in elephants.</note>. Among them were the **Bhadra**, **Mandra**, and **Mriga** breeds <margin>Bhadra elephants have compact limbs; Mandras are stocky and heavy; Mrigas are lean and tall.</margin>, as well as hybrids of these three types. They were as majestic as **Airavata** <note>The multi-tusked white elephant that carries the god Indra.</note> and descended from the noble lineages of **Mahapadma**, **Anjana**, and **Vamana** <note>In Hindu cosmology, these are the "World-Elephants" who support the earth at different compass points.</note>. 

This city extended over two **leagues** <term>Yojana: A traditional Indian unit of distance, roughly 8 to 9 miles (13-15 km).</term>, and it truly lived up to its name, **Ayodhya** <margin>The name literally translates to "The Unconquerable" or "One against whom it is impossible to wage war."</margin>. Ruling over his enemies, the city was governed by the great and exceedingly powerful King **Dasharatha**, just as the Moon oversees the stars. This lord of the earth, who resembled **Sakra** <note>Another name for Indra, the King of the Gods.</note>, governed Ayodhya with its strong gates, heavy bolts, and auspicious, beautiful palaces that housed thousands of people.

# RAMAYANA #

### SECTION VII ###

---

That high-souled king of **Ikshwāku’s** <note>The legendary solar dynasty of ancient India.</note> line was supported by competent counsellors, men capable of managing the affairs of state, gifted in discerning the motives of others, and ever dedicated to the welfare of the monarch. This heroic king had eight famous ministers who were pure-hearted and devoted to royal service—namely, **Dhrishti**, **Vijaya**, **Surāshtra**, **Rāshtravardhana**, **Akopa**, **Dharmapāla**, and **Sumantra** the eighth, who was particularly skilled in matters of finance.

He also had two family priests who were after his own heart: those most excellent of saints, **Vasistha** and **Vāmadeva**. Alongside them, he sought the counsel of other sages: **Suyajna**, **Jāvali**, **Kāçyapa**, **Gautama**, the long-lived **Mārkandeya**, and the **regenerate** <note>original: "regenerate"; a reference to the 'twice-born' (dvija), those who have undergone spiritual initiation.</note> **Kātyāyana**. 

Ever working alongside these **Brahmarshis** <term>Brahmarshis: The highest class of "God-realized" sages or seers.</term>, the king's priests and ministers—who had served the dynasty from father to son—were learned yet modest. They were disciplined, auspicious, and high-souled men who were experts in policy and the art of weaponry. Renowned and cautious, they were men of their word, possessing great energy, a spirit of forgiveness, and a stellar reputation. 

They always began their speech with a smile and never spoke a lie, whether moved by anger, personal interest, or desire. They expertly employed spies to monitor activities both within their own kingdom and in hostile lands. These ministers were masters of social interaction and had been tested in their friendship with the king. They remained constantly active in replenishing the treasury and organizing the military. 

Even toward their enemies, they held no ill will if those enemies were innocent. They were heroic, high-spirited followers of law and policy, protecting the citizens who were upright. They bore no malice toward the **Brāhmanas** <term>Brāhmanas: The priestly and scholarly class.</term> or the **Kshatriyas** <term>Kshatriyas: The warrior and ruling class.</term>. By imposing fines and punishments only in proportion to the crimes committed, they kept the treasury full. During the time when these pure-hearted men of one mind presided over the justice of the kingdom, there

# THE BOOK OF CHILDHOOD #
<note>original: "Bālakāṇḍam"; the first book of the Ramayana.</note>

There was neither in the city nor the provinces anyone who was a liar, or wicked, or who pursued the wives of others. Peace reigned throughout the city and the surrounding lands. The ministers wore excellent raiment and ornaments; they were dedicated to observing pure vows and always kept their "eye of policy" <note>A metaphor for political foresight and strategic wisdom.</note> open in the interests of the monarch. 

The king considered them to be crowned with virtues. They were famed for their prowess and reached unerring conclusions because of their intelligence regarding other countries. In all regions and at all times, they manifested their noble qualities. They were well-versed in the arts of war and peace, and possessed a deep understanding of **goodness, passion, and ignorance** <term>three qualities: Known as the *gunas* (Sattva, Rajas, and Tamas), these are the three fundamental forces that Indian philosophy teaches make up the character of every human and the nature of the universe.</term>. 

They could keep their own counsel, judge matters with fine detail, and were experts in the art of politics and fair speech. Surrounded by such counselors endowed with these various qualities, the faultless King **Dasharatha** <note>original: "Daçaratha"</note> ruled the earth. He gathered intelligence by means of **spies** <note>The original manuscript contains a manual ink underline under this word.</note>, righteously protected his subjects, and preserved the people without sacrificing his duties—becoming famous across the three worlds. 

Munificent and firm in his promises even in the heat of battle, that best of men ruled the earth. He never met an enemy who was his equal or his superior. Blessed with friends and obedient commanders, and having removed all "thorns" <note>A common ancient term for internal rebels or external enemies that threaten the peace of a kingdom.</note> by his might, the king ruled the earth just as the **Lord of Celestials** <note>Refers to Indra, the King of the Gods.</note> rules heaven. Surrounded by those counselors who were studious of his welfare, affectionate, clever, and competent, the king—by virtue of his power in subduing others—resembled the Sun surrounded by its rays.

<meta>This page continues from the description of King Dasharatha’s wise administration and his virtuous counselors. Having established his worldly success, the narrative now turns to his personal sorrow—the lack of an heir—and his decision to seek divine intervention through a grand ritual.</meta>

### RAMAYANA ###

Turning the matter over in his mind, the high-souled one <note>The Sanskrit term is *Mahatman*, referring to a person of great or noble spirit.</note> thought, "Why do I not celebrate a **horse-sacrifice** <term>horse-sacrifice: Known as the *Ashvamedha*, this was one of the most significant and complex royal rituals in ancient India, performed to demonstrate a king's power and, in this specific context, to ask the gods for the birth of a son.</term> with the intention of obtaining a son?" 

And that highly-energetic, pious and intelligent monarch, in consultation with all his counsellors of sedate minds, having made up his mind to celebrate the sacrifice, said unto that best of counsellors, **Sumantra**—"Do thou speedily summon my spiritual guides, along with the family priests." 

Thereupon, going out speedily, Sumantra of swift movements called together all the spiritual guides, as well as others versed in the **Vedic ritual** <note>Sacred rites derived from the Vedas, the oldest scriptures of Hinduism.</note>; *namely* <note>original: "viz."</note>, Suyajna, and Vāmadeva, and Jāvali, and Kāçyapa, and Vasistha, and other principal **twice-born** <term>twice-born: A term (Sanskrit: *dvija*) for members of the upper social classes who have undergone a spiritual initiation ceremony, symbolizing a second, spiritual birth. Here, it refers to the learned Brahmanas.</term> ones. 

And having paid homage unto them, the virtuous king **Dasharatha** <note>original: "Daçaratha"</note> then spoke unto them these sweet words, consistent with duty and interest—"Ever pining on account of a son, I know no happiness—therefore it is my intention that I should celebrate a horse-sacrifice. I intend to celebrate it according to the ordinance. Do ye, therefore, consider how I may attain my object." 

Thereat, the **Brahmanas** <note>The priestly and scholarly class responsible for performing religious rites.</note> with Vasistha at their head, exclaiming "Well! Well!" approved the words that had fallen from the lips of the monarch. And exceedingly pleased, they spoke unto Dasharatha saying—"Do thou order the necessary articles, loose the horse <note>In the Ashvamedha, a consecrated horse is released to wander for a year; the king's army follows it to claim any territory it enters.</note>, and prepare the sacrificial ground on the north bank of the **Sarayu** <note>The river that flows past the capital city of Ayodhya.</note>. And, O king, since with the intention of obtaining offspring thou purposest so piously, thou wilt surely obtain sons after thy heart." 

And hearing these words of the regenerate ones, the king was highly gratified. And with eyes expanded in delight, he spoke unto his ministers—"Do ye procure the necessary sacrificial articles, according to the injunction of my spiritual preceptors; and loose a horse protected by a competent person, and followed by one of the chief family priests; and do ye prepare the sacrificial ground on the north bank of the Sarayu; and do ye in due order and according to the ordinance perform the rites required to..."

# THE RAMAYANA
## BĀLAKĀNDAM <note>The "Book of Youth," which is the first of the seven books comprising the Ramayana epic.</note>

secure an uninterrupted completion to the ceremony. This ceremony is incapable of being celebrated by every king. Particular care should be taken that the sacrifice is not defective on account of any serious omission; inasmuch as with learned **Brāhma-Rākshasas** <term>Brāhma-Rākshasas: In Hindu mythology, these are powerful and dangerous spirits of deceased Brahmins (priests) who led unholy lives or misused their great knowledge. They are said to haunt rituals, searching for a single mistake to invalidate the sacrifice and destroy the performer.</term> ever on the look-out to espy shortcomings in the ceremony, the performer thereof speedily perishes, should anything take place not consonant to the ordinance. And do ye possessed of ability so arrange, that this sacrifice may be completed in harmony with the ritual." Thus addressed with due respect, the counsellors listened to the words of the monarch, and said, "So be it."

Then taking the permission of that best of kings, those **regenerate ones** <note>A reference to the "twice-born" or Brahmins, who have undergone spiritual initiation.</note> knowing duty, having blessed the monarch, returned to their respective quarters. And dismissing those **Brāhmanas** <term>Brāhmanas: Also spelled Brahmins; the priestly class responsible for maintaining sacred knowledge and performing rituals.</term>, the king spoke unto his minister, saying,—"Do ye, even as the family priests have ordered, arrange for the sacrifice!" Having said this, that mighty-minded and best of men dismissed his minister, and himself entered into the inner apartment. And coming there, that lord of men said unto his favourite wives,—"Do ye know it for certain that in order to obtain a son I am going to perform a sacrifice." And hearing those sweet words, the countenances of those shining dames looked resplendent, like lotuses after the cold season is over. <note>This metaphor compares the queens' joy to flowers blooming at the end of winter.</note>

### SECTION X.

Thus asked by the king, **Sumantra** said these words—"I will relate to you how the counsellors brought Rishyasringa. Listen now, along with your own ministers! The royal priest together with the counsellors spoke to **Romapada** <note>The King of Anga and a friend of Dasharatha.</note>, saying—'The means that we have devised can never fail to work.' 

Rishyasringa has been brought up entirely in the woods; he is constantly engaged in spiritual austerities and the study of the **Vedas** <term>Vedas: The oldest and most sacred scriptures of Hinduism, composed in archaic Sanskrit.</term>; and he is completely ignorant of the pleasures that come from contact with women. By using things that appeal to the senses and delight the soul, we shall bring him to the city. Therefore, make these arrangements! Let beautiful women, dressed in fine ornaments, travel to his forest. If they treat him well, they will find a way to bring him here." Hearing this, the king said to the priest, "So be it!" and gave him the responsibility—who, in turn, passed the task to the courtiers. And the courtiers acted accordingly.

And in accordance with the instructions, the **courtesans** <note>Highly skilled women trained in the arts, music, and dance, often employed by royal courts for diplomatic or social missions.</note> entered that great forest; and remaining at some distance from the hermitage, they endeavoured to meet with the virtuous son <margin>†</margin> of the saint who dwelt forever in the woods. Because he was satisfied with serving his father, he never strayed far from the hermitage; and consequently, he had never seen men, women, or any other creatures living in cities and towns.

And it came to pass that on one occasion, walking about as he pleased, the son of **Vibhandaka** <note>original: "Bibhāndaka"; a powerful sage known for his stern asceticism.</note> came to that spot and beheld the courtesans. Excellently attired and singing in sweet voices, the women said unto the saint's son—"Who art thou? And what dost thou do here, O **Brahmin** <note>original: "Brāhmana"; a member of the priestly class responsible for sacred learning and rituals.</note>? We wish to learn all this. And why is it that thou rangest alone through this far-off forest?"

Beholding these beautiful damsels, whom he had never seen before, he felt great delight and hastened to inform them of his lineage: "My father is **Vibhandaka**, and I am his son, born of his own loins. My name is **Rishyasringa** <note>original: "Rishyasringa"; his name means "deer-horned," as he was said to have been born with a small horn.</note>; and my occupation is known the world over. This auspicious hermitage nearby belongs to us, and there I shall receive you all in due form."

Hearing the words of the saint's son, they all consented, and the women went to see that asylum. And when they had arrived, the saint's son received them hospitably, saying—"Here is the **ritual offering** <note>original: "Arghya"; a traditional offering of respect to an honored guest, usually consisting of water, milk, or honey.</note>," "Here is water for washing the feet," and "Here are fruits and roots." Thereupon, they readily received his hospitality.

However, driven by the fear of the saint **Vibhandaka**, they set their minds upon departing quickly. They said—"Do thou also, O <term>twice-born one</term> <note>original: "dvija"; a term for members of the higher social classes who have undergone a spiritual initiation, effectively a "second birth."</note>, receive from us these excellent fruits! And may good fortune follow thee, O <term>Vipra</term> <note>original: "Vipra"; a learned priest or sage.</note>, do not delay!"

Thereupon, embracing him joyfully, they gave him sweetmeats and various kinds of savoury foods. Tasting those things, that youth of exceeding spiritual energy mistook them <margin>†</margin> for fruits, for such things had never been tasted before by the dwellers of the forest. Then, having greeted him, the women feigned the observance of a religious vow and went away, inspired by their fear of the father.

### SECTION XII

Then after a long while, when the charming spring <note>Spring is considered the most auspicious season for major Vedic rituals in ancient Indian tradition.</note> had appeared on the earth, the king conceived the desire of celebrating the sacrifice. Then bowing down his head unto that **learned sage** <note>original: *Vipra*; a term for a wise man or priest who has mastered the sacred scriptures.</note> who shone with celestial light, he appointed him to undertake the ceremony for the purpose of obtaining offspring to perpetuate his line. 

Then that **Brahmin** <note>Referring to Rishyasringa, who was invited specifically for his purity and power to bring rain and sons.</note> said unto that lord of the earth, the king,—“So be it! Do thou order the necessary provisions, loose the horse <note>The "horse-sacrifice" (*Ashvamedha*) involved releasing a consecrated horse to wander for a year; the land it traveled across was claimed by the king, provided no one could stop the horse or the army following it.</note>, and prepare a sacrificial ground on the north bank of the **Sarayu River**.” 

Then the king spoke unto **Sumantra** <note>Dasharatha's trusted chief minister and charioteer.</note>, saying,—“O Sumantra, do thou summon speedily **Brahmins** versed in the **Vedas** <note>The most ancient and sacred scriptures of India.</note> and priests professing the **Vedanta** philosophy <note>The school of thought focused on the ultimate philosophical teachings of the Vedas.</note>—Suyajna, and Vamadeva, and Javali, and Kashyapa, and the priest **Vasishtha** <note>The hereditary royal preceptor and spiritual advisor to the solar dynasty of kings.</note>, together with other excellent **twice-born** ones <note>original: *Dvija*; a term for the upper social classes who have undergone spiritual initiation, here specifically referring to the priestly class.</note>.” 

Thereupon Sumantra, endowed with activity and bestirring himself, summoned all those versed in the Vedas. Then, honoring them duly, the virtuous king **Dasharatha** spoke unto them these amiable words, consistent with duty and interest,—“Aggrieved on account of a son, I have no happiness on earth—and therefore, I have intended to celebrate a horse-sacrifice. And by the grace of the **saint’s son** <note>Referring again to Rishyasringa, the son of the sage Vibhandaka.</note>, I shall obtain my desire.” 

Thereupon, the Brahmins with **Vasishtha** at their head honored the words that fell from the king’s lips, saying,—“Well.” And the Brahmins headed by **Rishyasringa** addressed the king, saying,—“Do thou arrange for the provisions, loose the horse, and prepare the sacrificial ground on the north bank of the Sarayu! And since thou purposest so virtuously for obtaining offspring, thou shalt obtain four sons of immeasurable prowess.” 

And hearing those words of the **priestly ones** <note>original: *regenerate ones*; another English translation for *Dvija* or twice-born.</note>, the king was exceedingly delighted. And cheerfully he spoke these auspicious words to his courtiers,—“In accordance with the direction of my spiritual guides,

# BĀLAKĀNDA <note>The "Book of Youth," the first book of the epic Ramayana.</note>

“Hurry and gather these supplies—release the horse, which must be well-guarded and accompanied by a priest—and set up the ritual grounds on the northern bank of the **Sarayu River**. Make sure you perform the ceremonies capable of protecting these rites from any interference. Every king is certainly capable of attempting this sacrifice, yet we must take extreme care that no error occurs. For any flaws in this most important of sacrifices are watched for by the learned **Brahma-Rakshasas** <note>original: *Brahma-Rākshasas*; formidable spirits or demons who were once priests but fell from grace; they are said to haunt rituals looking for technical errors to exploit.</note>. If the celebration violates the sacred laws, the person performing it will face immediate ruin. So, see to it that this sacrifice of mine is completed according to the established ritual.” 

Upon hearing the king's words, the ministers obeyed his orders. After praising the king for his devotion to duty, the **twice-born** ones <note>original: *dvija*; a term for members of the three higher social classes whose spiritual initiation is considered a 'second birth.'</note>, having received the Monarch’s permission, returned to their own homes. Once the **Vipras** <note>original: *Vipras*; inspired poets or priests learned in sacred lore.</note> had departed, the great-minded king dismissed his advisors and retired to his private quarters.

---

### SECTION XIII

A full year later, when spring had returned once more to the world, the powerful king—determined to obtain children through the **horse-sacrifice** <note>The *Ashvamedha*, one of the most significant and complex royal rituals of ancient India, involving the release of a consecrated horse.</note>—went to visit **Vasishtha** <note>The hereditary spiritual guide and chief priest of the royal family.</note>. After greeting Vasishtha and showing him proper respect, the king spoke humbly to that greatest of the **twice-born** regarding his desire for heirs. 

“O **Brahmin** <note>original: *Brāhmana*; a member of the priestly class dedicated to spiritual and scriptural study.</note>, please undertake the performance of this sacrifice for me according to the sacred laws, O leader of those who practice self-denial! See to it that no obstacles hinder the ritual! You are my loyal friend and my most powerful spiritual guide. By taking this on, you will carry the entire responsibility for the ceremony.” 

Then, that greatest of **Brahmins** replied, “So be it! I shall do everything that you ask.”

<summary>King Dasharatha issues final orders for the horse-sacrifice, warning of the spiritual dangers of technical errors, before formally requesting the sage Vasishtha to lead the complex ritual a year later.</summary>
<keywords>Dasharatha, horse-sacrifice, Ashvamedha, Vasishtha, Sarayu River, Brahma-Rakshasas, ritual purity, Vedic sacrifice</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the preparations for King Dasharatha’s Ashvamedha (horse sacrifice). Having gathered his advisors in the previous section, the King now directs the physical construction of the ritual grounds and the logistical arrangements for thousands of guests.</meta>

->### RAMAYANA<-

“...as you have asked.” He then spoke to the elderly <term>Brāhmanas</term> <note>original: *Brāhmanas*; members of the priestly and scholarly class responsible for sacred rituals.</note> who were experts in sacrificial affairs, as well as experienced <term>car-makers</term> <note>original: *rathakāras*; skilled chariot-builders who held a specialized role in Vedic society.</note>, highly pious elders, and servants who oversee ceremonial operations until their conclusion. He also addressed artists, carpenters, diggers, astrologers, artisans, dancers, theater directors, and pure, learned individuals well-versed in various branches of knowledge:

“By the King’s command, engage yourselves in the work of the sacrifice! Fetch thousands of bricks! Build structures for the visiting kings that provide every possible convenience. Erect hundreds of fine and comfortable buildings for the <term>Brāhmanas</term>, well-stocked with various foods and drinks. You must provide spacious quarters for the citizens and those coming from the provinces, separate housing for princes arriving from foreign lands, stables for horses, dressing rooms, and wide apartments for both local and foreign warriors. 

“Construct dwellings filled with diverse kinds of <term>viands</term> <note>original: *anna-pāna*; a variety of choice foods and beverages.</note> and everything one could desire, as well as beautiful mansions for the lower orders of the citizens. All food should be served with genuine respect, not with the careless indifference often seen at festivals, so that everyone feels they have been treated with honor. No one is to be disregarded out of personal desire or temper. Those workers and artisans who labor diligently for the sacrifice should be specially entertained in their turn; likewise the servants, who, having been motivated with gifts, perform every task completely and omit nothing. Act with hearts softened by love, so that all our friends may be well-pleased with us.”

Then they approached **Vasishtha** <note>The hereditary high priest and spiritual guide of the Solar Dynasty.</note>, saying, “Everything has been performed properly, with nothing left out. Whatever you command shall be done, and nothing will be neglected.” 

Then, summoning **Sumantra** <note>King Dasharatha’s chief minister and charioteer.</note>, **Vasishtha** said these words: “Invite all those kings who are pious, as well as <term>Brāhmanas</term>, <term>Kshatriyas</term> <note>original: *Kshatriyas*; the warrior and ruling class.</note>, and <term>Sudras</term> <note>original: *Śūdras*; the laboring and artisan class.</note> by the thousands. Bring people from every country with the honors due to them.”

“And with proper honor, you yourself must bring the righteous, truthful, and heroic Janaka, the lord of Mithilā <note>Mithilā was the capital of the Videha kingdom; Janaka is famously the father of Sītā, the heroine of this epic.</note>. “Next, bring the highly pious and elderly King of Kekaya <note>The father of Queen Kaikeyī, King Dasharatha's second wife.</note> along with his son, for he is the father-in-law of our great monarch! The virtuous Sumantra, following the sage's instructions, personally set out to bring the monarchs. Meanwhile, the servants came and informed the wise Vasishtha that the materials for the <term>sacrifice</term> <note>original: *yajña*; a ritual of offerings to the gods, in this case, the horse sacrifice intended to obtain a son for the King.</note> were ready. Then that best of the <term>twice-born</term> <note>original: *dvija*; a member of the upper social classes—priests, warriors, or merchants—who has undergone spiritual initiation.</note>, the ascetic Vasishtha, said to them:

> “Do not give anything away disrespectfully or lightly. A gift given with disrespect undoubtedly destroys the giver.”

Then, for several days, kings began to pour into Dasharatha’s city day and night, bringing various types of gems. Thereupon Vasishtha, well-pleased, said to the King:

> “O best of men, the kings have come here in obedience to your command; and I, too, according to their merit, have...”

### SECTION XIV
---

And after the passing of one full year, when the sacrificial horse <note>In the Ashvamedha or "Horse Sacrifice," a horse is set free to roam for a year to claim territory; its return marks the beginning of the final rites.</note> had returned, the sacrifice of the king commenced on the north bank of the **Sarayu** <note>The sacred river flowing past the capital city of Ayodhya.</note>. And with Rhishyasringa at their head, the principal <term>twice-born ones (a term for the upper social classes who have undergone spiritual rebirth through initiation)</term> began the proceedings in that mighty horse-sacrifice of that high-souled monarch. 

The priests, each performing his proper part correctly and according to the law, engaged in the ceremony in harmony with the scriptures. And the priests, having performed the <term>pravargya (a preliminary ritual involving the offering of heated milk)</term> as well as the <term>upasada (a series of preparatory offerings and fasts)</term> according to the law, duly completed the additional ceremonies. 

Then, worshipping the deities with glad hearts, those foremost of ascetics duly performed the morning ablutions and the other prescribed rites. The oblations of clarified butter <note>original: "ghrita" or Ghee; a staple offering in Vedic fire sacrifices.</note> first having been offered to **Indra** <note>The king of the gods and the god of rain and storms.</note> according to the ritual, the king with a purified heart performed his ceremonial baths. And then the mid-day ritual followed.

The ritual baths took place in their proper sequence. And the most distinguished <term>Brahmans (members of the priestly and scholarly class)</term>, in due form and according to the sacred ordinances, officiated at the third ritual bath of that great-souled monarch. The priests, presided over by Rishyasringa, invoked <note>original: "Sakra"</note> Sakra—the King of the Gods—and the other deities, reciting <term>mantras (sacred chants)</term> of precise meter. 

The sacrificial priests, chanting sweet <term>Sāmas (melodic hymns from the Sama Veda)</term> and soft prayers, and duly invoking the inhabitants of the heavenly regions, offered each deity their designated share of the oblations. No part of the ceremony was performed improperly or omitted; everything was successfully celebrated with the appropriate sacred chants. 

On that day, no Brahman ever felt weariness or hunger. There was not one among them who was not deeply learned, nor any who was not followed by a hundred attendants. Brahmans and <term>Sudras (the laboring and service class)</term>, including among them ascetics, <term>Sramanas (wandering monks or seekers)</term>, the elderly, the infirm, women, and children, were all continuously fed. Every day, food prepared properly in the traditional manner was seen in countless heaps resembling hills. Men and women coming from various countries to the sacrifice of that great-souled king were excellently entertained with meats and drinks. 

The most eminent <term>twice-born ones (those of the upper social classes who have undergone spiritual initiation)</term> said, "The food has been prepared in the prescribed form, and it tastes excellent. We are gratified. May good betide you!" All of this was heard by that descendant of <note>The Raghu dynasty is the lineage of King Dasharatha and later, Rama.</note> Raghu. Persons adorned with ornaments distributed the food among the Brahmans, assisted by others wearing jewelled pendants.

### RAMAYANA.

Neither those who were ignorant of the <term>Sacred Scriptures (note original: "Vedas")</term> and the <term>Six Auxiliary Sciences (note original: "Vedangas")</term>, nor those who failed to observe their spiritual vows, nor those who were not profoundly learned were present—nor did anyone assist at the sacrifice who was unable to engage in skilled philosophical debate. And when the time came for planting the <term>Sacrificial Posts (note original: "Yupas")</term>, craftsmen and those knowledgeable in sacrificial rites prepared six posts made of <term>Bael wood (note original: "Vilwa")</term>, six of <term>Khair wood (note original: "Catechu")</term>, six of <term>Flame-of-the-Forest wood (note original: "Palasa")</term>, one of <term>Indian Cherry wood (note original: "Sleshmataka")</term>, and two of <term>Himalayan Cedar (note original: "Devadaru")</term>, all well-crafted and measuring two outstretched arms in width.

Experts in the arts and the science of sacrifice constructed these sacrificial posts. At the time of erecting them to embellish the ritual, these twenty-one posts—each measuring twenty-one <term>Cubits (note original: "Aratnis")</term> <note>An "Aratni" is an ancient unit of measure, approximately the length of the forearm from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger.</note>, possessing eight angles, and featuring smooth surfaces—were adorned with twenty-one pieces of cloth and firmly planted by the artisans with the appropriate ceremonies. Wrapped in cloths and honored with flower offerings, they resembled the <term>Seven Sages (note original: "Rishis")</term> <note>The "Seven Rishis" refers to the Saptarishi, often identified with the stars of the Big Dipper.</note> appearing in the sky.

A sufficient number of bricks was also prepared according to the rules for the ceremony. <term>Brahmans (the priestly class)</term> accomplished in these arts constructed the sacrificial altar with those bricks. That altar belonging to that lion among kings, built by skillful priests and consisting of eighteen bricks on three sides, was shaped like the <term>Divine Golden-Winged Eagle (note original: "Garura")</term> <note>Garuda is the celestial bird and mount of the god Vishnu; the altar's shape was meant to symbolize spiritual ascent.</note>. For the purpose of sacrificing them to their respective deities, a collection was made of beasts, reptiles, birds, horses, and aquatic animals.

The priests sacrificed all these in the proper form. To these posts were bound three hundred animals, as well as the finest of the great horses belonging to King <term>Dasharatha (note original: "Daçaratha")</term>. Then Queen <term>Kausalya (note original: "Kauçalyā")</term>, having performed the preliminary rites, slew the horse with three strokes, filled with great joy. Seeking to gain spiritual merit, Kausalya, with a steady heart, passed one night with that horse <note>In the Ashvamedha ritual, the senior queen's contact with the horse symbolized the transfer of the animal's vitality and fertility to the royal line.</note> which was described as being "furnished with wings." Then the <term>Invoking Priests (note original: "Hotas")</term>, the <term>Ritual Officiants (note original: "Adhwaryus")</term>, and the <term>Chanting Priests (note original: "Udgatas")</term> joined the King’s <term>Favored Wife (note original: "Vavata")</term> along with his <term>Chief Queen (note original: "Mahishi")</term> and <term>Lower-ranking Wife (note original: "Parivriti")</term>. <note>The original translator notes that the King could marry from different social classes: the warrior class (Kshatriya) wife is the Mahishi, the merchant class (Vaishya) wife is the Vavata, and the laboring class (Shudra) wife is the Parivriti.</note> And priests of—

# BALAKANDAM.

With his senses mastered and being well-versed in the performance of sacrificial rites, the King began to offer the oblations using the <term>fat (note original: "vapa")</term> of the horse, exactly as the <term>ritual ordinances (note original: "Shastras")</term> prescribe. Seeking to wash away his sins, that lord of men inhaled the fragrance of the smoke rising from the burning fat at the prescribed time, in accordance with the sacred scriptures. 

Then, sixteen sacrificial priests offered the various parts of the horse into the holy fire in the established form. While it is customary in other sacrifices to offer oblations using a branch of the <term>Plaksha tree</term> <note>A species of fig tree, *Ficus religiosa*, sacred in Hindu ritual.</note>, in this horse-sacrifice, a cane is used instead. This horse-sacrifice, according to the <term>Ritual Manuals (note original: "Kalpa Sutras")</term> and the <term>Commentaries (note original: "Brahmanas")</term> <note>The Kalpa Sutras are handbooks on ritual procedures, while the Brahmanas are ancient texts explaining the significance of the Vedic rites.</note>, lasted over three days. 

Thereafter, on the first day, the <term>Chatushtoma ritual</term> was celebrated; on the second, the <term>Uktha</term>; and on the third, the <term>Atiratra</term>. Following these, the <term>Jyotishtoma</term>, the <term>Ayushtoma</term>, another <term>Atiratra</term>, the <term>Abhijit</term>, the <term>Viswajit</term>, and the <term>Aptoryama</term>—all these great and various sacrifices—were celebrated with the proper rites.

In this mighty horse-sacrifice, established long ago by <insert>the Creator</insert> <term>Svayambhu (note original: "Sayambhu")</term> <note>Literally "The Self-Existent One," an epithet for the god Brahma.</note>, the King—the protector of his lineage—bestowed the Eastern quarter of his kingdom upon his chief sacrificial priest, the Western quarter upon his <term>Adhwaryu</term> <note>The priest responsible for the physical details of the sacrifice.</note>, the Southern quarter upon the <term>Brahma</term> <note>The presiding priest who oversees the entire ceremony.</note>, and the Northern quarter upon the <term>Udgatha</term> <note>The priest who chants the hymns of the Sama Veda.</note> as <term>Ritual Fees (note original: "Dakshinas")</term>. 

Upon completing the sacrifice, the King, that foremost of men and continuer of his race, conferred the entire earth upon the priests. Having made this gift, the auspicious descendant of <term>Ikshvaku (note original: "Iskhaku")</term> experienced profound joy. However, the priests then spoke to the monarch, whose sins had now been completely purged, saying: 

"You alone are worthy to protect the entire world. We do not desire the land, nor can we rule it, O Lord of the Earth, for we are constantly occupied with <term>Vedic studies (note original: "Vaidika")</term>. Therefore, grant us something else in its place as the price for it. Bestow upon us gems, or gold, or cattle, or anything of that nature; for, O best of kings, we have no use for the Earth."

Addressed in this way by the <term>Brahmans</term> who were masters of the Vedas, that greatest of kings bestowed upon them ten <term>Lakhs (note original: "lacs")</term> <note>A lakh is 100,000.</note> of cows, ten <term>Crores (note original: "Kotis")</term> <note>A koti (crore) is 10,000,000.</note> of gold pieces, and forty crores of silver. Then, those priests together—

Then, having celebrated that excellent and sin-destroying sacrifice—which brings one to heaven and is a feat incapable of being celebrated by any but the foremost of monarchs—King <term>Dasharatha (note original: "Daçaratha")</term>, well pleased, spoke unto <term>Rishyasringa</term>, saying: "O thou of excellent vows, it is right for thee to do that whereby my lineage may increase."

---

-># BĀLAKĀNDAM.<-
->### (The Book of Childhood)<-

For the King’s sake, that effulgent sage set about the <term>son-conferring ceremony (note original: "Putrakama Ishti")</term> <note>A specific Vedic ritual performed to invoke the gods to grant male heirs.</note>; and in accordance with the sacred ordinances, and with <term>mantras</term> <note>Sacred chants or spiritual formulas used in rituals.</note>, he offered oblations into the sacrificial fire. And the deities, along with the <term>Gandharvas (note original: "Gandharbas")</term> <note>Celestial musicians and singers in the heavenly court.</note>, and the <term>Siddhas</term> <note>Perfected beings who have attained supernatural powers through spiritual practice.</note>, and the principal saints, assembled there duly, with the object of each obtaining his share of the offerings. 

And having duly assembled there, the deities addressed these words unto <term>Brahma (note original: "Brāhmā")</term> <note>The creator god in the Hindu trinity.</note>, the lord of creatures:—“O thou possessed of the six divine attributes, through thy grace, a <term>Rakshasa (note original: "Rākshasa")</term> <note>A powerful supernatural being, often depicted as a demon or titan in Hindu mythology.</note> named <term>Ravana (note original: "Rāvana")</term> oppresses us all by his prowess—nor can we baffle him. And, O lord, as thou hast, well-pleased, conferred on him a <term>boon</term> <note>A divine gift or promise of protection granted after intense devotion or penance.</note>, we always suffer his presence in deference to it. And that wicked-minded one harasses the three worlds furnished with prosperity, and bears ill-will toward them. And blinded by the boon he has received, that irrepressible one intends to bring down the lord himself of the celestials, and the <term>Yakshas</term> <note>Nature spirits and guardians of hidden treasures.</note>, and the <term>Gandharvas</term>, and the <term>Brahmins (note original: "Brāhmanas")</term>, and the <term>Asuras</term> <note>A class of powerful beings often in conflict with the gods.</note>. And the Sun does not burn him, or the wind blow about him; and at the sight of him, even the Ocean—that one engarlanded with billows—dares not stir. Therefore, great is the fear that afflicts us, coming from that <term>Rakshasa</term> of dreadful appearance. And O lord, it behoves thee to devise some means for destroying him.” 

Thus addressed by the deities in a body, Brahma said,—“Alas! I have, however, decided on the means of destroying that wicked-souled one. He had asked—‘May I be incapable of being slain by <term>Gandharvas</term>, and <term>Yakshas</term>, and gods, and <term>Rakshasas</term>!’—whereat I said—‘Be it so!’ Through disdain, the <term>Rakshasa</term> did not at that time mention men. Therefore, by men alone he is capable of being slain; nor can his end be achieved by any other means.” 

Hearing this welcome speech uttered by <term>Brahma</term>, the deities and the <term>Maharshis</term> <note>The "Great Rishis" or supreme ancient seers.</note> became exceedingly delighted. You, O God, are the ultimate path for us all, O repressor of foes! To bring destruction to the enemies of the gods, turn your thoughts toward being born as a human." 

Thus petitioned, that greatest of gods and chief of the celestials, Vishnu, worshipped by all creatures, addressed the gathered deities—with the Grand-sire at their head—in accordance with Duty, saying:—"Cast away your fear! For your sake, I will slay in battle the deceitful and unstoppable Ravana, who is a terror to the saints and the gods, along with his sons, grandsons, friends, advisors, relatives, and acquaintances. I will remain among mortals, ruling this earth for ten thousand and as many hundred <note>This equals 11,000 years, a traditional span for a divine reign in epic chronology.</note> years." ## SECTION XVI.

Thus pleaded with by the leaders of the deities, that <term>Searcher of Hearts</term> <note>original: "Narayana"; derived from "Nara" (the multitude of souls) and "Ayana" (dwelling-place). It implies the divine presence within all beings.</note>, **Vishnu**, though he already knew the way to destroy **Ravana**, spoke these kind words to the gods: "What, O gods, is the means of accomplishing the destruction of that lord of the <term>Rakshasas</term> <note>A race of powerful, often malevolent supernatural beings or titans.</note>? By what method may I slay that thorn in the side of the holy ascetics?"

When addressed this way, the deities answered **Vishnu**—who is incapable of decay—saying: "Assuming the form of a human being, do you slay **Ravana** in battle! He, O repressor of foes, has for a long time performed severe penances; because of those efforts, the creator of all things, the first-born **Brahma**...

...was well pleased. And won over by his austere penances, the **Grand-sire** <note>original: "Pitamaha"; a title for the creator god Brahma, the progenitor of the universe.</note> conferred a blessing upon the **Rakshasa** <term>Rakshasa</term> <note>A class of powerful, often shape-shifting supernatural beings or demons in Hindu mythology.</note> to the effect that, except for mortal man, he should fear no being from the various orders of life. In requesting that boon, **Ravana** had previously disregarded humans, viewing them as too insignificant to be a threat. Swollen with pride because of this gift from the **Grand-sire**, he now ravages the three worlds and abducts beautiful women by force. "Therefore, O subduer of enemies," the gods said, "we have fixed upon man as the instrument of his death." Hearing this plea from the heavenly ones, **Vishnu**, possessing a disciplined soul, chose King **Dasharatha** to be his father. At that very time, the glorious King **Dasharatha**, who was childless and longed for sons, was performing the sacred sacrifice intended to grant male offspring. Having decided on his course of action, **Vishnu** bid farewell to the **Grand-sire** and vanished from that place, honored by the gods and the **Maharshis** <term>Maharshis</term> <note>Great seers or "High Sages" who have attained the highest level of spiritual knowledge.</note>.

And then, from out of **Dasharatha’s** sacrificial fire, there arose a magnificent being of incomparable power, immense energy, and great strength. He was dark in complexion, dressed in crimson robes, with a red face and a voice like a trumpet's blast. His body was covered in hair like a lion's mane, and he possessed a fine beard and head of hair. Adorned with divine ornaments and auspicious marks, he stood tall like a mountain peak, possessing the ferocity of a flaming tiger and glowing like the sun or flickering tongues of fire. In his hands, he carried a large vessel made of polished gold with a silver lid—as precious as a bride and radiant with divine creative energy—filled with celestial **Payasa** <term>Payasa</term> <note>A sacred preparation of rice, milk, and sugar, often used in Vedic rituals as a holy offering.</note>. Fixing his gaze upon **Dasharatha**, he addressed the king, saying: "O Monarch, recognize me as a messenger sent by **Prajapati** <term>Prajapati</term> <note>The "Lord of Creatures," a deity presiding over procreation and the protection of life.</note>." Hearing these words, **Dasharatha**, with his hands joined in reverence, replied: "Lord, you are most welcome. What can I..."

Do you, foremost of kings, accept this excellent and divinely-prepared **sacred rice pudding** <term>Payasa</term> <note>A ritual offering made of rice cooked in milk and sugar, meant here as a fertility-granting substance.</note>, conferring sons, health, and affluence—which you are to give unto your worthy consorts, saying, '*Partake of it.*' Through them you will, O monarch, obtain sons, for the sake of whom you have performed this sacrifice." 

Thereupon, saying, "So be it," the lord of men delightedly placed that divinely-bestowed golden vessel, filled with the celestial **sacred rice pudding** <note>original: "Payasa"</note>, upon his head. And having saluted that wonderful being of gracious presence, he, in an excess of joy, began to circle around him again and again in reverence. 

Then *Dasharatha*, having received that divinely-prepared **pudding**, grew exceedingly glad, like a pauper suddenly attaining great wealth. Then that highly radiant being of a wonderful form, having performed his mission, vanished then and there. And *Dasharatha’s* inner apartment, being graced with the rays of joy, looked like the **sky** <note>original: "welkin"</note> flooded with the lovely beams of the autumnal moon. 

Then, entering the inner apartment, he spoke to *Kausalya*, saying, "Take this **pudding**; for this will make you bear a son." Having said this, the king offered her a portion of the **pudding**. Then he conferred upon *Sumitra* a fourth of it. Then, in order that she might have a son, King *Dasharatha* made over to *Kaikeyi* an equal portion of what remained. And then, having reflected, the mighty-minded one gave to *Sumitra* the remaining portion of the **pudding**, which resembled **divine nectar** <note>original: "ambrosia"; likely referring to Amrita, the drink of the gods.</note>. 

Thus the king dispensed the **pudding** to each and all of his wives. And those foremost wives of the king, having received that **pudding**, became exceedingly delighted and considered themselves highly honored. Then those excellent consorts of the lord of the earth, having separately partaken of that choice **pudding**, shortly bore offspring who resembled fire or the Sun. And the king, beholding those wives of his bearing children, obtained his desire and became delighted; even as the **Lord of Progeny**...

### SECTION XVII.

---

When **Vishnu** <note>The supreme deity tasked with preserving the universe.</note> had accepted the role of becoming the son of that high-souled king, the **Self-Created Lord** <note>A title for Brahma, the creator god.</note> addressed the gods, saying:

"To assist the heroic Vishnu—who is firm in his promises and always seeks the welfare of us all—you must create powerful beings. These beings should be able to assume any shape at will, be masters of illusions, and possess great heroism. They must be as fast as the wind, well-versed in morality, highly intelligent, and equal to Vishnu himself in prowess. Make them invulnerable, knowledgeable in the ways of war and peace, gifted with excellent bodies, capable of resisting all weapons, and resembling the immortals.

"From the bodies of the foremost **celestial nymphs** <note>original: "Apsaris"</note>, and **heavenly musicians** <note>original: "Gandharbis"</note>, and **nature spirits** <note>original: "Yakshis"</note>, and **serpent-maidens** <note>original: "Pannagis"</note>, and **female bears** <note>original: "Rikshis"</note>, and **wisdom-holders** <note>original: "Vidhyádharis"</note>, and **celestial choristers** <note>original: "Kinnaris"</note>, and **female monkeys** <note>original: "Vanaris"</note>, you are to produce sons wearing the shapes of monkeys. 

"Previously, I created the lord of bears, **Jambavan** <note>original: "Jāmbuvāna"</note>, who suddenly emerged from my mouth while I was yawning."

Hearing this mandate from the Lord who possesses the **six divine attributes** <note>In Sanskrit tradition, these are sovereignty, power, glory, beauty, knowledge, and renunciation.</note>, the gods began to produce sons in the form of monkeys. High-souled ascetics, **perfected beings** <note>original: "Siddhas"</note>, **wisdom-holders** <note>original: "Vidhyádharas"</note>, **serpent-spirits** <note>original: "Uragas"</note>, and **celestial singers** <note>original: "Cháranas"</note> all fathered heroic sons who would roam the woods.

**Indra** <note>The King of the Gods.</note> fathered the foremost of monkeys, **Vali** <note>original: "Vāli"</note>, who was as mighty as the Mahendra hill. The **Sun**—the best of those who provide heat—fathered **Sugriva**. **Brihaspati** <note>original: "Vrihaspati"; the teacher and priest of the gods.</note> fathered the mighty and highly intelligent monkey named **Tara** <note>original: "Tārā"</note>, the most excellent of the lead monkeys. And the **Giver of Riches** <note>A reference to Kubera, the god of wealth.</note> fathered the graceful monkey...

And the beautiful **Twin Physicians of the Gods** <term>Ashwins</term>, who possessed the treasure of loveliness, fathered Maindra and Dwivida. 

**The Lord of Waters** <term>Varuna</term> fathered the monkey named Sushena; and **the Rain Cloud God** <term>Parjanya</term> fathered Sarava, who possessed great strength. And **the Wind God** <term>Vayu</term> fathered the graceful monkey named Hanuman, whose body was as hard as a diamond; he was as fast as **the son of Vinata** <note>A reference to Garuda, the divine eagle-king.</note> and was the most intelligent and powerful among all the leaders of the monkeys. 

Thus, there suddenly came into being by the thousands mighty bears and monkeys, including the **cow-tailed monkeys** <note>original: "Go-puchchhas"</note> of immeasurable strength. They were heroic, powerful, and able to change their shapes at will. Their bodies were as massive as elephants or mountains—it was these beings who would eventually work to destroy **the Ten-headed One** <note>The demon-king Ravana.</note>. 

The sons of these deities clearly retained the specific colors, forms, and prowess that defined their various fathers. Those born from the **long-tailed monkeys** <note>original: "Golangulas"</note> possessed even more power than the gods themselves. Likewise, thousands upon thousands of monkeys were gladly fathered upon **female bears** <note>original: "Rikshis"</note> and **celestial choristers** <note>original: "Kinnaris"</note> by gods, **great sages** <term>Maharshis</term>, **celestial musicians** <term>Gandharvas</term>, **eagle-beings** <term>Tarkshyas</term>, famous **nature spirits** <term>Yakshas</term>, **serpents** <term>Nagas</term>, **humanoid spirits** <term>Kimpurushas</term> <note>Mythical beings often described as half-man and half-beast.</note>, **perfected masters** <term>Siddhas</term>, **wisdom-holders** <term>Vidyadharas</term>, and **crawling spirits** <term>Uragas</term>.

Furthermore, the **celestial bards** <term>Charanas</term> fathered heroic monkeys of gigantic size upon the primary **celestial nymphs** <term>Apsaras</term>, the **female wisdom-holders** <term>Vidyadharis</term>, the daughters of the **serpent-folk** <term>Nagas</term>, and the **female musicians** <term>Gandharvis</term>. All these monkeys were gifted with immense strength; they could take any shape they wished and travel anywhere they pleased.

## SECTION XVIII.

---

When the **horse sacrifice** <term>Ashvamedha</term> <note>A grand Vedic ritual performed by kings to prove their sovereignty and, in this case, to obtain children.</note> of the high-souled Dasharatha had been completed, the immortals, accepting each his share of the offerings, returned to where they had come from. And the monarch, having observed all the rules of religious initiation, entered the palace with his carriages and retinue. And the lords of the earth, having been received suitably by the king, set out for their own countries with joyful hearts, saluting that foremost of ascetics, Rishyasringa. Clad in bright apparel, the delighted armies belonging to those graceful kings returned to their own homes, looking exceedingly beautiful. When the lords of the earth had gone away, the graceful King Dasharatha re-entered his palace, with the foremost of the **twice-born** <term>regenerate ones</term> <note>A term for Brahmins, referring to their second, spiritual birth during initiation.</note> at his head. Followed by the intelligent monarch and his retinue, Rishyasringa, having been duly honored, set out with his wife Shanta. Having thus dismissed them all, the king, his object attained, began to dwell there happily, expecting the birth of his sons.

And then, when six seasons <note>A period of one year, as the Indian calendar traditionally counts six seasons of two months each.</note> had passed after the completion of the sacrifice, in the twelfth month, on the ninth lunar day <note>This day is still celebrated today as Rama Navami.</note>, under the influence of the Punarvasu asterism, when the Sun, the Moon, *Saturn*, *Jupiter*, and *Venus* were in the signs of *Aries*, *Capricorn*, *Libra*, *Cancer*, and *Pisces*—and when *Jupiter* had arisen with the Moon in *Cancer*—Kausalya gave birth to that lord of the universe, bowed unto by all the worlds, Rama. He was a descendant of the Ikshvaku dynasty, marked with excellent physical signs—the one half of the god **Vishnu** <note>The Preserver in the Hindu trinity; Rama is considered his seventh avatar.</note>—exceedingly righteous, with rosy eyes, mighty arms, a crimson lower lip, and endowed with a voice like the resonant sound of a **kettledrum** <note>original: "kettledrum," referring to the deep, booming sound of the Dundubhi drum.</note>. Then, having given birth to that son of immeasurable prowess, Kausalya looked resplendent, like...

-># RAMAYANA<-

Just as **Aditi** <note>The mother of the gods in Hindu mythology.</note> had brought forth that foremost of celestials—the **wielder of the thunderbolt** <note>original: "Indra"</note>—so did the Queen give birth to her son. And then was born of **Kaikeyi**, **Bharata**, a prince of true prowess, endowed with every virtue, and embodying one fourth of the divine essence of **Vishnu** <note>The Preserver god; the princes are considered earthly incarnations (avatars) of his divine power.</note>. And then **Sumitra** gave birth to **Lakshmana** and **Shatrughna**, heroes skilled in all weapons, who together possessed the remaining half of Vishnu’s essence. 

**Bharata**, a child of clear intelligence, was born under the auspicious lunar mansion of **Pushya** <note>The eighth lunar mansion or asterism in Hindu astrology, associated with nourishment and growth.</note>, while the Sun was in the sign of **Pisces**; while the two sons of Sumitra were born when the Sun arose in **Cancer**, under the asterism of **Ashlesha** <note>The ninth lunar mansion, associated with the serpent deities.</note>.

And thus were born separately four high-souled sons to the King, crowned with noble qualities, graceful, and in their loveliness resembling the constellations of **Prosthapada** <note>Referring to the constellations also known as Uttarabhadrapada and Purvabhadrapada.</note>. At their birth, the **Gandharvas** <note>Celestial musicians.</note> began to chant sweetly, and the **Apsaras** <note>Celestial nymphs or dancers.</note> began to dance. The heavenly kettledrums sounded, and blossoms showered down from the sky. 

Great festivities were commenced by the crowds in **Ayodhya** <note>The capital city of the kingdom of Kosala, described as "unconquerable."</note>. The wide highways became filled with actors and dancers, glittering with all kinds of gems, and resounding with the music of singers and instrumentalists. The King bestowed gifts upon bards, genealogists, and those who sing praises, and he also gave cattle by the thousands to the **Brahmins** <note>The priestly and scholarly class.</note>.

And when the eleventh day had passed, the King performed the naming ceremony for his sons. Experiencing great delight, the sage **Vashistha** <note>The royal family’s spiritual preceptor and high priest.</note> conferred the names. The high-souled eldest one was called **Rama**; Kaikeyi’s son was called **Bharata**; Sumitra’s [elder] son was called **Lakshmana**; and the last was named **Shatrughna**. The King fed the Brahmins as well as the inhabitants of the countryside and the city, and he bestowed heaps of jewels upon the priests. Thus did he celebrate the birth rites of the princes. And among all...

<page-num>51</page-num>

# BALAKANDAM.

Among those princes, the eldest, **Rama**, who was like the celestial body **Ketu** <note>original: "Ketu"; historically referred to as the ninth planet in Indian astronomy, it is a shadow planet associated with spiritual influence and karmic destiny.</note> and the particular joy of his father, became the object of universal admiration, much like the **Self-Created Creator** <note>original: "Brahmā"</note> himself. All the brothers were well-versed in the **Vedas** <note>The most ancient and sacred scriptures of Hindu tradition, containing hymns, philosophy, and guidance for ritual.</note>, heroic in spirit, and dedicated to the welfare of others. Each was highly educated and possessed of great virtue.

Among them all, the exceptionally powerful **Rama**, who drew his strength from truth, was beloved by everyone; he was as stainless as the **Moon** <note>In Sanskrit literature and mythology, the Moon is a male deity, often cited as a symbol of cool, gentle, and refreshing beauty.</note>. He was skilled in riding elephants and horses and was an expert in maneuvering **chariots** <note>original: "cars"</note>. He was constantly engaged in the study of weaponry and remained dedicated to serving his father.

From his early youth, **Lakshmana**—that bringer of good fortune—was deeply devoted to his eldest brother **Rama**, the delight of all. Like another life force to Rama, the virtuous Lakshmana was attentive to his brother's every wish, even if it meant neglecting his own needs. This most excellent of men would not even sleep unless Rama was with him, nor would he eat any offered sweet unless Rama shared it. Whenever **Raghava** <note>A patronymic title for Rama, identifying him as a "descendant of King Raghu."</note> went hunting on horseback, Lakshmana followed behind him, bow in hand, to protect him.

Likewise, Lakshmana’s younger brother, **Shatrughna**, became dearer to **Bharata** than life itself.

Because of these exalted and well-loved sons, **Dasharatha** experienced an abundance of joy, similar to that which the **Grandfather of the Universe** <note>An epithet for the god Brahma, the creator.</note> feels for the gods. As they grew in knowledge, were crowned with virtue, and became modest, famous, wise, and far-sighted, Dasharatha—the father of such powerful and radiant sons—was as delighted as the Lord of Worlds, **Brahma**.

And those best of men, ever engaged in the study of—

# RAMAYANA.

...the <term>Vedas<note>The most ancient and sacred scriptures of Hindu tradition, containing hymns, philosophy, and guidance for ritual.</note></term>, were accomplished in the art of archery—and always intent upon ministering unto their father.

And once upon a time, when the virtuous king **Dasharatha**, surrounded by his priests and friends, was reflecting about the nuptials of his sons, unto that high-souled one engaged in thought in the midst of his counsellors came the mighty ascetic **Vishvamitra**. And desirous of seeing the king, he said unto the warders,—“Do ye speedily announce that I, **Gadhi’s** son sprung in the **Kushika** line, have come!” Hearing those words of his, they urged on by them, all hurriedly began to run towards the royal chambers. And coming to the royal apartments, they communicated to **Ikshvaku’s** descendant the arrival of the ascetic **Vishvamitra**. 

Hearing those words of theirs, **Dasharatha** surrounded by his priests, went out delightedly to meet him, like <term>Indra<note>original: "Vāsava"; a name for the King of the Gods.</note></term> going out to meet <term>Brihaspati<note>original: "Vrihaspati"; the priest and spiritual guide of the gods. The original text uses the term "Brahmana," which, according to the sacred scriptures (original: "Sruti"), refers to Brihaspati as the chief priest among the deities.</note></term>. And having come unto that ascetic observing vows and of flaming energy, the monarch with a cheerful countenance offered him the <term>Arghya<note>A traditional hospitality offering composed of water and other ritual ingredients, presented to honored guests.</note></term>. And thereupon, having accepted the king’s ritual offering in accordance with the ordinance, he enquired of the lord of men as to his continued prosperity and peace. 

And the exceedingly virtuous descendant of **Kushika** asked the king concerning the welfare of the exchequer, and the provinces; and the peace of his friends and acquaintances. “And are thy captains submissive: and hast thou vanquished thy foes? And hast thou performed well the human and the divine rites?” And approaching **Vashistha** and the other anchorites, that foremost of ascetics of exalted piety duly asked them touching their welfare. And having been properly received by the monarch, they with glad hearts entered the royal residence, and sat them down according to precedence. Then gladly worshipping the mighty ascetic, **Vishvamitra**, the exceedingly generous king,

### BĀLAKĀNDAM <note>The Book of Youth; the first section of the Ramayana, detailing the early lives of the princes.</note>

well-pleased, addressed him, saying,—“Like unto the obtaining of nectar <note>original: "Amrita"; the substance that grants immortality to the gods.</note>, like unto a shower in a land suffering from drought, like unto the birth of sons of worthy wives to him without issue, like unto the recovery of a lost thing; yea,—like unto the dawning of a mighty joy, I consider this thy arrival. O illustrious ascetic, thou art welcome. What is even that which is nearest to thy heart. What shall I do for thee, experiencing sincere pleasure? Thou, O <term>Brahmana<note>original: "Brāhmaṇa"; a member of the priestly and scholarly class, the highest of the four Hindu castes.</note></term>, art worthy of my best services. By luck it is that, O bestower of honor, I have gained thee. To-day my birth hath been crowned with fruit—to-day hath my life attained its object. And truly yesternight hath been succeeded by an auspicious morning, since I have beheld thee. Having first attained exceeding effulgence by virtue of austerities performed for obtaining the title of **Royal Sage** <note>original: "Rājarshi"; a king who has attained a high state of spiritual enlightenment.</note>, thou hast afterwards obtained the status of a **Brahminic Sage** <note>original: "Brahmarshi"; the highest class of sages, possessing the greatest spiritual power.</note>. Thou art worthy of manifold homage from me. This thy exceedingly holy arrival appeareth wonderful. O lord, by beholding thee, surely my body hath been rendered pure. Tell me, what is it that thou wouldst have,—and what is the purpose of thy coming? I wish that I may be obliged by doing thy will. And, O thou of excellent vows, thou ought not to hesitate. I will every way accomplish thy will; for thou art my god. O twice-born one <note>original: "regenerate one" or "Dvija"; a term for members of the upper castes who have undergone spiritual initiation.</note>, surely great prosperity cometh to me in consequence of thy coming, in as much as it shall be the means of bringing me entire and excellent merit, O <term>Brahmana</term>!” Hearing this soul-soothing, ear-charming, and free-humble speech that was uttered, that illustrious prime of ascetics crowned with virtues, and furnished with all perfections, experienced exceeding delight.

<header>RAMAYANA.</header>

### SECTION XIX.

---

**H**EARING those astonishing words of that lion-like king, the highly-energetic Vishvamitra, with the hair on his body standing on end from excitement, said,—“O foremost of kings, born from an illustrious line and having Vasishtha for your guide, these words become you alone on earth and no one else. Do you, O best of kings, consider the course of action regarding the matter I hold in my heart; and prove firm in your promise!

For the purpose of celebrating a sacrifice, I, O foremost of men, am following a certain prescribed course <gloss>u</gloss>. <note>The original text uses "course" in the context of a "Vrata," or a religious vow involving specific rituals.</note> And it has come to pass that two <term>Rakshasas<note>original: "Rākshasas"; powerful, shape-shifting demonic beings known for disrupting holy rituals.</note></term>, who can assume any shape at will, have become intent on disturbing the ceremony. In that sacrifice which I have determined to bring to a completion, and which is on the very eve of being finished, both these demons, Maricha and Suvahu—who are highly skilled in arms and possessed of great prowess—shower flesh and blood upon the altar.

When the ceremony was thus disturbed and my purpose frustrated, I considered my labors to be entirely lost, and therefore, I have left my country in deep dejection. And, O monarch, I cannot bring myself to release my wrath; for such is the nature of that sacred business, that it is not proper for one engaged in it to utter a curse.

Therefore, O foremost of monarchs, it is right for you to grant me your eldest son, the heroic Rama of genuine prowess, who still wears the youthful side-locks <note>original: "Kākapaksha"; literally "crow's wings," referring to the side-locks of hair left on the temples of young warriors who have not yet reached full adulthood.</note>. By virtue of his own divine energy, and being protected by me, he is capable of even destroying those demons who disturb the ceremony. And I will, without doubt, confer upon him many various blessings—by means of which he will secure the high opinion of the three worlds. 

Upon encountering Rama, they will by no means be able to withstand him, nor is there any other person who dares to slay them. Puffed up with their own energy, they have already been caught in the snares of <term>Death<note>original: "Kāla"; Time personified as the god of death who eventually claims all beings.</note></term>,*—and, O best of monarchs, they are no match for him."

* <note>original: "Yama"; the god of death and the judge of spirits.</note>

<header>THE BOOK OF YOUTH <note>original: "BĀLAKĀNDAM"</note></header>

> Rama. O King, you should not give in to your fatherly affection. For only ten nights, Rama must stay there with the goal of killing those enemies of my sacrifice—the <term>Rakshasas<note>original: "Rākshasas"; powerful, shape-shifting demonic beings who sought to disrupt holy rites.</note></term> who disturb our sacred rituals. I tell you, you may consider these demons as already slain. I am fully aware of Rama’s true strength—as are the highly energetic <term>Vasishtha<note>original: "Vasishtha"; the royal family's priest and a legendary sage.</note></term> and the other ascetics present here. And if you, O King, have set your heart on earning religious merit and great fame on this earth, then grant me Rama! And, O <term>descendant of Kakutstha<note>original: "Kākutstha"; a royal title derived from the words *Kakud* (the hump of a bull, an emblem of royalty) and *Stha* (residing), meaning a prince who descends from the grandson of Ikshwaku.</note></term>, if your advisors and the <term>Brahmans<note>original: "Brāhmanas"; the priestly and scholarly class.</note></term> led by Vasishtha agree, then let Rama go! This is my only wish—and he has now come of age. Therefore, part with your son, the lotus-eyed Rama, for the ten days of the sacrifice! Act now, O descendant of <term>Raghu<note>original: "Raghu"; a celebrated king of the Solar Dynasty and Rama's ancestor.</note></term>, so that the time appointed for the ceremony does not pass by. May good fortune be with you! Do not let your mind sink into grief!"

Having spoken these words, which were consistent with both virtue and the king's own interests, the great-minded and powerful Vishvamitra fell silent. Upon hearing the auspicious but demanding words of Vishvamitra, that greatest of kings shook with a deep sorrow and became overwhelmed. Then, regaining his senses, he stood up, but was cast down by dread. Having heard the words of the hermit—words capable of tearing at his mind and heart—the high-souled king of men was struck with grief and trembled in his seat.

# RĀMĀYANA.

[Upon regaining] his senses, he spoke thus:—"My lotus-eyed Rāma is not yet sixteen years old; and I do not perceive his fitness to cope with <term>demons<note>original: "Rākshasas"; powerful, shapeshifting beings often depicted as enemies of Vedic rituals.</note></term> in battle. I am the lord of this **<term>massive military host<note>original: "Akshauhini"</note></term>** of forces. Marching with this, I myself will engage with these rangers of the night. And these servants of mine are valiant, warlike, accomplished in weapons, and capable of fighting the demons; therefore, it is not right for you to take Rāma.

And I myself, bow in hand, stationed at the front of the army, will battle with the night-rangers as long as life is spared to me. Thus well-protected, your sacrifice will proceed without any obstacles. Therefore, I will go there myself—and it is not right for you to take Rāma. He is youthful and inexperienced, not knowing the difference between true strength and weakness, and he is not yet equipped with the **power of military science** <note>The original English "energy of science" refers to *Astra-vidya*, the specialized knowledge of divine weapons and supernatural warfare.</note>. Being unskillful in combat, he is no match for the demons, who are deceitful warriors. 

Bereft of Rāma, O best of ascetics, I cannot live for a moment. Therefore, it is not right for you to take him. If, O <term>priest<note>original: "Brāhmana"</note></term>, it is your intention to take Rāma, then, O you of excellent vows, do you also take me along with the **<term>four-fold forces<note>original: "Chaturanga"</note></term>**! O <term>son of Kushika<note>original: "Kucika's son"; an ancestral name for the sage Vishvamitra.</note></term>, I am sixty thousand years old; and at this age I have obtained Rāma only after undergoing extreme troubles—it therefore is not right for you to take Rāma. And among my four sons, I find my highest delight in Rāma, my first-born and the most virtuous of them all—therefore, it is not right for you to take Rāma. 

What is the power of these demons? Whose sons are they? Who are they exactly? And what are the sizes of their bodies? And who protects them, O foremost of ascetics? And by what means shall either Rāma, or my forces, or I myself, O priest, be able to slay those deceitful warriors—the demons—in fight? Tell me everything,"

## BOOK OF CHILDHOOD <note>original: "BALAKANDAM"</note>

"O venerable one, since they are so puffed up with their own might, how can I possibly stand against them in battle?" 

Hearing his words, Vishvamitra said: "There is a <term>demon<note>original: "Rākshasa"; powerful, often shapeshifting beings in Hindu mythology who frequently disrupt sacred rituals.</note></term> named Ravana, born from the lineage of the sage Pulastya. Having obtained a boon of power from **Brahma** <note>The Creator god</note>, he boldly defies the three worlds, being possessed of great strength and prowess, and supported by countless demons. And, O mighty monarch, I also hear that this lord of the demons is the very brother of **Vaishravana** <note>Another name for Kubera, the god of wealth</note> and the son of the ascetic Vishrava. When that powerful being does not stoop to disturb the sacrifice himself, those powerful demons, Maricha and Subahu, acting on his orders, disturb the rites." 

The ascetic having spoken thus, the king then answered him: "I am unable to stand against that evil-souled being in battle. Therefore, O you who are learned in morality, please extend your favor to my son! Unfortunate as I am, you are my guide and my god. Not even the gods, the *Dānavas* <note>A race of titans or giants who often war with the gods</note>, the *Gandharbas* <note>Celestial musicians and spirits</note>, the great birds, or the serpent-beings are capable of enduring Ravana in battle—what hope then for a man? In conflict, he strips even the most powerful of their courage. I cannot fight him, nor can I fight his forces. 

And, O foremost of ascetics, whether you are accompanied by my son or by my entire military host, you will not be able to withstand him. And how can I, O <term>priest<note>original: "Brāhmana"</note></term>, hand over my son to you? He is of such tender years, looking like a young god, yet he is entirely ignorant of warfare. I will not part with my son. The sons of Sunda and Upasunda <note>Referring to Maricha and Subahu</note> resemble **Time** <note>original: "Kāla"; here referring to the personification of Death or the inevitable end of all things</note> himself in battle, and it is they who are disturbing your sacrifice. Therefore, I will not part with my son. Maricha and Subahu are full of prowess and skilled in the use of weapons. I am willing to go with my friends to encounter one of them. If you do not agree to this, I beg of you and my friends—please, do not ask this of me!"
