HEARING those words of King Dasharatha <note>original: "Daçaratha's"</note>, which were spoken in a voice trembling with fatherly affection, Vishvamitra <note>original: "Kuçika's son"</note>, stirred up with anger, answered the monarch, saying—"Having first given me your promise, you now try to take it back. This is surely unworthy of a descendant of King Raghu, and such an act can bring destruction upon your entire dynasty. If, O King, you believe you have acted properly, then I will return to the place I came from. O descendant of Kakutstha <note>An ancestor of the royal line</note>, you who are false to your word, may you find happiness staying here with your friends." 

And as the wise Vishvamitra was moved to such fury, the entire earth began to tremble, and even the gods were filled with fear. Seeing that the whole universe was in terror, the mighty saint, the calm Vashishtha <note>The king's family priest and advisor</note> who is known for his excellent vows, spoke these words to the king—"Born into the royal line of Ikshvaku, you are the very embodiment of virtue. Endowed with patience, blessed, and faithful to your sacred duties, you must not abandon righteousness. The descendants of Raghu are famous throughout the three worlds <note>Heaven, Earth, and the Underworld</note> for being righteous souls. Maintain your habit of keeping your word; it is not right for you to act dishonestly. If you promise 'I will do this' and then do not follow through, the spiritual merit you have earned by performing good deeds like digging public water tanks will be lost. Therefore, you must let Rama go! 

Whether or not he is yet skilled with weapons, the <term>demons<note>original: "Rākshasas"; malevolent beings often depicted as disruptors of sacred rituals.</note></term> cannot harm him while he is protected by Vishvamitra, just as the nectar of immortality <note>original: "ambrosia"</note> cannot be touched when it is guarded by a blazing fire. This sage is virtue incarnate; he is the greatest of all men in power. He surpasses all others in learning, and is the refuge of..."

asceticism. This sage is aware of everything that exists in the three worlds <note>The realms of Heaven, Earth, and the Underworld</note>, which are populated by all things both moving and still. Others do not truly know him now, nor shall they know him hereafter. Neither the gods, nor the saints, nor the immortals, nor the **Rakshasas** <term>Rakshasas: powerful, often malevolent shape-shifting beings</term>, nor the finest of the **Gandharvas** <term>Gandharvas: celestial musicians and spirits</term> and **Yakshas** <term>Yakshas: nature spirits and guardians of earthly treasures</term>, nor the **Kinnaras** <term>Kinnaras: celestial beings often depicted as half-human and half-horse or bird</term>, nor the mighty **Nagas** <term>Nagas: divine serpentine beings</term> can fully grasp his power.

In earlier times, while this descendant of Kushika <note>Vishvamitra</note> was still ruling his kingdom, the god Shiva conferred upon him the highly famous sons of Krishashwa in the form of divine weapons. These "sons" of Krishashwa were the offspring of the daughters of **Prajapati** <term>Prajapati: a Vedic deity presiding over procreation and the protection of life</term>. They were endowed with various forms and were radiant and terrifying. The elegant daughters of Daksha, named Jaya and Suprabha, brought forth a hundred of these exceedingly bright weapons. By virtue of a divine blessing, Jaya obtained fifty sons of immeasurable strength who possessed the power of becoming invisible for the purpose of slaughtering the hosts of the **Asuras** <term>Asuras: a class of powerful beings who frequently battle the gods</term>. Suprabha also brought forth fifty sons named the *Sanharas* <term>Sanharas: "Annihilators" or "Withdrawers"; specialized weapons used to neutralize or retract other divine armaments</term>, who are unbearable, infallible, and powerful. Vishvamitra <note>original: "Kuçika's son"</note> is perfectly skilled in the use of all these weapons. 

Furthermore, O descendant of Raghu <note>King Dasharatha</note>, this man who knows his duty is also capable of creating new and wonderful weapons. There is nothing in the past, present, or future which is not known by that great-souled leader of ascetics, who is so deeply aware of morality. Such is the prowess that the highly famous Vishvamitra possesses through his mighty energy. Therefore, O King, you should not hesitate to allow Rama to go. Vishvamitra is himself capable of defeating the **Rakshasas**; it is only for your son’s own welfare and growth that he has come to you and asked for him."

At this speech from the ascetic <note>Vashishtha</note>, the King—the foremost of the line of Raghu—became greatly pleased and delighted. Now finding the idea of Rama’s journey favorable, the famous monarch began to reflect on the best way to entrust his son to the care of Vishvamitra <note>original: "Kuçika's son"</note>.

### SECTION XXII.

Upon Vashishtha's <note>The royal family priest and advisor</note> recommendation, King Dasharatha himself, with a serene expression, summoned Rama and Lakshmana to him. And when the auspicious rites of blessing had been performed by both Rama's father and mother, and when the priest Vashishtha had uttered **mantras** <term>mantras: sacred chants or prayers used in Hindu rituals to invoke divine power</term>, King Dasharatha, smelling his son's crown <note>In ancient Indian culture, smelling the top of a child's head was a traditional gesture of profound parental love and blessing, similar to a kiss</note>, with a glad heart, entrusted him to the descendant of Kushika <note>Vishvamitra</note>. 

Then there blew a breeze free from dust and delicious to the touch, bearing witness to the lotus-eyed Rama being placed in the hands of Vishvamitra. And as the high-souled one was about to set out, blossoms began to shower down abundantly, accompanied by the sounds of celestial kettle-drums and the loud blasts of conchs. 

Vishvamitra went first, and next the highly famous Rama with his side-locks <note>Original: *kakapaksha*. These were specific tufts of hair left on the sides of the head, characteristic of the warrior caste's youth who had not yet reached full adulthood</note>, holding his bow. And following him was Sumitra's son <note>Lakshmana</note>. Equipped with quivers and with bows in hand, gracing the ten cardinal points and resembling three-hooded serpents, they followed the high-souled Vishvamitra, like the two stalwart **Ashwins** <term>Ashwins: the twin Vedic gods associated with medicine, health, and the dawn</term> following the Grand-sire <note>Brahma, the creator god</note>. 

And those radiant ones of faultless limbs went in the wake of the ascetic, illuminating him with their grace. Like the sons of Shiva—**Skanda** and **Vishakha** <term>Skanda and Vishakha: divine warrior sons of Shiva, often associated with youth and strength</term>—following the incomprehensible deity **Sthanu** <note>A name for Lord Shiva meaning "The Pillar" or "The Motionless One"</note>, those youthful brothers of handsome person and faultless limbs, Rama and Lakshmana, highly radiant, carrying bows in hand, adorned with ornaments, and equipped with scimitars, with their fingers encased in monitor lizard skin <note>Original: "Guana skin." These were protective gloves or finger-guards used by archers to prevent the bowstring from chafing the skin</note>, flamingly followed Kushika's son, beautifying him with their splendor. 

And having proceeded over half a **Yojana** <term>Yojana: a traditional Indian measure of distance, roughly equal to 8 or 9 miles</term>, and arriving at the right bank of the Sarayu river, Vishvamitra addressed these sweet words to Rama:

"O Rama! Do thou, O child, take of this water; let no delay occur. Receive the **mantras** known as *Vala* <note>original: *Balā*, meaning "Strength"</note> and *Ativala* <note>original: *Atibalā*, meaning "Super-strength"</note>—and then thou wilt not feel fatigue or fever, nor undergo any change of look, and..."

<summary>King Dasharatha formally entrusts Rama and Lakshmana to the sage Vishvamitra, and the trio departs Ayodhya amidst divine omens. Upon reaching the Sarayu River, Vishvamitra begins Rama's initiation by offering him two powerful mantras for endurance.</summary>

<page-num>61</page-num>
<header>BALAKANDAM.</header>

Whether asleep or heedless, the demons <note>Original: *Rākshasas* — a class of powerful, often malevolent supernatural beings in Hindu mythology</note> will not be able to surprise you. And, O Rama, the might of your arms will be unequalled in this world—nay, in all the three worlds. Do you, O descendant of Raghu <note>Original: *Rāghava*</note>, recite the secret sciences of Strength and Extreme Strength <term>Valā and Ativalā: Two sacred mantras or spiritual disciplines that grant physical endurance and immunity to fatigue</term>, O child! And, O sinless one, when you have secured these two kinds of knowledge, none in this world will equal you in good fortune, or in talent, or in philosophic wisdom, or in subtle apprehension, or in the capacity of answering a controversialist; for Strength and Extreme Strength are the nurses of all knowledge. 

And, O Rama, O foremost of men, if you recite Strength and Extreme Strength on the way, neither hunger nor thirst will affect you, O descendant of Raghu! And if you recite these, you will attain fame on earth. Those sciences fraught with energy are the daughters of the Creator <note>Original: *Grandsire*, referring to the god Brahma</note>. I intend to confer them upon you, O descendant of Kakutstha <note>Original: *Kākutstha* — a title for Rama referring to his ancestor King Kakutstha</note>; and, O lord of earth, they are worthy to be conferred upon you; and they are possessed of various virtues. You need not entertain any doubt about it. And if you bear them in consonance with the precepts, they will prove of manifold good unto you." 

Thereat Rama, with a cheerful countenance, sipping water <note>Performing *achamana*, a ritual purification where water is sipped from the palm while reciting prayers</note>, with a purified body, received those sciences from the *Great Sage* <term>Maharshi: 'Great Seer' or sage, referring here to Vishvamitra</term> of subdued soul. And furnished with the sciences, Rama of dreadful prowess appeared resplendent, even like the adorable autumnal Sun invested with a thousand rays. Then Rama, having rendered unto the son of Kushika <note>Vishvamitra</note> all the duties appertaining to a spiritual guide, the three happily spent that night on the banks of the Sarayu <note>A sacred river flowing through the kingdom of Kosala</note>. And although those excellent sons of Dasharatha <note>The King of Ayodhya</note> lay down on an unbeseeming bed of grass, yet in consequence of the sweet converse of the son of Kushika, the night seemed to pass pleasantly away.

## SECTION XXIII.

<margin>✓</margin>
And when the night had passed away, the mighty ascetic spoke unto the descendant of Kakutstha <note>Original: *Kākutstha* — a title for Rama referring to his ancestor King Kakutstha</note>, who was lying down on a bed of leaves—"O Rama, surely hath Kausalya <note>Rama's mother</note> given birth to an excellent son! The first twilight prayer <term>Sandhyā: Devotions that must be performed three times daily—at sunrise, noon, and sunset</term> should now be performed. Do thou, O tiger among men, arise! Thou shouldst perform the purificatory rites and contemplate the gods." Hearing those proper words of the ascetic, those foremost of men, endowed with heroism, bathed, and, sipping water, began to recite the Gayatri <term>Gāyatri: A sacred solar hymn from the Vedas used for meditation and spiritual awakening</term>. And having performed these daily duties, those exceedingly powerful ones, greeting Vishvamitra—who possesses asceticism as his only wealth—stood before him, with the object of starting on their journey.

And as those ones endowed with exceeding prowess were proceeding, at the shining confluence of the Sarayu and the Ganges <note>Original: *Gangā*</note> they beheld a noble river flowing in three branches. And there lay a holy hermitage, belonging to ascetics of subdued souls, where they had been carrying on their high austerities for thousands of years. And beholding that sacred asylum, those descendants of Raghu <note>Rama and Lakshmana</note>, exceedingly delighted, spake unto the high-souled Vishvamitra these words—"Whose is this sacred hermitage? And what man liveth here? O worshipful one, we are desirous of hearing this. Surely, great is our curiosity." 

At those words of theirs, that foremost of ascetics, smiling, said—"Hear, O Rama, as to whom the asylum hath belonged in time past. Kandarpa, called Kama <note>The god of love and desire</note> by the wise, was once incarnate on earth. And it came to pass that as that lord of the deities, Sthanu <note>A name for the god Shiva, meaning 'The Firm' or 'The Eternal'</note>, having performed here his austerities in accordance with the prescribed restrictions, was wending his way in company with the storm-gods <note>Original: *Maruts*</note>, that fool-hardy wight dared disturb his equanimity..."

...of his mind. Thereupon, O descendant of Raghu <note>Original: *Rāghava*</note>, uttering a roar, the high-souled Rudra <note>A name for Shiva in his fierce aspect</note> eyed him steadfastly. And thereat all the limbs of that perverse-hearted one became blasted. And on his body being consumed by that high-souled one, Kāma <note>The god of love and desire</note> was deprived of his person in consequence of the ire of that foremost among the deities; and, O descendant of Raghu, from that time forth, he hath become known as Ananga <note>Original: *Ananga* — literally, "the bodiless one"</note>. And the place where he was deprived of his body is the lovely land of Anga <note>The kingdom of Anga, historically located in modern-day Bihar and West Bengal</note>. And this sacred hermitage belongs to Shiva; and these ascetics engaged in pious acts, O hero, have been from father to son his disciples. And no sin toucheth them. 

Here, O Rama, in the midst of the sacred streams, will we spend the night, O thou of gracious presence, crossing over on the morrow. Let us then, having purified ourselves, enter the holy hermitage! It is highly desirable for us to sojourn here,—here will we happily spend the night, having bathed, and recited the *mantras* <term>Mantra: A sacred sound, syllable, or verse used in prayer and meditation to invoke divine power</term>, and offered oblations unto the sacrificial fire, O best of men!"

As they were conversing thus, the ascetics were highly delighted on discovering them by means of their far-reaching spiritual vision,—and they rejoiced greatly. Then giving Kuçika’s son <note>Referring to Vishvamitra, the son of King Kushika</note> water to wash his feet and *Arghya* <term>Arghya: A ritual offering of water or other ingredients presented to a guest of high honor</term>, and extending unto him also the rites of hospitality, they next entertained Rama and Lakshmana. And having experienced their hospitality, they (the guests) delighted them with their talk. And then the saints with collected minds recited their evening prayers. And having been shown their destined place of rest along with ascetics of excellent vows, they happily passed that night in that hermitage affording every comfort. And that foremost of ascetics, the righteous-souled son of Kuçika, by means of his excellent converse, charmed the prepossessing sons of the monarch.

### SECTION XXIV.

---

THEN next morning, which happened to be fine, those subduers of their foes, with <term>Vishvamitra</term> <note>Original: *Viçwāmitra*</note> at their head, came to the banks of the river <note>The river referred to here is the Ganges</note>. And those noble-minded ascetics who were observing their vows, having brought an elegant boat, addressed <term>Vishvamitra</term>, saying, "Do thou board the boat with the princes at your head! May your journey be auspicious: let no delay occur!"

Thereupon saying, "So be it!", and having paid homage to those ascetics, <term>Vishvamitra</term> set about crossing that river which had replenished the ocean <note>According to Hindu mythology, the saint <term>Agastya</term> once swallowed the ocean. The Ganges later descended from heaven to refill the ocean's bed</note>. 

And it came to pass that while thus engaged, they heard a sound made louder by the dashing of the waves <note>The translator notes a variation in the original Sanskrit: the text says *Cucrāva* ("he heard"), but another reading is *Tatas Cucruvatus Çābdam* ("then they heard a sound")</note>. And having come to the middle of the stream, the highly energetic <term>Rama</term> with his younger brother became curious to find out the cause of that sound. Reaching the middle of the river, <term>Rama</term> asked that best of ascetics, "What is this loud uproar that seems to come tearing through the waters?"

Hearing the words of the descendant of Raghu <note>Original: *Rāghava*, an epithet for Rama</note> prompted by curiosity, that righteous-souled one spoke, unfolding the true cause of that noise: 

"O <term>Rama</term>, there is on Mount <term>Kailash</term> <note>Original: *Kailāça*</note> an exceedingly beautiful lake, created mentally by <term>Brahma</term> <note>The creator deity in the Hindu trinity</note>, O foremost of men—and therefore this watery expanse is known by the name of the <term>Manasa Pool</term> <note>Now known as Lake Manasarovar in the Himalayas</note>. And the stream that issues from that liquid flow runs through <term>Ayodhya</term>: the sacred <term>Sarayu</term> issues from that pool of <term>Brahma</term>. And as the <term>Sarayu</term> meets the <term>Ganges</term> <note>Original: *Jāhnavi*, a name for the Ganges</note>, this tremendous uproar is heard, produced by the clashing of the waters. Do thou, O <term>Rama</term>, bow down to them with a concentrated mind." 

Thereupon, both of—

Those exceedingly righteous ones bowed down before the merging streams. Stepping onto the right bank, they began to move forward with swift energy. Beholding a forest that was both dreadful and without a path, <term>Rama</term> <note>Original: "that son of the foremost of men, Ikshwāku's descendant"</note> asked the great sage:

"Ah! This forest is deep and echoing with the sound of crickets. It is filled with terrible, wild beasts and various birds that shriek with shocking, frightful voices. It is inhabited by lions, tigers, boars, and elephants, and it is thick with many types of trees: the <term>Dhava</term>, the <term>Ashva</term>, the <term>Karna</term>, the <term>Kukubha</term>, the <term>Vilwa</term>, the <term>Tinduka</term>, the <term>Patala</term>, and the <term>Badari</term>. Why is this forest so dreadful?"

The mighty ascetic <term>Vishvamitra</term>, endowed with great spiritual energy, answered him:

"Listen, O <term>Rama</term> <note>Original: Kākutstha, a family name for Rama</note>, to the history of this forbidding forest. Formerly, O prince, there were two flourishing provinces here named Malada and Karusha, which were built by the architects of the gods. 

In the ancient past, during the war to destroy the demon <term>Vritra</term>, <term>Indra</term> <note>Original: "the thousand-eyed one"</note> became stained with the sin of killing a <term>Brahmin</term> <note>Vritra was a powerful dragon-like being who, despite being an enemy of the gods, was born of the priestly caste. Killing him brought a spiritual stain upon Indra</note>. Because of this, Indra suffered from a consuming hunger and was covered in impurities. 

When Indra was thus afflicted, the gods and the great saints—whose only wealth is their spiritual practice—washed him here to cleanse the dirt from his body. The gods deposited the filth and the hunger that had clung to the mighty Indra into this earth. Once he was relieved of it, they were filled with joy. Indra, now purified, returned to his former brightness and was no longer hungry. Mightily pleased with this region, he granted it a wonderful blessing, saying: 'Since these two—'"

"Because these places have held the impurities from my body, they shall be known by the names of Malada and Karusha. They shall attain exceeding prosperity and fame among men." And beholding the land thus honored by the intelligent <term>Shakra</term> <note>Original: Saçra. A common title for Indra, the King of the Gods, meaning 'the powerful one.'</note>, the deities said unto the <term>Subduer of Paka</term> <note>Original: Pāka. An epithet for Indra, referring to his victory over the demon Paka.</note>—"Well done!" "Well done!" And, O repressor of foes, these two places, Malada and Karusha, enjoyed prosperity for a long time and were blessed with corn and wealth. 

Then, after a space of time, there was born a <term>Yakshini</term> <note>A female nature spirit or supernatural being; while often benevolent, they can be terrifying when cursed or malevolent.</note> capable of assuming any form at will, and endowed with the strength of a thousand elephants. Her name—may good fortune attend you!—was <term>Tataka</term> <note>Original: Tārakā.</note> and she was the spouse of the intelligent Sunda. Her son is the <term>Rakshasa</term> <note>A powerful, often man-eating demon or ogre in Hindu mythology.</note> <term>Maricha</term>, who possesses the prowess of Indra himself. <margin>✓</margin> He has rounded arms, a massive head, a wide mouth, and a gigantic body. And that demon of dreadful form daily frightens the people. 

And, O descendant of Raghu, Tataka of wicked deeds daily commits havoc upon these countries, Malada and Karusha. And now, at the distance of over half a <term>Yojana</term> <note>A traditional unit of distance, roughly 8 to 9 miles.</note>, she stays here, obstructing the way. Since this forest belongs to Tataka, you should go there; and, relying on the might of your own arms, slay this one of wicked deeds. By my direction, do you again rid this region of its thorn; for no one dares to approach such a place, infested as it is, O Rama, by the dreadful and unbearable Yakshini. And now I have related to you all about this fearful forest. To this day, that Yakshini has not ceased from committing ravages right and left."

### BĀLAKĀNDAM.

“How then can one of the weaker sex possess the strength of a thousand elephants?” Hearing these words uttered by <term>Raghava</term> <note>Original: Rāghava. A title for Rama, meaning "descendant of King Raghu."</note> of immeasurable energy, <term>Vishvamitra</term>—delighting that subduer of foes, Rama, and Lakshmana with his amiable words—said:

“Listen now to the means by which she attained such terrible strength; this woman came to possess this power and prowess by virtue of a divine boon. In former times, there was a mighty and exceedingly powerful <term>Yaksha</term> <note>A class of nature spirits or supernatural beings who act as caretakers of the natural world and treasures.</note> named Suketu. He was childless, yet he followed pure practices and performed rigid austerities. 

And, O Rama, the <term>Grand-sire</term> <note>Original: Brahma. The creator god of the Hindu trinity, often called the "Grandfather" of all beings.</note>, well pleased with that lord of the Yakshas, conferred upon him a gem of a daughter by the name of <term>Taraka</term>. The Grand-sire endowed her with the strength of a thousand elephants; yet that illustrious one did not bestow a son upon that Yaksha. When she had grown and attained her youth and beauty, he gave that famous damsel to Sunda, the son of Jambha, to be his wife.

After a length of time, that <term>Yakshini</term> <note>A female Yaksha.</note> gave birth to a son named <term>Maricha</term>, who possessed irrepressible energy—he who eventually became a <term>Rakshasa</term> <note>A powerful, often man-eating demon or ogre in Hindu mythology.</note> as the result of a curse. And, O Rama, when Sunda had been destroyed, Taraka and her son set their hearts upon afflicting the excellent saint <term>Agastya</term> <note>A legendary and powerful sage credited with many miraculous deeds.</note>. Enraged with Agastya, she rushed at him with a roar, intending to devour him. 

Upon seeing her rushing thus, that worshipful saint Agastya said to Maricha, ‘May you become a Rakshasa!’ and, in exceeding wrath, he also cursed Taraka: ‘O mighty Yakshini, since you have desired to eat a human being while in this frightful guise with a hideous face, leave your original shape immediately and take on a terrible form!’

Thus cursed by Agastya and overwhelmed with rage, Taraka now lays waste to this fair region where Agastya carries out his austerities. Therefore, O descendant of Raghu, for the welfare of <term>Brahmins</term> <note>The priestly and scholarly class of ancient India.</note> and cows, you must slay this exceedingly terrible Yakshini of wicked ways and vile prowess! “<term>Descendant of Raghu</term> <note>Original: Raghu. Nor should you, O best of men, shrink from slaying a woman; for even this should be accomplished by a prince in the interests of the <term>four social orders</term> <note>The traditional classes of society: priests, warriors, merchants, and laborers.</note>. Whether an act be cruel or otherwise, slightly or highly sinful, it must be performed by a ruler for the protection of his subjects. For those engaged in the heavy task of government, this is the eternal rule of conduct. Do you, O <term>descendant of Kakutstha</term> <note>Original: Kakutstha. Another ancestral title for Rama.</note>, slay this impious one, for she knows no righteousness! We hear, O king, that in days of old, <term>Indra</term> <note>Original: Sakra. And formerly, O Rama, <term>Vishnu</term> <note>The Preserver god.</note> destroyed the mother of <term>Kavya</term> <note>Kavya is another name for Shukra, the preceptor of the demons.</note>, the devoted wife of the sage <term>Bhrigu</term>, who had set her heart upon making the world devoid of Indra.
<margin>✓</margin>
By these examples, as well as by innumerable other princes—the foremost of men—have wicked women been slain. Therefore, O king, renouncing any personal dislike, do you, by my command, slay this woman."

### SECTION XXVI.

---

Hearing those bold words of the ascetic, the son of that foremost of men, <term>Rama</term> <note>Original: Rāghava.</note>, firm in his vows, answered with clasped hands: “In accordance with the desire of my father, and in order to honor it, I ought fearlessly to do even as <term>the son of Kushika</term> <note>Original: Kuçika's son. Refers to the sage Vishvamitra.</note> says. Having been instructed to that end while at <term>Ayodhya</term> <note>The capital city and Rama's home.</note> by that high-souled one, my father <term>Dasharatha</term>, in the presence of my spiritual guides, I ought not to ignore your words. Therefore, commanded by you, a keeper of the <term>sacred knowledge</term> <note>Original: Veda.</note>, I will, in accordance with my father's mandate, undoubtedly bring about that welcome event—the death of <term>Taraka</term>.”

### BALAKANDAM. 69

And in the interests of <term>priests</term> <note>Original: Brāhmaṇas. The scholarly and ritualistic class of society.</note>, <term>sacred cows</term> <note>Original: kine. Cattle were viewed as symbols of life and religious duty.</note>, and <term>gods</term> <note>Original: celestials.</note>, I am ready to act as desired by thee of immeasurable energy." Having said this, that <term>subduer of enemies</term> <note>Original: repressor of foes. A common title for Rama denoting his martial prowess.</note>, with clenched fist, twanged his bow-string, filling the <term>ten directions</term> <note>The four cardinal points, the four intermediate points, the zenith, and the nadir.</note> with the sounds. And at those sounds, the dwellers in Taraka's forest were filled with perturbation—and Taraka also amazed at those sounds, became exceedingly wroth. And, rendered almost insensible by anger, that <term>demoness</term> <note>Original: Rakshasi. A malevolent, shape-shifting being.</note> furiously rushed in amain towards the spot whence had come the report. 

And beholding that frightful one of hideous visage and colossal proportions, transported with rage, <term>Rama</term> <note>Original: Raghu's descendant. Rama is a member of the solar dynasty founded by King Raghu.</note> spake unto <term>Lakshmana</term> <note>Rama's younger brother and constant companion.</note>—"Behold, O Lakshmana, the terrible and hideous body of yonder <term>spirit-woman</term> <note>Original: Yakshini. A class of supernatural beings; here, one who has become monstrous.</note>! The sight of her striketh terror into the hearts of even the brave. <margin>✓</margin>Mark! This irrepressible one, possessing all the resources of illusion, will I oppose, and deprive her of ears and nose. But I dare not slay her, she being protected by virtue of her feminineness. I intend only to oppose her course, and deprive her of her prowess." 

As Rama was speaking thus, Taraka, deprived of sense through ire, uttering roars, with uplifted arms rushed against him. And thereat the <term>celestial sage</term> <note>Original: Brahmar-shi. The highest class of sages, possessing divine knowledge.</note>, <term>Vishvamitra</term> <note>The sage who is guiding the young princes.</note>, uttering a roar, upbraided her, and said—"**Blessings!** <note>Original: Swasti. A Sanskrit word used for benediction or wishing success.</note> May victory attend the descendants of Raghu!" 

And raising thick clouds of dust, Taraka instantly bewildered both the descendants of Raghu. And then by help of illusion, she began to pour upon them a mighty shower of crags. And thereat Rama was wroth. And resisting that mighty shower of crags by vollies of shafts, <term>Rama</term> <note>Original: Rāghava. Another name for Rama, meaning 'born of Raghu'.</note> with arrows cut off her hands. And with the fore-parts of her arms lopped off, as she was roaring before them, <term>Lakshmana</term> <note>Original: Sumitra's son. Lakshmana's mother was Queen Sumitra.</note> waxing wroth deprived her of her ears and nose. <margin>✓</margin>Thereupon that one, capable of assuming forms at will, began to assume various shapes; and to vanish from

Thus addressed, Rama, displaying his <term>skill in aiming by sound</term> <note>Original: skill in aiming by sounds. In mythology, these "sons" are personified magical weapons (astras) gifted by the creator-lord Krishashva.</note>, of true prowess, and charged with ascetic energy. And ever following thee, he, O <term>priest-sage</term>, is fit to receive them of thee. And this son of the king is to accomplish a mighty task in the interests of the <term>gods</term>.” Saying this, the deities, having paid homage unto Vishvamitra, joyfully entered the celestial regions.

And now came evening on, when that best of ascetics, gratified at the destruction of Taraka, <term>blessed Rama with a kiss on the head</term> <note>Original: smelt Rāma’s crown. A traditional Indian gesture of deep paternal affection and spiritual blessing (mūrdhni-āghrāṇa).</note>.

### SECTION XXVII.

->[ornamental horizontal flourish]<-

Having passed that night, the illustrious Vishvamitra, smiling with satisfaction, spoke sweetly to the <term>descendant of Raghu</term> <note>Original: Raghava. A patronymic for Rama, whose lineage descends from King Raghu.</note>, saying,—“I am pleased with you. May prosperity be yours, O highly famous prince! With supreme pleasure, I confer upon you all the <term>divine weapons</term> <note>Original: Astras. And all those celestial weapons—may they bring you good fortune—I will confer upon you. And I will confer upon you, O Rama, the celestial and mighty <term>Discus of Punishment</term> <note>Original: Dandachakra.</note>, and the <term>Discus of Righteousness</term> <note>Original: Dharmachakra.</note>, and also the <term>Discus of Time</term> <note>Original: Kalachakra.</note>. O prince, I bestow upon you the peerless <term>Brahma weapon</term> <note>The most powerful weapon in the epics, associated with the creator god Brahma.</note>, and O <term>descendant of Kakutstha</term> <note>original: Kākutstha; a patronymic for Rama.</note>, the two excellent maces, the flaming <term>Modaki</term> <note>Meaning 'the Intoxicating'.</note> and <term>Cikhari</term> <note>Meaning 'the Peaked'.</note>. # THE BOOK OF CHILDHOOD <note>original: Bālakāṇḍam</note>

He also bestowed upon <term>Rama</term> <note>original: Rāghava; a title meaning 'descendant of King Raghu'.</note> those weapons, which even the gods are incapable of possessing in their entirety. As that intelligent ascetic, <term>Vishvamitra</term> <note>A former king turned powerful sage.</note>, recited <term>sacred incantations</term> <note>original: mantras</note>, all those invaluable weapons appeared before that descendant of Raghu. And, with hands joined in respect, they addressed Rama with great pleasure: “We are your servants, O highly generous <term>descendant of Raghu</term>. ### SECTION XXIX.

Incarnation
of Vishnu</margin>
HEARING those words of Rama of measureless prowess, who had asked the question, the highly energetic <term>Vishvamitra</term> <note>original: Viçwāmitra; the great sage and former king guiding Rama.</note> answered, saying,—“Here, O mighty-armed Rama, <term>Vishnu</term> <note>The Preserver god of the Hindu trinity.</note> of mighty asceticism worshipped of all the deities, for years upon years, and hundreds of <gloss>ages</gloss> <note>original: Yugas; vast epochs of time in Hindu cosmology.</note> Yugas, dwelt for carrying on his austerities and <term>Yoga</term> <note>Spiritual discipline involving meditation and asceticism.</note>. This, O Rama, was formerly the hermitage of the high-souled <term>Vamana</term> <note>original: Vāmana; the fifth incarnation of Vishnu, who appeared as a dwarf.</note>. And this is famed as <term>Siddhashrama</term> <note>original: Siddhāçrama; literally "The Hermitage of Fulfillment," where one attains spiritual perfection.</note>, in consequence of that one of potent asceticism having attained fruition there.

And it came to pass that at this time Virochana’s son, king <term>Bali</term> <note>original: Vāli; this refers to the demon-king Bali (or Mahabali), not the monkey-king of the same name found later in the epic.</note>, having vanquished the celestials with <term>Indra</term> <note>The King of the Gods.</note> and the <term>Maruts</term> <note>Storm deities who serve as Indra's companions.</note>, established that dominion of his, famous in the three worlds. And that mighty chief of the <term>Asuras</term> <note>A class of powerful, often titanic beings in constant conflict with the gods.</note> celebrated a sacrifice. And as Bali was performing that sacrifice, the deities with <term>Agni</term> <note>The god of fire.</note> at their head, coming unto Vishnu himself at this asylum, addressed him, saying,—‘Virochana’s son, Bali, O Vishnu, is celebrating a sacrifice. Do thou, before the ceremony is finished, accomplish thy own end. He duly confers upon such as repair unto him from various quarters all those things that they ask for. And do thou thyself, O Vishnu, aided by thy power of illusion, assuming a Dwarf-form, accomplish the welfare of the gods.’

In the meantime, O Rama, the wonderful <term>Kashyapa</term> <note>original: Kaçyapa; a venerable ancient sage, father of both gods and demons.</note> resembling fire in splendour, and flaming in energy, having in company with, and with the assistance of, the divine <term>Aditi</term> <note>The mother of the gods.</note>, O Rama, accomplished his vow, lasting

->RAMAYANA<-

...for a hundred years, began to sing hymns to the <term>destroyer of Madhu</term> <note>original: Madhusūdana; a name for the god Vishnu, referring to his victory over the demon Madhu.</note>, who was ready to grant blessings. "By means of intense spiritual heat, I behold you as the embodiment of penance, a mass of sacred disciplines, and endowed with a form and a soul of pure austerity. In your person, O Lord, I see this entire universe. In you—who have no beginning and who cannot be fully defined—do I take refuge!"

Thereupon, exceedingly pleased, <term>Hari</term> <note>A title for Vishnu, meaning "the one who takes away" (sins or suffering).</note> spoke to <term>Kashyapa</term> <note>original: Kaçyapa; an ancient sage and the father of the gods.</note>, whose sins had been washed away, saying: "Ask for a blessing! May good fortune attend you. I believe you deserve a boon."

Hearing these words, the son of Marichi, Kashyapa, said: "<term>Aditi</term> <note>The mother of the gods.</note>, the gods, and I myself crave this of you—it is right for you, who are pleased with us, to grant this blessing, O you of excellent vows! O sinless one, may you be born as my son through Aditi, O adorable deity! Become the younger brother of <term>Shakra</term> <note>original: Çakra; another name for Indra, the King of the Gods.</note>, O destroyer of the <term>Asuras</term> <note>Powerful beings often in conflict with the gods.</note>. It is your duty to help the heavenly beings who are afflicted with grief. And through your grace, this place will attain the name <term>Siddhashrama</term> <note>Literally "The Hermitage of Fulfillment."</note>. The work, O Lord of the celestials, has been accomplished. Do you now, O you of the <term>six attributes</term> <note>original: Ṣaḍguṇa; the divine qualities of knowledge, sovereignty, strength, power, energy, and splendor.</note>, ascend from <insert>Handwritten marking in margin</insert> here!"

And accordingly, <term>Vishnu</term> of mighty energy took his birth through Aditi. Assuming the form of a <term>dwarf</term> <note>This refers to the Vamana avatar, the fifth incarnation of Vishnu.</note>, he presented himself before the son of Virochana <note>This refers to King Bali, a powerful ruler of the Asuras who had seized control of the three worlds.</note>. Asking for as much earth as could be covered by **three footsteps**, that one who is ever engaged in the welfare of all creatures—with the object of securing the good of all—stood occupying the entire universe in his cosmic form. Having by his power restrained <term>Bali</term> <note>original: Vāli (in this transcription); the king of the Asuras.</note>, that one of exceeding energy again granted the three worlds to the mighty Indra and made them subject to his control.

Formerly, Vishnu used to dwell in this asylum, which is capable of removing all fatigue. Out of reverence for the Dwarf incarnation, I reside here now. But this hermitage is currently infested by <term>Rakshasas</term> <note>Shape-shifting demons who disrupt holy rites.</note> who disturb our sacred rituals. O most powerful of men, you should slay those of wicked ways here. Today, O Rama, I will return to this supremely excellent hermitage.

->**The Hermitage of Fulfillment**<-

"The <term>Hermitage of Fulfillment</term> <note>original: Siddhāçrama; the place where one attains spiritual perfection or "Siddhi."</note>. And this sanctuary, child, belongs as much to you as it does to me.” Saying this, and taking <term>Rama</term> and <term>Lakshmana</term> with him, the powerful ascetic—feeling immense delight—entered that sanctuary. In doing so, he appeared as graceful as the Moon emerging from the mist when it joins the <term>Punarvasu stars</term> <note>A constellation in the night sky; the name implies the "return of the light."</note>. 

Upon seeing <margin>7</margin> <term>Vishvamitra</term> <note>original: Viçwāmitra</note>, the ascetics who lived in the Hermitage of Fulfillment rose suddenly in joy and worshipped that intelligent man. They also extended the traditional rites of hospitality to the princes. After they had rested for a short while, those blameless princes—the <term>descendants of Raghu</term>—with their hands pressed together in respect, addressed that leader of ascetics: 

“May you be initiated into the ceremony this very day. May good fortune attend you, O best of ascetics! Let this Hermitage of Fulfillment truly reach its fruition, and let your words be proven true!” 

Thus addressed, that powerful saint of immense energy, with a subdued mind and his senses under total restraint, began the initiation for the ceremony. Like the <term>Divine Youths</term> <note>original: Kumāras; referring to the warrior gods Skanda and Vishakha.</note>, Rama and Lakshmana passed the night pleasantly and rose in the morning. After finishing their morning worship and reciting the primary <term>sacred chants</term> <note>original: mantras</note> with purity and self-control, they paid their respects to the sacrificial fire and to the master of the sacrifice, Vishvamitra, who was seated.

### RĀMĀYANA

The ascetics, greatly pleased, began to praise the sons of the king. “From this day forward, you must protect us for six nights,” they said. “This ascetic <note>Referring to Vishvamitra.</note> has been initiated into the <term>sacrifice</term> <note>A Vedic ritual or 'Yajna' performed to maintain cosmic order or achieve specific spiritual goals.</note> and must therefore observe a vow of silence.”

Hearing these words, the illustrious princes renounced sleep and began to guard the hermitage for six days and nights. Those heroic and mighty archers, wearing their armor, protected that greatest of ascetics and conqueror of enemies. 

When time had passed and the sixth day arrived, <term>Rama</term> said to <term>Lakshmana</term> (the son of Sumitrā), “Being well equipped, stay vigilant!” When Rama—showing his eagerness for the coming encounter—had said this, the priests and spiritual guides lit the altar. Along with <term>Vishvamitra</term> and the family priests, they prepared the altar, furnishing it with <term>Kuça</term> and <term>Kāça</term> <note>Sacred varieties of grass used in Vedic purification rituals.</note>, as well as ladles, firewood, and flowers. 

As they began to perform the sacrifice properly by reciting <term>mantras</term> <note>Sacred chants or prayers used to invoke divine power.</note>, a mighty and dreadful uproar arose in the sky. Just as during the rainy season masses of clouds appear and shroud the heavens <note>original: "gamanam avaryya"—obstructing passage. This appears to be a corrupted reading in the original manuscript; it should likely be "gaganam avaryya"—enveloping the firmament.</note>, the <term>Rākshasas</term> <note>Powerful shape-shifting demons or celestial ogres who sought to disrupt holy rites.</note> began to rush forward, using their powers of illusion. 

<term>Māricha</term> and <term>Suvāhu</term>, appearing in terrifying forms together with their followers, began to shower blood down upon the altar. Seeing the altar deluged with gore, Rama suddenly rushed forward and looked up into the sky. Seeing them charging down in a frenzy, the lotus-eyed Rama fixed his gaze on Lakshmana and said:

“Behold, O Lakshmana! By means of a <term>Mānava weapon</term> <note>A mystical projectile associated with Manu, the progenitor of humanity; it is often used to blow enemies away like a gale.</note>, I shall undoubtedly drive away these wicked, flesh-eating Rākshasas, just as the wind drives away the clouds. Truly, I cannot yet bring myself to slay such as these.” 

Saying this, Rama—the <term>descendant of Raghu</term> <note>A title for Rama, referring to his ancestor King Raghu, founder of their solar dynasty.</note>—fixed an exceedingly mighty and gloriously dazzling Mānava weapon upon his bow and discharged it with great force.

<summary>Rama and Lakshmana guard Vishvamitra's ritual for six days until the demons Maricha and Suvahu attack by raining blood on the altar. Rama prepares to repel the invaders using the mystical Manava weapon.</summary>

# BALAKANDAM

...wrath at Māricha's chest. And wounded by that foremost of <term>Mānava weapons</term> <note>A celestial weapon associated with Manu, the legendary first man and lawgiver.</note>, *Māricha* was carried off a full hundred *Yojanas* <note>An ancient measure of distance; a hundred yojanas would be roughly 800 miles.</note> and dropped into the middle of the ocean. Finding *Māricha* senseless, spinning, and afflicted by the might of the weapon—overcome, yet still alive—Rāma addressed Lakshmana, saying: 

"Behold, O Lakshmana, this <term>Mānava weapon</term> first used by <term>Manu</term>; it has deprived him of his senses and carried him away, and yet it has not taken his life! But these shameless, wicked, and blood-drinking <term>Rākshasas</term> <note>Demonic beings who disrupt sacred rituals and feed on flesh.</note>, who are addicted to wrongdoing and are disturbers of sacrifices, I shall now slaughter."

Having said this, and instantly showing Lakshmana his quickness of hand, the descendant of <term>Raghu</term> <note>Rama, who belongs to the prestigious solar dynasty founded by King Raghu.</note> took out a mighty <term>Agneya weapon</term> <note>A "fire-weapon" powered by Agni, the god of fire.</note> and discharged it at the breast of *Suvāhu*. Pierced by that shaft, he fell down dead upon the ground. Then, taking a <term>Vāyavya weapon</term> <note>A "wind-weapon" powered by Vayu, the god of the air.</note>, the illustrious and exceedingly generous <term>Rāghava</term> <note>A title for Rama, meaning 'the descendant of Raghu.'</note> brought delight to those ascetics by slaying the rest of the demons.

Having destroyed all those <term>Rākshasas</term> who were disturbing the sacrifices, the descendant of *Raghu* was honored by the saints, just as <term>Indra</term> <note>The King of the Gods.</note> was honored in days of old after having vanquished the <term>Asuras</term> <note>The ancient enemies of the gods.</note>. And when the sacrifice had been completed, the mighty ascetic <term>Viçwāmitra</term>, seeing all sides cleared of the <term>Rākshasas</term>, spoke to <term>Kākutstha</term> <note>Another ancestral name for Rama.</note>, saying: 

"O mighty-armed one, I have obtained my desire; and thou hast executed thy preceptor’s mandate. O illustrious hero, thou hast truly made this a <term>Siddhāçrama</term> <note>Literally the 'Hermitage of Fulfillment' or 'Success.'</note>." 

Having thus praised Rāma, he took Rāma and Lakshmana to perform his evening devotions.

### SECTION XXXII.
---

Once upon a time there was a mighty son of **Brahmā** <note>The creator god in the Hindu trinity.</note>, of high austerities, named **Kuça**. And he was cognizant of duty, and ever engaged in observing vows and honoring good men. And that high-souled one begat on **Vaidarbhi** <note>original: "Vaidarbhi," meaning a princess from the Kingdom of Vidarbha.</note>, sprung from a respectable line, and endowed with all noble qualities, four sons like unto himself, and possessed of extraordinary prowess—**Kuçāmva**, and **Kuçanābha**, and **Asurtarajas**, and **Vasu**, resplendent and breathing exhaustless spirits. 

And with the desire of enhancing <term>Kshetrya</term> <note>Also spelled *Kshatriya*; the social class of kings, warriors, and administrators whose duty is to protect the people.</note> merit, **Kuça** said unto his truthful and virtuous sons,—‘Ye sons! do ye engage in the task of governing,—and thereby acquire immense merit.’ Hearing **Kuça's** words, those four

The foremost of men and best of sons set about establishing the seats of their government. The highly energetic **Kucāmva** founded the city of **Kaucāmvi**; the righteous **Kuçanābha** founded the capital of **Mahodaya**; the noble-minded **Asurtarajas** founded **Dharmaranya**; and King **Vasu** founded **Girivraja**, the finest of capitals. This city, surrounded by five mighty, shining mountains—also known as **Vasumati**—belongs to the high-souled Vasu. The river known by the name of **Sumāgadhi** flows through the lands of the **Magadhas**. Running through the center of those five peaks, it looks like a garland. This river, the **Māgadhi**, O **Rāma**, belongs to the noble Vasu; it follows an easterly course, O Rāma, flowing through fertile fields rich with grain.

And, O descendant of **Raghu**, the virtuous <term>Rājarshi</term> <note>A "Royal Sage"—a king who has attained a high state of spiritual enlightenment.</note> **Kuçanābha** fathered one hundred peerless daughters by **Ghritāchi** <note>A celestial nymph or Apsara.</note>. It so happened that these girls—blossoming in youth, beautiful as lightning in the monsoon, and adorned in fine jewelry—went to their garden. There they were merrily singing, dancing, and playing musical instruments, O **Rāghava** <note>A title for Rama, meaning "descendant of Raghu."</note>!

As they spent time in the garden—perfect in every limb, unparalleled in beauty on earth, gifted with every virtue, and full of youthful grace—they looked like stars nestled among the clouds. The "Life of All," the **Air** <note>The god Vayu, deity of the wind.</note>, saw them and said: “I desire you; become my wives. Cast off this human form and gain long lives. Youth is fleeting, especially for mortals; by attaining unfading youth, you shall become immortal.”

Hearing these words from the Wind, who possesses ever-fresh energy, the maidens laughed at him and said: “O foremost of gods, you move through the hearts of all creatures, and we are well aware of your power. Why then do you insult us? O divine one, we are the daughters of **Kuçanābha**. Being divine ourselves in spirit, we could strike you from your place, but we hold back to protect our <term>ascetic merit</term> <note>Spiritual power or "capital" earned through self-discipline; using it to curse someone would deplete their hard-earned holiness.</note>. O foolish one, may the time never come when we disregard...”

“...our truthful father; we, following our own sense of duty, shall abide by his choice. Our father is truly our lord and our supreme god. We shall only become the wives of the man to whom our father gives us away.” 

At these words of theirs, that powerful and worshipful deity, the **Air** <note>The god Vayu, the personification of the wind and the "vital breath" that dwells within living beings.</note>, became exceedingly enraged. He then entered into their bodies and broke all their limbs. Their bodies being thus broken by the **Air**, those young women—exceedingly distressed and overwhelmed with shame—entered the residence of the king with tears in their eyes. 

Finding his supremely beautiful and favorite daughters with their limbs broken and in such a miserable state, the bewildered king asked: “My daughters, what is this? Who is it that כך disregards <term>Dharma</term> <note>The sacred law of right conduct and cosmic order.</note>? By whose hand have you all come to have these crooked forms? Why do you only show me your grief and not answer me?” Having said this, the king heaved a deep sigh and became eager to hear the full account.

***

### SECTION XXXIII.

***

Hearing those words of the wise **Kushanabha**, his hundred daughters touched his feet with their heads and said: “O King, the ‘Life of All,’ the **Air**, desired to overcome us by taking an improper path; he showed no regard for morality. We told him: ‘We have a father, may prosperity be with you; we have no will of our own. Do you ask our father about this; if he consents, he will give us to you.’ But that wicked being did not listen to our words; and even as we were saying this, we were roughly handled by him.” 

Hearing those words of theirs, the highly pious and powerful king addressed his hundred beautiful daughters, saying: “You have displayed a magnificent example of forgiveness, a virtue which all who seek to be patient should follow. That you have unanimously protected the honor of my house is also a matter for high praise. Forbearance is a virtue alike to men and women...”

<summary>The daughters of Kushanabha reject the advances of the wind god Vayu, asserting their father's right to choose their husbands. Enraged, Vayu physically deforms them, leading to a scene where the King praises his daughters' resilience and commitment to family honor.</summary>
<keywords>Kushanabha, Vayu, Wind God, Dharma, forgiveness, family honor, physical transformation, Rama, Vishvamitra</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the speech of King Kushanabha to his hundred daughters, who have just been physically cursed by the Wind God for refusing his advances. The king praises their patience and then the narrative shifts to the origin of the king who will eventually marry them.</meta>

-># RAMAYANA<-

“...for women, <term>forbearance</term> <note>The Sanskrit word is *kshama*, meaning patience, forgiveness, or the strength to endure. In this context, it refers to the daughters' decision not to curse the god back.</note> is an ornament. It is a difficult thing to practice, especially when dealing with the <term>celestials</term> <note>The gods, or *Devas*.</note>. May every descendant of mine possess a spirit of forgiveness like yours! Forbearance is charity; forbearance is truth; forbearance, my daughters, is the highest sacrifice; forbearance is fame; forbearance is virtue—indeed, the entire universe is established upon this virtue.”

Then, dismissing his daughters, the king—who possessed the power of the gods and was wise in statecraft—began to consult with his ministers regarding the marriage of his daughters. He sought to find a husband of equal lineage and merit, considering the proper time and place for such a union.

It happened that at this time, there was an ascetic named Chuli. He was a man of great spiritual radiance, a practitioner of pure deeds who had mastered his senses and was performing <term>Brahmya</term> <note>Austerities related to the creator god, Brahma, or seeking the ultimate reality.</note> penances. While this saint was engaged in his meditations, a <term>Gandharvi</term> <note>A celestial musician or nymph.</note> named Somada—the daughter of Urmila—served him faithfully. 

With great humility, that virtuous woman spent a long period of time ministering to his needs. Eventually, her spiritual teacher was deeply pleased with her service. O descendant of Raghu, he once said to her: 

“I am gratified by you, may prosperity be yours! What blessing shall I grant you?” 

Recognizing that the hermit was pleased, the Gandharvi, who was skilled in the art of speech, was delighted. She sweetly addressed the sage: 

“You are filled with the fire of sacred knowledge; you are like the Creator himself and possess great spiritual power. I desire from you a righteous son, gifted with the virtues of a holy ascetic. I have no husband, may you be blessed, and I am not the wife of any man. To me, who am your servant, you should grant such a child through your spiritual power.”

Well pleased with her, the <term>Brahmarshi</term> <note>A "God-sage" or "Divine Seer," the highest class of sages.</note> Chuli granted her an excellent, "mind-begotten" son named Brahmadatta <note>Literally "Given by Brahma."</note>. Later, King Brahmadatta founded the flourishing city of Kampilya, just as the ruler of the gods established the heavenly realms. 

And so it was, O <term>Kakutstha</term> <note>A title for Rama, meaning "descendant of Kakutstha."</note>, that the righteous King Kushanabha finally decided to bestow his hundred daughters in marriage to this Brahmadatta.

<summary>King Kushanabha praises his daughters' endurance before the story shifts to the miraculous birth of Brahmadatta, the son of the sage Chuli and the nymph Somada. Kushanabha decides that Brahmadatta is the worthy suitor for his hundred daughters.</summary>
<keywords>forbearance (kshama), Kushanabha, Chuli, Somada, Brahmadatta, Kampilya, asceticism, Gandharvi</keywords>

...daughters to Brahmadatta. And inviting Brahmadatta, that highly energetic king, Kushanabha conferred his hundred daughters upon him with a joyful heart. 

O descendant of Raghu, King Brahmadatta—who resembled the lord of the <term>celestials</term> <note>The gods, specifically Indra.</note>—took their hands in marriage one by one. As soon as he touched them, the hundred daughters were cured of their crookedness; they were freed from their physical suffering and were once again endowed with supreme beauty. 

Upon seeing his daughters delivered from the curse of the <term>Air</term> <note>The Wind God, Vayu, had physically deformed the daughters when they rejected his romantic advances. This restoration marks the end of their penance.</note>, King Kushanabha became exceedingly delighted and rejoiced again and again. He then dismissed the newly married King Brahmadatta, who departed with his wives, the royal priests, and his attendants. The <term>Gandharvi</term> Somada rejoiced deeply at the completion of her son’s wedding; embracing her daughters-in-law repeatedly and praising her son, she expressed the fullness of her joy.

---

### SECTION XXXIV

---

“And, O <term>Raghava</term> <note>A title for Rama, meaning 'descendant of King Raghu.'</note>, when Brahmadatta was married, the sonless King Kushanabha took up a <term>sacrifice</term> <note>Likely the *Putrakameshti*, a specialized Vedic ritual performed to invoke the birth of a son.</note> with the intention of obtaining male offspring. 

When the sacrifice began, the noble Kusha—who was a son of the creator-god Brahma <note>Kusha is Kushanabha’s father and the patriarch of this royal line.</note>—spoke to King Kushanabha, saying: 

‘O son, there will be born to you a virtuous son just like yourself. You shall obtain a son named Gadhi, and through him, you will achieve enduring fame in this world.’ 

Having said this to King Kushanabha, Kusha ascended into the sky and returned to the eternal regions of Brahma. After some time, an eminently virtuous son named Gadhi was born to the wise Kushanabha. 

O <term>Kakutstha</term> <note>Another name for Rama, referring to his ancestor Kakutstha.</note>, that highly pious Gadhi is my own father. And I, known as <term>Kaushika</term> <note>The name 'Kaushika' identifies the narrator as being of the lineage of Kusha. The narrator is the Great Sage Vishvamitra.</note>, am sprung from the line of Kusha. And, O...”

“<term>Raghava</term><note>A title for Rama, meaning 'descendant of King Raghu.'</note>, I had an elder sister of noble vows. Her name was Satyavati, and she was given in marriage to the sage <term>Richika</term><note>A powerful ascetic and member of the Bhrigu clan.</note>. Following her husband, she eventually ascended to heaven in her own physical body. 

My highly generous sister, <term>Kaushiki</term><note>Now known as the Kosi River, which flows through Nepal and India. In Hindu tradition, rivers are often personified as goddesses.</note>, has ultimately assumed the form of a mighty river. To ensure the welfare of all living creatures, my sister now flows as a noble and charming river of sacred waters, originating from the <term>Himavat</term><note>The Himalayas, personified as a mountain god.</note> mountains. 

Because of my deep affection for my sister Kaushiki, I have ever since lived happily in the vicinity of the Himalayas, O Raghava. That virtuous Satyavati—as firmly established in religion as she is in truth, chaste and eminently pious—is now the foremost of streams. 

O Rama, it was only for the purpose of completing my <term>sacrifice</term><note>A Vedic ritual (yagna) that Vishvamitra was performing, which was being disrupted by demons.</note> that I left her behind and came to <term>Siddhashrama</term><note>The 'Hermitage of Fulfillment,' the sacred site where the current action is taking place.</note>. And now, by the power of your energy, I have attained success in my goal. 

I have now narrated to you, O mighty-armed one, the circumstances connected with the history of my lineage and myself, as well as the history of this place, which you had asked me to relate. 

But, O <term>Kakutstha</term><note>An ancestral name for Rama, referring to his ancestor Puranjaya who rode on the 'hump' (kakud) of a bull.</note>, while I was speaking, half the night has already passed. You must sleep now—may good fortune attend you—so that you do not feel any exhaustion during our journey tomorrow. 

The trees stand motionless, the beasts and birds are silent, and, O descendant of Raghu, all sides have become wrapped in the gloom of night. The midnight is gradually slipping away; the sky, studded thick with stars that resemble many eyes, is lit up by their glow. The mild-beaming moon, that dispeller of darkness, is rising, gladdening the hearts of all creatures with his splendor. Now, night-wandering beings—terrible, flesh-eating <term>Yakshas</term> and <term>Rakshasas</term><note>Nature spirits and demonic beings that grow powerful in the darkness.</note>—roam here and there.” 

Having said this, the mighty ascetic of great energy fell silent. The other ascetics, honoring him, cried out, “Excellent! Excellent! This lineage of the <term>Kushikas</term><note>The clan of the patriarch Kusha, to which Vishvamitra belongs.</note> is forever exalted and devoted to virtue. And those foremost of men born in the line of Kusha...”

### SECTION XXXV.

---

Having passed the remainder of the night on the banks of the <term>Sone</term><note>A major southern tributary of the Ganges River.</note> in the company of the ascetics, Vishvamitra spoke as the day broke: “O Rama, the night has passed, and the morning has come. The hour for performing your early devotions has arrived. Arise! Arise! May good fortune attend you! Prepare for our departure.” 

Hearing these words, Rama finished his morning devotions and rites, and being ready for departure, said: “This is the Sone, with its excellent waters, fathomless depth, and many islands. O <term>Brahmin</term><note>A member of the priestly or scholarly class.</note>, by which way shall we cross?” 

Thus addressed by Rama, Vishvamitra replied: “I have already decided upon this path—the same one that the <term>Maharshis</term><note>The 'Great Seers' or most exalted sages.</note> travel.” 

And having proceeded far, when the day was half spent, they beheld that foremost of streams, the <term>Jahnavi</term><note>A name for the River Ganges (Ganga), referring to the legend of the sage Jahnu.</note>, which is worshipped by ascetics. Upon seeing that river, filled with sacred waters and frequented by swans and cranes, the ascetics who accompanied <term>Raghava</term><note>A title for Rama, meaning 'descendant of King Raghu.'</note> were exceedingly delighted. They took up their quarters on the banks of the river. Then, after bathing and duly offering oblations of water to the gods and the spirits of their ancestors, and performing <term>Agnihotra</term><note>A Vedic ritual involving a sacrifice made into a consecrated fire.</note> sacrifices, and partaking of...

# RAMAYANA

...clarified butter like nectar, those noble and auspicious souls sat down with joyful hearts around <term>Vishvamitra</term><note>A famous king-turned-sage who serves as a mentor and spiritual guide to Rama and Lakshmana.</note>. Then <term>Rama</term>, his heart overflowing with cheerfulness, spoke to Vishvamitra, saying:

"O adorable one, I wish to hear how the <term>Ganga</term><note>The Ganges river, the most sacred river in Hindu tradition, believed to have a celestial origin.</note>, which flows in three directions and embraces the three worlds, falls into the ocean, the lord of all streams and rivers."

Moved by Rama’s request, the powerful ascetic Vishvamitra began the history of the Ganga’s origin and journey.

"O Rama, the great storehouse of minerals, <term>Himavat</term><note>The personification of the Himalaya mountains, considered a living deity and the king of mountains.</note>, is the greatest of all mountains. To him were born two daughters, whose beauty is unmatched on this earth. O Rama, their mother was the slender-waisted and lovely daughter of <term>Meru</term><note>A sacred mountain that represents the center of the universe in ancient cosmology.</note> named <term>Mena</term>. She was the beloved wife of Himavat, and she gave birth to Ganga, the elder daughter of Himavat. O <term>Raghava</term><note>A title for Rama, meaning "descendant of Raghu."</note>, a second daughter was also born to him, named <term>Uma</term><note>Also known as Parvati, she is a powerful goddess and the consort of Shiva.</note>.

"It so happened that once, the deities—seeking to accomplish a task required of them as divine beings—approached the king of mountains as a group and asked for Ganga, the river that flows in three directions. Desiring the welfare of the three worlds, Himavat, acting out of a sense of duty, gave them his daughter, who flows everywhere at her own will and purifies all living beings. To benefit the three worlds, the gods accepted her and departed with Ganga, feeling that their great desire had been fulfilled.

"The other daughter of the mountain, O descendant of Raghu, took a stern vow and began to perform intense <term>austerities</term><note>Self-disciplined spiritual practices (tapas) used to gain spiritual power or the favor of the gods.</note>, making her asceticism her only wealth. Then, that best of mountains gave his daughter Uma—who was filled with the power of her fiery spiritual practice and worshipped by all the worlds—to <term>Rudra</term><note>An early and fierce name for the god Shiva.</note>, who possesses an incomparable form. O Raghava, these are the two daughters of that king of mountains, worshipped by everyone: Ganga, the foremost of all rivers, and the divine Uma. Now, O best..."

### SECTION XXXVI.

---

When the ascetic had spoken thus, both the heroes, <term>Rama</term> and <term>Lakshmana</term>, saluting that foremost of hermits, said,—“O <term>Brahmana</term><note>A member of the priestly and scholarly class in ancient India, dedicated to spiritual and academic pursuits.</note>, you have delivered this noble narration filled with morality. Now it is right for you to speak about the elder daughter of the mountain-king [Himavat]. You are extensively knowledgeable about everything relating to men or gods. Why is it that this purifier of the worlds washes three directions? And why is that foremost of streams, the <term>Ganga</term><note>The river Ganges, considered a goddess and a purifying force in Hindu tradition.</note>, famous for traveling in three ways? And, O you who are aware of morality, what are her deeds in the three worlds?” 

At that, <term>Vishvamitra</term><note>A famous king-turned-sage who serves as a spiritual mentor to the princes.</note>, who holds asceticism as his only wealth, began to relate that history in detail to <term>Kakutstha</term><note>A title for Rama, meaning "descendant of Kakutstha," an ancient king in his lineage.</note> in the midst of the other sages. 

“In days of old, O Rama, the blue-throated one of mighty asceticism [<term>Shiva</term><note>One of the principal deities of Hinduism. He is called "blue-throated" because he drank a world-destroying poison to save the universe, which turned his neck blue.</note>], having entered into marriage, began his union with the goddess [Uma]. And as that intelligent, blue-throated god, <term>Mahadeva</term><note>Literally "The Great God," a common title for Shiva.</note>, was thus engaged in divine play, a hundred divine years passed; and yet, O Rama, subduer of enemies, no son was born to her. 

At this, all the gods, with the <term>Grand-sire</term><note>The god Brahma, the creator of the universe.</note> at their head, became exceedingly anxious. 'Who will be able to endure the offspring of this union?' And thereupon the gods, going to Mahadeva, addressed him, bowing low—‘O god of gods, O mighty deity, ever engaged in the welfare of all, it is right for you to be gracious toward the humble greetings of the gods. The worlds, O foremost of gods, are..."

For this, you shall not be able to father children on your own wives. From this day forward, your wives shall remain childless." 

Having thus spoken to the gods, she cursed the Earth also, saying, "O Earth, you shall have many forms, and many masters shall rule over you! Stained by my anger, you shall never experience the pleasure of having a son—O you of wicked mind, you who did not wish me to have a son!" Hearing this speech, she assumed a divine appearance. And beholding her mightiness, Agni was shrunk up on all sides. And then Pavaka from all sides discharged his energy into her,—and thereat all her streams became surcharged with it, O descendant of Raghu. And unto him staying at the head of all the deities, Ganga spoke, saying,—'O god, I am incapable of sustaining this new-sprung energy of thine: I am burning with that fire, and my consciousness fails me.' Thereupon that partaker of the oblations offered unto the gods <note>A poetic title for Agni, as sacrificial offerings are cast into the fire to reach the heavens.</note>, said unto Ganga,—'Do thou bring forth thy embryo on the side of this <term>Himavat</term><note>The personified deity of the Himalayan mountains and father of Ganga.</note>!' Hearing Agni's words, Ganga of mighty energy cast her exceedingly effulgent embryo on her streams, O sinless one. And as it came out of her, it wore the splendour of molten gold; and in consequence of its fiery virtue, objects near and objects far were converted into gold and silver of unsurpassed sheen,—while those that were more distant were turned into copper and iron. And her excreta were turned into lead. In this wise, various metals began to increase on earth. And as soon as the embryo was brought forth, the woods adjoining the mountain being overspread with that energy, were turned into gold. And from that day, O descendant of Raghu, gold of effulgence like unto that of fire, became

---

-> * Fire.—T. <-

And having drunk the milk, that lord, although then possessed of a tender frame, by virtue of his inborn prowess, in one day vanquished the <term>Danava</term><note>A race of demons or titans who opposed the gods.</note> forces. And because he was possessed of mighty effulgence, the celestials assembled with <term>Agni</term><note>The god of fire.</note> as their leader and sprinkled him with water, by way of installing him as their generalissimo. 

He who, O <term>Kakutstha</term>, reveres <term>Kartikeya</term> on this earth is blessed; he attains righteousness, and being long-lived and obtaining sons and grandsons, he eventually repairs to the heavenly regions of <term>Skanda</term>.

Who wishes for what boon,—between a single perpetuator of the line, and innumerable sons, possessed of mighty strength, and furnished with fame, and endowed with high spirits?’

# RAMAYANA.

<note>original: "RAMAYANA"; The Great Epic of the Deeds of Rama.</note>

Their enemies, the thirty-three <note>The "three and thirty celestials" refers to a specific pantheon of Vedic gods: 8 Vasus (elemental deities), 11 Rudras (storm deities), 12 Adityas (solar deities), and 2 Ashwins (divine physicians).</note> heavenly gods, being greatly rejoiced, returned to their respective homes. And as the sons of King Sagara were tearing open the Earth, a mighty noise arose, like the crashing of thunder. Then, having split open the entire Earth and searched everywhere, the sons of Sagara returned to their father and spoke to him, saying: "We have extensively surveyed the Earth; we have slain powerful <term>Danavas (ancient titans)</term>, <term>Rakshasas (demons)</term>, <term>Pishachas (flesh-eating ghouls)</term>, and <term>Uragas and Pannagas (celestial serpent-beings)</term>. Yet we have found neither the horse nor the one who stole it. What should we do now? May good fortune befall you—please consider this carefully."

Hearing these words from his sons, that greatest of kings became angry and said, "O descendant of Raghu <note>original: "Raghava"; a title for Prince Rama, to whom this story is being narrated by the sage Vishvamitra.</note>, go and dig into the earth yet again. Find the thief who stole the horse, and do not stop until you succeed." Receiving this command from their father, the sixty thousand sons of the high-souled Sagara rushed toward the depths of the earth.

As they were busy excavating, they saw the <term>Elephant of the Quarter</term> named Virupaksha, who was as large as a mountain and was holding up the earth. O son of Raghu, that mighty elephant, Virupaksha, supports the entire earth with its mountains and forests upon his head. O Kakutstha <note>Another title for Rama, referring to his ancestor Kakutstha, 'the one who sits on the hump of a bull.'</note>, on sacred days when the mighty elephant shakes his head from fatigue, an earthquake occurs. 

Thereupon, O Rama, circling that mighty elephant and honoring him properly, they continued to pierce through the under-earth. Having dug through the East, they dug through the South—and in the Southern quarter, they also saw a mighty elephant named Mahapadma. He was like a massive hill, holding the earth upon his head. They marveled greatly at the sight. After circling him, the sixty thousand sons of the high-souled Sagara began to penetrate the Western region. In the Western quarter, those highly powerful men saw the elephant of that direction named Saumanasa, who resembled a mighty mountain.

# BALAKANDAM

And having gone round him <note>The practice of *pradakshina*, or walking clockwise around a sacred being as a sign of respect.</note> and asked him as to his welfare, they continued digging on until they arrived at the Northern region. Then repairing to the famous North-eastern region, Sagara's sons, becoming enraged, began to dig the earth. And there also, experiencing exceeding delight, O descendant of Raghu, they found his horse, browsing hard by.

Stay! And thou it is that hast stolen our sacrificial horse, O thou of wicked understanding; know that thou hast fallen into the hands of the sons of Sagara.’ 

Hearing this speech of theirs, Kapila, O descendant of Raghu, overwhelmed with rage, uttered a tremendous roar. And then, O Kakutstha <note>Another ancestral title for Rama.</note>, the sons of Sagara were reduced to ashes by the high-souled and incomparable Kapila.

# BALAKANDAM.

The bird-king of mighty strength spoke unto him, saying—'Do not lament, O foremost of men. The destruction of these was for the welfare of all. These highly powerful ones had been consumed by the peerless **Kapila** <note>An ancient sage and an incarnation of Vishnu whose meditative power is so intense it can manifest as fire.</note>—therefore, thou ought not to offer water unto them in consonance with social usage <note>Standard funeral libations (tarpana) offered with ordinary water were insufficient for those killed by a sage’s divine fire.</note>. **Ganga**, O foremost of men, is the elder daughter of **Himavat** <note>The personification of the Himalayan Mountains.</note>. In her streams do thou perform the watery rites of thy uncles, O mighty-armed one: let that purifier of the worlds wash over these, who are now reduced to a heap of ashes. And on these ashes being watered by **Ganga**, dear unto all, the sixty thousand sons of **Sagara** will repair unto the celestial regions. Do thou, O highly pious one, go back, taking this horse, O foremost of men; and do thou complete the sacrifice of thy grandfather, O hero.' 

Hearing **Suparna's** <note>original: "Suparna," a name for the divine eagle Garuda, who was also the maternal uncle of Sagara's sons.</note> speech, the exceedingly powerful **Anshuman** <note>original: "Ançumat"</note> of mighty asceticism speedily taking the horse, retraced his steps. Then coming to the king who had been initiated into the ceremony <note>The "Diksha" or period of consecration where a king must remain within the sacrificial grounds.</note>, he, O descendant of **Raghu**, faithfully communicated unto him the words of **Suparna**. Hearing this sorrowful intelligence, the king duly finished the sacrifice agreeably to the scriptures. And having seen the completion of the sacrifice, that lord of earth entered his capital; but the king could not see how to bring **Ganga** on earth. And without being able to ascertain it, the mighty monarch after a long course of time, and having reigned for thirty thousand years, ascended heaven <note>A common phrasing for the death of a righteous king.</note>."

And, O **Raghu's** son, consigning unto **Dilipa** his kingdom, **Anshuman**...
