# RAMAYANA.

[**Anshuman**] entered upon rigid austerities on the romantic summit of the **Himavat** <note>The personification of the Himalayan mountain range.</note>. And having for the space of thirty-two hundreds of thousands years carried on austerities in the woods, that highly famous one, crowned with the wealth of asceticism, attained the celestial regions. 

And the exceedingly powerful **Dilipa**, hearing of the destruction of his grand-fathers <note>Referring to the 60,000 sons of Sagara who were burned to ashes by the sage Kapila.</note>, was stricken with grief; yet he could not ascertain his course about it. And he constantly thought as to how **Ganga** could be brought down, how to perform their <term>watery rites</term> <note>original: "tarpana"; libations of water offered to the deceased to ensure their peace in the afterlife.</note>, and how to deliver them. And as that pious one furnished with self-knowledge was always meditating upon this, an eminently virtuous son was born unto him named **Bhagiratha**. 

And performing numerous sacrifices, the mighty king **Dilipa** reigned for thirty thousand years. And without having arrived at any definite decision in regard to their deliverance, the king, O puissant one <note>The narrator, Sage Vishvamitra, is addressing Prince Rama.</note>, being attacked with an ailment, breathed his last. And having sprinkled his son **Bhagiratha** in the way of installing him in the kingdom, that prime of men, the king, by virtue of his own acts, repaired to the region of **Indra** <note>The king of the gods; "repairing to the region of Indra" is a poetic way of saying he went to heaven.</note>. 

And, O descendant of **Raghu** <note>Another address to Rama, identifying him by his famous ancestor, King Raghu.</note>, that royal saint **Bhagiratha** was possessed of righteousness. And being without issue <note>Having no children.</note>, and desiring to obtain it, the mighty monarch consigned his kingdom and his subjects to the care of his counsellors, and engaged in bringing down **Ganga**. 

And, O **Raghu’s** descendant, restraining his senses, and eating once a month, and surrounding himself with <term>five fires</term> <note>A severe penance known as *Panchatapas*, where an ascetic sits amidst four fires with the sun as the fifth fire above.</note>, and with arms upraised, he for a long lapse of time performed austerities at **Gokarna** <note>A sacred coastal site in modern-day Karnataka, revered as a place of intense spiritual power.</note>. And as he was performing his terrible austerities, a thousand years rolled away. 

And thereat that possessor of the <term>six attributes</term> <note>original: "Bhagavan"; the six divine opulences: infinite wealth, strength, fame, beauty, knowledge, and renunciation.</note> of all creatures, **Brahma**, was well-pleased with that high-souled monarch. "O thou of excellent vows, ask for the boon thou wouldst have." And to hold her, O king, find I none save the wielder of the Trident." <note>Shiva, the destroyer in the Hindu trinity, who carries a trident (trishula).</note> Having thus addressed the monarch, and greeted **Gangā**, the creator of the worlds repaired to heaven with the celestials.

# RAMAYANA.

With great force, she <note>The river goddess Ganga.</note> threw herself from the sky <note>original: "welkin"; an archaic term for the heavens.</note> upon **Shiva's** gracious head. And that divine one, **Ganga**, who is exceedingly difficult to restrain, thought—"I will sweep away **Shiva** <note>original: "Sankara"; a name for Shiva meaning "The Giver of Happiness."</note> by my currents and carry him down to the underworld." Knowing her proud intention, the adorable **Shiva** <note>original: "Hara"; a name for Shiva meaning "The Destroyer."</note> grew angry; and the three-eyed deity determined to ensnare her. 

And, O **Rama**, as that sacred river plunged upon **Shiva's** <note>original: "Rudra"; Shiva's fierce and ancient form.</note> holy head of matted locks <term>matted locks</term> <note>The "jata" or braided, unkempt hair of an ascetic, which in Shiva's case represents his immense spiritual power.</note>, which resembled the vast **Himalayas**, she could not reach the earth despite all her efforts; nor could she find a way out from under those tangled braids. She wandered trapped within them for many years. 

Finding **Ganga** in this plight, **Bhagiratha** again began to perform intense spiritual penance. Because of this, **Shiva**, O descendant of **Raghu**, was greatly pleased; he released **Ganga** in the direction of the **Bindu** lake. As she was let off, seven streams branched out from her. Three streams of the excellent and auspicious **Ganga** flowed toward the east; while the **Suchakshu**, the **Sita**, and that mighty river the **Sindhu** <note>The Indus River.</note> flowed toward the auspicious west. 

The seventh stream followed **Bhagiratha's** chariot. And that royal saint, the incredibly powerful **Bhagiratha**, mounted on a magnificent chariot, led the way; and **Ganga** followed behind him. She descended from the sky upon **Shiva's** head, and from there alighted upon the earth; her waters flowed with a sound like thunder. The earth looked beautiful, covered with swarms of fallen and falling fish, tortoises, and porpoises <note>likely referring to the Gangetic dolphin.</note>. 

Then the gods, saints, **Gandharvas** <term>Gandharvas</term> <note>Celestial musicians and singers who serve the gods.</note>, **Yakshas** <term>Yakshas</term> <note>Nature spirits and guardians of the earth's treasures.</note>, and **Siddhas** <term>Siddhas</term> <note>Perfected beings who have attained high spiritual levels and supernatural powers.</note>—mounted on excellent elephants, horses, and chariots as large as cities—watched as **Ganga** descended to the earth. The gods in their chariots were struck with wonder, and all living creatures marveled at the magnificent descent of **Ganga**. Eager to witness the spectacle, celestial hosts of immeasurable energy gathered there. Because of the arrival of the gods and the brilliance of their ornaments, the sky, which was free from clouds...

### BĀLAKĀNDAM.

Falling from the clouds, the river shone as if lit by a hundred suns. The sky was graced with fast-fleeting porpoises, serpents, and fish that looked like flashes of lightning. The heavens <note>original: "welkin"; an archaic term for the sky or the vault of heaven.</note>, scattered with thousands of pale flakes of foam, appeared like the autumn sky filled with swarms of cranes. 

The river proceeded in many ways: sometimes rapidly, sometimes winding <note>original: "awry."</note>, sometimes in great volumes, sometimes sloping down, and sometimes ascending upward. Sometimes the water clashed against itself, and sometimes, after climbing an upland, it descended into a valley. 

The clear and pure water first descended upon the head of **Shiva** <note>original: "Sankara"; a name for Shiva meaning the "Giver of Happiness."</note> and then onto the earth, appearing exceedingly beautiful. There, the saints and the **Gandharvas** <note>Celestial musicians and spirits.</note>, as well as the inhabitants of the earth, touched the sacred water flowing from the body of **Shiva** <note>original: "Bhaba"; a name for Shiva representing existence and the source of all.</note>. 

Those who had fallen from the sky to the earth due to a curse bathed there; their sins were washed away by that sanctifying water, allowing them to ascend to the sky once more and re-enter their own divine regions. Through the power of that shining water, all beings felt delight and rejoiced; by bathing in the **Ganga**, they became cleansed of sin.

Stationed on a magnificent chariot <note>original: "car."</note>, that mighty king and royal saint **Bhagiratha** went first, and the **Ganga** followed behind him. Following in the wake of the **Ganga** with joyful hearts were the gods, the saints, the **Daityas**, the **Danavas**, the **Rakshasas** <note>These are various classes of divine and demonic beings: the Daityas and Danavas are often called titans, while Rakshasas are powerful, often fierce spirits.</note>, the greatest of the **Gandharvas** and **Yakshas** <note>Nature spirits and guardians of wealth.</note>, the **Kinnaras** <note>Celestial beings often depicted as half-human and half-horse.</note>, the mighty <term>Uragas</term> <note>Powerful serpent-beings of the underworld.</note>, the serpents, the **Apsaras** <note>Celestial nymphs known for their beauty and dancing.</note>, and all the aquatic animals. 

Wherever King **Bhagiratha** went, the famous **Ganga**—the greatest of streams, capable of destroying all sins—followed. Eventually, the **Ganga** flooded the sacrificial grounds of the high-souled **Jahnu**, a man of wonderful deeds, while he was performing a sacred ritual. Seeing her boldness, **Jahnu** grew angry and, in a marvelous display of power, drank up all her waters. At this, the deities—

### SECTION XLIV.

"THEN having arrived at the Ocean, the king wending in **Ganga’s** wake, entered underneath the Earth, at that spot where those sons of **Sagara** <note>The sixty thousand ancestors of Bhagiratha who had been turned to ashes by the wrath of the sage Kapila.</note> had been reduced to ashes. And, O lord of earth, as long as the waters of the ocean shall endure in the world, **Sagara's** sons shall reside in heaven like unto celestials. And this **Ganga** shall become thy eldest daughter; and she shall attain celebrity among all, being called after thy name. And **Ganga** is called both *Tripathagā* <note>Meaning "the river of the three paths," as she flows through Heaven, Earth, and the Underworld.</note> and *Bhāgirathi*. And she is known as *Tripathagā*, in consequence of her proceeding in three directions. ### SECTION XLV.

Hearing the words of **Vishwamitra** <note>Spelled Viçwāmitra in the text; a royal sage and mentor to the young princes.</note>, **Raghava** <note>A title for Rama, meaning "descendant of King Raghu."</note>, together with **Lakshmana**, was struck with amazement and spoke unto **Vishwamitra**, saying:

"O **Brahmana** <term>Brahmin</term> <note>A member of the priestly and scholarly class.</note>, wonderful is the story that you have recited to us—namely, that of **Ganga's** <note>The Ganges river.</note> sacred descent and the replenishing of the Ocean. And, O afflicter of foes, as we had been reflecting upon all this at length, the night has passed away as if it were a moment. The entire night has passed as I, in the company of **Sumitra’s son** <note>Lakshmana, the son of Queen Sumitra.</note>, was pondering over **Vishwamitra's** auspicious speech."

Then, in the bright morning, that subduer of his foes, **Raghu's** descendant <note>Rama</note>, addressed the ascetic **Vishwamitra**, who had finished his morning devotions:

"The auspicious night is past, and we are ready to listen again to your wonderful narrations. Let us now cross over this sacred stream—the foremost of rivers—which flows in three ways <note>The Ganges is known as *Tripathaga*, meaning "flowing through three paths," referring to its presence in heaven, on earth, and in the underworld.</note>. Learning that you have arrived at this place, the pious ascetics have quickly come here and have also brought this boat equipped with a spacious carpet."

Hearing those words of the high-souled **Raghava**, **Kaushika** <note>A lineage name for Vishwamitra, denoting him as a descendant of King Kusha.</note> crossed over with the crowds of ascetics; and on reaching the north bank, he paid homage to the saints. When they had landed on the banks of the **Ganga**, they beheld a city named **Vishala** <note>An ancient city, historically linked to the site of Besarh in modern-day Bihar, India.</note>. Thereupon, that foremost of ascetics, in the company of **Raghava**, went quickly toward **Vishala**—a city as beautiful and elegant as the celestial regions of the gods. Then the highly wise **Rama**, with folded hands, asked the mighty ascetic **Vishwamitra** concerning the excellent city of **Vishala**:

"O mighty ascetic, what royal dynasty resides in yonder large city?"

# BALAKANDAM

"I desire to hear this; may prosperity be yours, for my curiosity is great." 

Hearing those words of **Rama**, that foremost of ascetics began to relate the history of **Vishala** <note>original: Viçālā; an ancient city located near the site where the Ganges and Gandaki rivers meet.</note>, saying—"Listen, O **Rama**, to the history I heard from **Sakra** <note>A title for Indra, the King of the Gods.</note>; and, O descendant of **Raghu**, listen to all that occurred in this city. 

Formerly in the **Krita age** <term>Satya Yuga</term> <note>The first and most perfect of the four world ages in Hindu cosmology, often called the Golden Age.</note>, O **Rama**, the highly powerful sons of **Diti** <note>The mother of the Daityas (demons/asuras).</note>, as well as those of **Aditi** <note>The mother of the Devas (gods/celestials).</note>, who were both powerful, virtuous, and high-souled, began to reflect: 'How can we become exempt from old age and disease, and become immortal?' 

As they reflected, it occurred to them: 'By churning the **Ocean of Milk** <note>Kshira Sagara; the cosmic ocean.</note>, we must obtain **ambrosia** <term>Amrita</term> <note>The nectar of immortality.</note>.' Then, deciding to churn the ocean, those beings of immeasurable energy used the serpent **Vasuki** <note>The King of Serpents.</note> as the churning rope and **Mount Mandara** as the churning staff, and began to churn the deep. 

After a thousand years had passed, the hoods of the serpent serving as the cord began to vomit virulent venom and bite at the crags with their fangs. From this, a powerful poison emerged that was like a blazing fire; consequently, the entire universe, including the gods, the **Asuras** <term>demons</term>, and men, began to burn. 

Seeking refuge, they appeared before that mighty god **Sankara** <note>A name for Shiva, meaning "Bringer of Happiness."</note>, also known as **Pashupati** <term>Lord of Creatures</term> or **Rudra** <term>The Howler</term>, praying to him: 'Save us! Save us!' 

While the Lord of the Gods was being addressed by the deities, **Hari** <note>A name for Vishnu, the Preserver.</note>, bearing the conch and the discus, appeared before them. Smiling, **Hari** said to the trident-bearing **Rudra**: 'O chief of the gods, since you are the foremost of the deities, this which has emerged from the ocean churned by the gods is yours. Remaining here, O Lord, receive this poison as the first offering.' 

Having said this, that best of gods vanished. Witnessing the dismay of the celestials and hearing the words of **Sarngin** <note>original: Çārngin; a name for Vishnu meaning "Wielder of the Bow."</note>, **Shiva** took in that dreadful poison as if it were nectar; then, leaving the deities, the worshipful **Hara** <note>Another name for Shiva.</note> departed. And then, O descendant of **Raghu**—"

# RAMAYANA.

As the gods <note>original: celestials</note> resumed the churning, that greatest of mountains which served as the churning pole sank into the lower regions. Thereupon the gods and the heavenly musicians <note>Gandharvas</note> began to praise the slayer of the demon Madhu <note>original: Madhu-sudana; a title for Vishnu referring to an ancient victory over a demon</note>, saying:—

> “You are the path for all beings, especially for the gods. O mighty-armed one, protect us and recover the mountain.”

Having heard this, **Hrishikesha** <note>original: Hṛṣīkeśa; "Lord of the Senses," a name for Vishnu</note>, also called **Hari** <note>A name for Vishnu meaning "The Remover of Sin/Sorrow"</note>, took the form of a tortoise and stood in the sea, supporting the mountain on his back. Then that Soul of All, **Keshava** <note>original: Keçava; a name for Vishnu</note>, the best of men, gripped the top of the mountain with his hand and began to churn the deep, standing in the midst of the gods. 

And after a thousand years had passed, there arose a man of exceedingly righteous soul named **Dhanwantari** <note>The physician of the gods</note>, who embodied the *Ayurveda* <gloss>Science of Medicine</gloss>, bearing in his hands a staff and a water-pot <note>Kamandalu</note>. And there arose also, from the foam of the churning waters, those magnificent women, the shining *Apsaras*. And, O foremost of men, as they emerged from the water, they are called *Apsaras* <gloss>Ap means water</gloss>. 

And there sprang forth sixty *Kotis* <note>A koti is ten million; thus, six hundred million</note> of shining *Apsaras*. And, O descendant of Kakutstha <note>Rama</note>, their female attendants are countless. Neither the gods nor the demons <note>Danavas</note> would accept them as wives; and because they were not accepted in marriage, they are known as women belonging to all. 

And then, O descendant of Raghu, arose the eminently pious daughter of the water-god **Varuna**, named **Varuni** <note>The goddess of wine</note>, who began looking for someone to accept her. The sons of **Diti** <note>The demons</note>, O Rama, did not accept the daughter of Varuna; but the sons of **Aditi** <note>The gods</note>, O hero, accepted that one of flawless beauty. And for this reason, the sons of Diti are known as **Asuras** <note>Literally "those who did not accept Sura/Varuni"</note>, and the sons of Aditi are known as **Suras** <note>Those who accepted Sura</note>. 

And the gods became exceedingly glad upon accepting Varuni. And, O foremost of men, next arose **Uchhaiçravā**—the best of horses— and also the jewel **Kaustubha**; and next, the excellent nectar of immortality <note>ambrosia or Amrita</note>. And, O Rama, tremendous was the slaughter for the possession of that nectar; and the sons of Aditi and Diti fought one another. And the demons <note>Asuras</note> assembled together with the—

### SECTION 46.

---

“And when her sons had been slain, **Diti**, afflicted with great grief, addressed her husband, **Kasyapa** <note>original: Kaçyapa</note>, the son of Marichi, saying:—‘O adorable one, your high-souled sons <note>The gods</note> have slain mine. I now wish for a son who, obtained through long spiritual trials <term>austerities: intense self-discipline and meditation known as Tapas</term>, will be able to slay **Indra** <note>original: Sakra</note>. I will engage in these trials; it is right for you to grant me such an embryo—it is right for you to promise me such a slayer of Indra.’ 

Hearing those words of hers, Marichi’s son, Kasyapa, who possessed exceeding energy, answered the deeply aggrieved Diti, saying:—‘So be it. Good fortune to you; you must become ritually pure, O ascetic. If you remain pure for a full thousand years, you will give birth to a son who will slay Indra in battle. And through me, you will give birth to a son that will destroy the three worlds.’ 

Having said this, that highly energetic sage rubbed her body with his palm. And having rubbed her, he said, ‘May you have luck!’ and then went away to carry out his own spiritual trials. And when he had gone, Diti, O foremost of men, became exceedingly delighted and went—

to Kuçaplava <note>An ancient holy retreat or asylum in the east, also known as Beshalaksha</note> and began to practice rigid spiritual trials <term>mortifications: intense self-discipline and physical penance known as Tapas</term>. And, O foremost of men, as she was practicing these austerities, the thousand-eyed deity <note>A title for **Indra**, the King of the Gods</note> most dutifully served her. And the thousand-eyed one provided for her fire, and *Kusa* <term>Kusa: a species of sacred grass used in Vedic rituals</term> and firewood, and water, and fruits, and roots, and other things that she required. And at all times, **Indra** <note>original: Sakra</note> served Diti by massaging her body and removing her fatigue. 

And when only ten years were left to complete the thousand years, Diti, O descendant of Raghu <note>Referring to the hero Rāma</note>, being exceedingly delighted, spoke thus to the thousand-eyed one:—“O best of those endowed with prowess, only ten years of my spiritual trials remain. And after that time, may good fortune attend you, for you will behold your brother. I will, O son, bind him to you in affection—he whom I had prayed for to bring about your destruction—so that, with the fever of your heart removed, you will enjoy victory over the three worlds along with him. After I petitioned your high-souled father, he, O foremost of the celestials, granted me the boon that after a thousand years, I should obtain a son.” 

And it came to pass that after she had said this, while the sun was at its highest point <note>Noon</note>, the worshipful Diti fell into a deep sleep, resting with her feet at the part of the bed where her head should have been. And seeing her in this position—with her feet where her head should be, and consequently in a state of ritual impurity—**Indra** <note>original: Sakra</note> was exceedingly delighted and smiled. And, O **Rāma**, **Indra** <note>original: Purandara; literally 'Destroyer of Cities'</note> entered into her womb, and that highly self-controlled one severed the embryo into seven parts. 

And the embryo, being pierced by the thunderbolt of a hundred knots <note>The Vajra, Indra's primary weapon</note>, cried out at the top of its voice, and at this Diti awoke. “Do not cry, do not cry!” exclaimed **Indra** <note>original: Sakra</note>; and even while it was crying, the mighty-minded **Indra** <note>original: Vāsava</note> continued piercing it. “Do not slay it; do not slay it,” said Diti. Thereupon, out of respect for his mother, **Indra** <note>original: Sakra</note> came out from her.

### SECTION 47

<image-desc>A small horizontal decorative flourish with a central loop.</image-desc>

When the embryo had been divided into seven, Diti, though deeply grieved, spoke humbly to the irrepressible thousand-eyed deity <note>A common epithet for **Indra**, king of the gods</note>, saying: "It is by my own fault that the embryo has been sundered in seven. O chief of the celestials, you are guilty of no transgression here, O destroyer of Vala <note>Indra earned this title after defeating the demon Vala who had stolen the cows of the gods</note>. And since this calamity has befallen the embryo, I wish to do you a kindness instead. Let these seven parts become the guardians of the seven **Storm Gods** <term>Maruts: a group of storm deities in Hindu mythology, often associated with the wind and Indra's host</term>. And, O son, let my sons take on noble forms and, becoming famous as the **Wind-Born** <note>original: Mārutas</note>, range the **Atmospheric Regions** <term>Vātaskandha: literally 'shoulders of the wind,' referring to the various layers of the atmosphere in ancient cosmology</term> in heaven. Let one travel through the regions of **Brahma** <note>The Creator</note>, another through the regions of **Indra**, and let the highly illustrious third also move about, being known as the **Divine Wind** <note>original: Divya Vāyu</note>. And, O best of the gods, by your command, let the four remaining sons of mine, known by the name you mentioned, travel about during their appointed periods."

Hearing her words, that destroyer of Vala, the thousand-eyed **Indra** <note>original: Purandara</note>, said with palms joined in respect: "All that you have said must come to pass; there is no doubt about it. May prosperity attend you; your sons, endowed with divine forms, shall travel through the heavens. And it has been told to us that having reached this agreement in that hermitage, the mother and the son, O **Rama**, went to heaven with their desires fulfilled. Even this, O **descendant of Kakutstha** <note>original: Kākutstha; a title for Rama referring to his royal lineage</note>, is the very place where—"

Surely none is more blessed than I am."

"...scimitars and bows and quivers, like unto the **Ashvinis** <note>The Ashvinis are twin Vedic gods associated with the dawn, known for their incredible beauty, youth, and role as physicians to the gods.</note> in grace, endowed with youth, like unto celestials <note>divine beings or gods</note> fancy-led descended from ethereal regions to the earth beneath—whose sons, O ascetic <note>A person who practices severe self-discipline and abstention, referring here to the sage Vishwamitra.</note>, are these boys? For what purpose have they come here, and why is it that they journey on foot? And adoring all directions, like the Sun or the Moon adorning the firmament <note>the heavens or the sky</note>, and resembling each other in personal proportions, expressions, and gestures, and equipped with excellent weapons, and war-like—how have these **paragons of men** <term>paragons<gloss>perfect examples of a particular quality; here, the height of human excellence</gloss></term> come into this impracticable way? I wish to hear all this related truly."

Having heard his words, Vishwamitra faithfully related all about it. Hearing Vishwamitra’s words, the king was extremely surprised; and having those sons of Dasharatha <note>The King of Ayodhya and father of Rama and Lakshmana.</note> as his all-worthy guests, he received those highly powerful ones deserving of hospitality with becoming respect. And meeting with such a splendid reception from Sumati, those descendants of Raghu <note>A famous ancestor of Rama; his descendants are often called the Raghus or Raghavas.</note> spent there a night, and the next day set out for **Mithila**. 

And beholding Janaka’s <note>The King of Mithila and father of Sita.</note> beauteous city, the ascetics exclaiming, "Excellent, excellent!" fell to admiring Mithila. And in a grove at Mithila, **Raghava** <note>A name for Rama, meaning "descendant of Raghu."</note> saw an ancient, lonely, and romantic asylum <term>asylum<gloss>In this historical context, a "hermitage" or "ashram"—a secluded place for spiritual retreat.</gloss></term>, and asked that foremost of ascetics, saying:

"What is this that looketh like an asylum, though without any ascetics? I wish to hear, O worshipful one, to whom this asylum belonged in time past."

Hearing this speech addressed by Raghu’s descendant, that one versed in speech, the highly energetic and mighty saint, Vishwamitra, answered:

"Ah! Do thou listen. I will tell thee through the wrath of what high-souled one this hermitage came to be cursed. O foremost of men, this excellent asylum honored by the celestials themselves, formerly belonged to the high-souled Gautama <note>A famous and powerful sage in Hindu tradition.</note>. And here, O illustrious prince, in days of yore, Gautama in company with **Ahalya** carried on austerities <note>Strict spiritual practices or penance intended to gain favor from the gods.</note> for a long series of years. And perceiving an occasion, **Sachi’s lord** <note>A title for Indra, the King of the Gods; Sachi is his wife.</note>, the thousand-eyed deity, assuming the form—"

The King of the Gods, disguised as the sage, addressed **Ahalya** <note>Ahalya was the wife of the sage Gautama and was renowned as the most beautiful woman in the world, created by the god Brahma.</note> saying, "O exceedingly beautiful one, those who are intent on pleasure do not wait for the proper ritual season. And, O graceful one, I desire to enjoy your company this very moment."

Thereupon, out of curiosity, she whose understanding had been led astray consented to the proposals of the chief of the **celestials** <note>divine beings or gods; here referring to Indra.</note>. Then, having attained her object, she spoke to that foremost of the gods, saying, "O best of the immortals, I have obtained my desire—now, go quickly from this place, O lord. O Lord of the Heavens, for the sake of our reputation, protect both yourself and me."

**Indra** <term>Indra<gloss>The King of the Gods and ruler of the heavens, often associated with thunder, rain, and earthly desires.</gloss></term> smiling, said to Ahalya, "O you of shapely hips, I am pleased. Now I return to my own place." Having known her thus, Indra—O **Rama**—feeling exceedingly fearful of the sage **Gautama**, then hurriedly hurried out of the thatched cottage. 

Just at this time, Indra saw the mighty ascetic Gautama entering—that foremost of hermits, who could not be held back by either the gods or the **Danavas** <term>Danavas<gloss>A race of powerful, often antagonistic beings or titans in Vedic mythology who frequently challenged the gods.</gloss></term>. He was filled with the power of his spiritual practice, having just bathed in the waters of holy spots; he was flaming like a fire, carrying firewood and **kusa grass** <term>kusa grass<gloss>A sacred grass used in ancient Indian rituals, particularly for purification and as a seating material for meditation.</gloss></term>. 

And seeing him, the face of the Lord of the Heavens turned pale. Seeing the wicked, **thousand-eyed deity** <note>A title for Indra; in some versions of the myth, his body was covered in a thousand eyes as a mark of his shame.</note> in the guise of an ascetic, the well-behaved hermit, burning with rage, said: 

"And since, O you of wicked mind, you have assumed my form and committed this foul deed, you shall lose your virility." <note>The original text uses "scrotum," representing the loss of Indra's physical and divine potency as a punishment for his lust.</note>

And as soon as the high-souled Gautama had spoken this in his anger, the manhood of the thousand-eyed one fell to the earth. Having seen **Sakra** <note>original: "Sakra"—another name for Indra, meaning "the Mighty One."</note> in this state, the sage cursed his wife also: 

"For a thousand years you shall live here feeding only upon air, without food, tormented by repentance, and you shall remain in this hermitage unseen by any living thing. And when Rama, the unstoppable son of **Dasharatha** <note>The King of Ayodhya and father of Rama.</note>, shall come to this deep forest, you shall be cleansed of your sin. And, O wicked one, by offering him the traditional rites of hospitality with a mind free from ignorance and—"

### SECTION 49

---

"And having been deprived of his testicles <note>original: "scrotum"</note>, **Indra** <note>original: "Sakra," a common title for Indra meaning 'The Mighty One.'</note>, with eyes trembling with fear, addressed the celestial beings with **Agni** <note>The God of Fire.</note> at their head, as well as the **Siddhas**, the **Gandharvas** <note>Celestial musicians.</note>, and the **Charanas**, saying: 'I have accomplished the work of the gods by provoking the anger of the high-souled **Gautama**, thereby disturbing his spiritual practices. In doing so, I have been castrated, and **Ahalya** has also been struck down. However, I have successfully drained him of his ascetic energy by causing him to utter such a powerful curse. Therefore, O gods, saints, and celestial singers, you should restore my manhood to me, for I have served the interests of the heavens.'

Hearing the words of the **Lord of a Hundred Sacrifices** <term>Satakratu<gloss>An epithet for Indra, referring to the one who has successfully performed one hundred horse sacrifices (Ashvamedha) to attain the throne of heaven.</gloss></term>, the deities, along with the **Maruts** <note>Storm deities and companions of Indra.</note> led by the Fire God, presented themselves before the divine **Ancestors** <term>Pitris<gloss>The spirits of the departed ancestors who dwell in their own realm and receive offerings from their living descendants.</gloss></term>. Then **Agni** addressed the Ancestors, saying: 'This ram here possesses testicles, while **Indra** has been deprived of his. Take the testicles of this ram and provide them to Indra. Although the ram will be deprived of them, it will still be able to grant you complete satisfaction. Furthermore, to those who offer such a ram for your nourishment, you shall bestow eternal and abundant merit.'

Hearing the speech of the Fire God, the assembled Ancestors uprooted the testicles of the ram and joined them to the person of the **Thousand-Eyed Deity** <note>Another title for Indra, symbolizing his all-seeing watchfulness.</note>. From that time forward...

<header>RAMAYANA.</header>

"O **Kākutstha** <term>Kakutstha<gloss>A common epithet for Rama, meaning 'descendant of Kakutstha,' an ancient king of the Solar Dynasty.</gloss></term>, the divine **Pitris** <note>The deified spirits of ancestors to whom offerings are made.</note> together feast upon rams that have had their testicles removed <note>original: "scrotum-less"</note>, for those parts had been joined to the person of **Indra**. And thenceforth, O **Rāghava** <note>Another name for Rama, meaning 'descendant of Raghu.'</note>, **Indra** also, through the high-souled **Gautama’s** ascetic energy, has been bearing the testicles of a ram. 

Therefore, O highly powerful one, enter the hermitage of that pious sage, and deliver the dignified and divinely fair **Ahalyā**." 

On hearing **Viçwāmitra’s** words, **Rāghava**, in the company of **Lakshmana**, placing **Viçwāmitra** at their front, entered the asylum; and they beheld that magnificent dame flaming with the energy of her long penance. She was a sight that could not be gazed at too closely even by the celestials or the **Asuras** <note>Demons or titans who are the traditional enemies of the gods.</note>. It was as if she were created by the Deity to be the perfectly charming Woman; she appeared like a flame hidden in smoke, or like the brightness of the full moon clouded and dimmed by mist, or like the solar splendor that cannot be beheld because of the surrounding clouds. 

By virtue of **Gautama’s** curse, she had remained invisible to any in the three worlds, until the sight of **Rāma**. And now, the curse having come to an end, she could finally be perceived by them. The two descendants of **Raghu** then took hold of her feet in a gesture of respect; but remembering **Gautama’s** original instructions, she, on her part, took hold of theirs. 

With a collected mind, she offered them water for their feet as well as **arghya** <term>arghya<gloss>A traditional libation or respectful offering made to an honored guest.</gloss></term>, and extended to them the full rites of hospitality. The **Kākutsthas** accepted the homage thus rendered according to the sacred ordinances. Blossoms began to shower down copiously to the sounds of celestial kettledrums, and the **Gandharbas** <note>Celestial musicians.</note> and the **Apsaras** <note>Celestial nymphs or dancers.</note> began to rejoice greatly. 

Exclaiming, "Excellent, excellent," the celestials honored **Ahalyā**, as—with her person purified by her long penance—she again came under **Gautama’s** governance <note>Meaning she was restored to her status as the sage's wife.</note>. The highly energetic **Gautama**, also happy at his reunion with **Ahalyā**, honored **Rāma** highly, and that master of mighty mortifications then returned to his austerities. Having duly received these high honors from the great ascetic **Gautama**, **Rāma** then set out for **Mithilā** <note>The capital city of King Janaka, where Rama will eventually meet Sita.</note>.

<summary>Rama and Lakshmana enter Gautama's hermitage, where Rama's presence breaks the curse on Ahalya, making her visible and pure again. The gods celebrate her restoration, she reunites with her husband Gautama, and the group continues their journey to Mithila.</summary>

Then, traveling toward the north-east, **Rāma**, accompanied by **Lakshmaṇa** <term>Sumitrā’s son<gloss>A common epithet for Lakshmana, Rama's loyal younger brother.</gloss></term> and led by **Viçwāmitra**, arrived at the site of the great ritual sacrifice. **Rāma** and **Lakshmaṇa** said to that powerful sage, "The grandeur and beauty of the noble **Janaka’s** sacrifice are truly magnificent. Many thousands of **Brāhmaṇas** <term>Brāhmaṇa<gloss>A member of the priestly and scholarly class in ancient India, responsible for maintaining sacred knowledge.</gloss></term> from various regions, all masters of the **Vedas** <note>The most ancient and sacred scriptures of Hinduism.</note>, have gathered here. The dwellings of the ascetics are crowded with hundreds of chariots. Please, O sage, find a place where we may stay." 

Upon hearing **Rāma’s** request, the great sage **Viçwāmitra** chose a quiet, well-watered spot away from the noise and crowds. When he heard of **Viçwāmitra’s** arrival, the faultless King **Janaka**, accompanied by the priest **Satānanda** and other high-ranking family priests, went out to meet them. Carrying the *honorific offering* <term>arghya<gloss>A traditional ritual offering of water, flowers, or grain given to a guest as a sign of high respect.</gloss></term>, the King approached in a humble manner and presented it to **Viçwāmitra** according to the sacred traditions. 

After accepting the King’s hospitality, the sage asked about the King’s well-being and the progress of his long sacrifice. The King and his priests likewise greeted the other sages, embracing them warmly and with proper etiquette. Then, with his hands joined in respect, the King spoke to the great hermit, saying, "O venerable one, please take your seat along with these other distinguished sages." 

At **Janaka’s** invitation, the mighty **Viçwāmitra** sat down. The King, his priests, and his advisors then took their seats according to their rank. Looking toward **Viçwāmitra**, the King said, "Today, by the favor of the gods, my sacrifice has reached its fulfillment. By seeing your holy self, I have truly reaped the rewards of this ritual. I am blessed and honored that you have graced this sacrificial ground with your presence, O **Brāhmaṇa** along with

"Twelve days, O <term>Brahmarshi<gloss>The highest class of sage, one who has attained divine knowledge while living a priestly life.</gloss></term>, have been fixed for the period of initiation by the sages. Having related all this unto the high-souled **Janaka** that one endowed with exceeding energy, the mighty ascetic **Viçwāmitra**, paused.

This righteous man [Vishwamitra] was a king for a long time—subduing his enemies, knowledgeable in morality, accomplished, and dedicated to the welfare of his subjects. It came to pass that once, with his four-fold forces <note>A traditional Indian army consisting of four divisions: chariots, elephants, cavalry, and infantry.</note> assembled, he set out to travel across the world. The king went on, visiting cities and kingdoms, rivers, mountains, and hermitages in turn. At length, that greatest of conquerors, the mighty **Vishwamitra**, came upon **Vasishtha’s** hermitage. It was filled with various blossoming plants and trees and was home to many animals. It was inhabited by **Siddhas** <term>Siddhas<gloss>Perfected beings who have attained supernatural powers.</gloss></term> and **Charanas** <term>Charanas<gloss>Celestial bards or singers.</gloss></term>, and graced by heavenly beings, **Danavas** <term>Danavas<gloss>A race of powerful titans.</gloss></term>, **Gandharvas** <term>Gandharvas<gloss>Celestial musicians.</gloss></term>, and **Kinnaras** <term>Kinnaras<gloss>Mythological beings often depicted as half-human and half-horse.</gloss></term>. It was filled with gentle deer and frequented by many birds. It was crowded with **Brahmarshis** <term>Brahmarshis<gloss>The highest class of sages who have realized the ultimate truth.</gloss></term> and inhabited by **Devarshis** <term>Devarshis<gloss>Sages who have attained the status of gods.</gloss></term>. Indeed, it was teeming with high-souled individuals of great spiritual success who glowed like fire. It was like another realm of the creator **Brahma**—graceful and adorned on all sides by noble saints, including the **Valakhilyas** <term>Valakhilyas<gloss>A group of divine, thumb-sized sages.</gloss></term> and **Vaikhananasas** <term>Vaikhananasas<gloss>A specific order of forest-dwelling hermits.</gloss></term> who resembled **Brahma**. Some lived only on water or air, while others lived on withered leaves or subsisted on fruits and roots. They were self-controlled, free from faults, masters of their senses, and engaged in reciting *mantras* <term>mantras<gloss>Sacred chants or prayers.</gloss></term> and performing *homas* <term>homas<gloss>Ritual offerings made into a consecrated fire.</gloss></term>."

Once the wise **Vishwamitra** <note>original: Viçwāmitra</note> had taken his seat, that greatest of ascetics properly welcomed him with a meal of fruits and roots. Having accepted **Vashistha’s** <note>original: Vasishtha</note> hospitality, that supreme monarch, the exceptionally energetic **Vishwamitra**, then inquired about the well-being of the sage’s spiritual practice, his **sacred fire rituals** <term>Agnihotras<gloss>Daily devotional fire sacrifices performed by Vedic practitioners to maintain cosmic order.</gloss></term>, his disciples, and even his trees.

In response, **Vashistha** confirmed that all was well to that best of kings. Then **Vashistha**, the son of the creator god **Brahma**, a man of intense spiritual discipline and the greatest of those who recite **sacred hymns** <term>mantras<gloss>Vedic chants or prayers believed to have spiritual and psychological power.</gloss></term>, asked the comfortably seated **Vishwamitra**:

"O King, is all well with you? Do you rule your subjects and keep them happy in accordance with your royal duties? O virtuous one, are your servants and officers properly supported by the kingdom’s wealth? Do they follow your commands? O destroyer of foes, have you conquered your enemies? O repressor of enemies, is it well with your forces, your treasury, your friends, and—O sinless one—your sons and grandsons?"

The highly powerful King **Vishwamitra** humbly informed **Vashistha** that everything in his life was indeed well. Having conversed for a long time, these two virtuous men found great joy in each other's company. 

Then, O descendant of **Raghu** <note>A reference to Prince Rama, to whom this story is being told; Raghu was a famous ancestor of Rama’s dynasty.</note>, after their conversation ended, the venerable **Vashistha** addressed **Vishwamitra** with a smile:

"O highly powerful one, I wish to properly host you and your immense power, as well as your entire army. Therefore, please accept my hospitality. Receive the welcome I wish to give you. O King, you are the most distinguished of guests and are worthy of the highest honor."

Being addressed this way by the great ascetic **Vashistha**, King **Vishwamitra** replied:

"By these kind words alone, you have already welcomed me. O worshipful one, even with the simple fruits and roots of your hermitage, the water for washing my feet and for sipping—and indeed, by the very sight of your revered self—I have already been excellently hosted, O profoundly wise one."

...apartment, was also heartily filled. And being hospitably entertained with his courtiers and counsellors and retainers, he, exceedingly delighted, spoke unto **Vashistha** <note>original: Vasishtha</note>, saying,—

Thus addressed by **Vishwamitra** <note>original: Viçwāmitra</note>, the righteous and adorable **Vashistha**—the best of ascetics—replied to that lord of the earth,—

"O king, neither for a hundred thousand nor for a hundred **crore** <term>koti<gloss>A unit in the Indian numbering system equal to ten million.</gloss></term> of cows, nor even for heaps of silver, will I part with **Shabala**. O subduer of enemies, she does not deserve to be separated from my side. Just like the fame of a great man, this **Shabala** is forever joined with me. My offerings to the gods and the **ancestors** <term>Pitris<gloss>The spirits of departed ancestors who are honored with ritual offerings.</gloss></term>, as well as my very survival, are established in her alone. My **perpetual fire-rituals** <term>Agnihotras<gloss>The daily maintenance of a sacred fire, a fundamental duty for a Vedic priest.</gloss></term>, **spirit offerings** <term>Bali<gloss>Offerings of food made to various spirits or creatures.</gloss></term>, and **burnt offerings** <term>Homa<gloss>A ritual in which offerings are poured into a consecrated fire.</gloss></term> depend upon her. All this is so; there is no doubt about it. Truly she is my everything, and in her I find my joy. There are many reasons, O king, why I cannot give **Shabala** to you."

Thus addressed by **Vashistha**, that master of speech, **Vishwamitra**, eagerly replied—

"I shall give you fourteen thousand elephants decorated with gold chains, gold neck-ornaments, and training hooks; and I will give you eight hundred golden chariots equipped with bells, each pulled by four white horses;"

### SECTION LIV

---

When the hermit **Vashistha** <note>original: Vasishtha</note> would not part with the **cow of plenty** <gloss>Another name for Shabala, the divine cow who produces all that is needed for rituals and hospitality.</gloss>, then **Vishwamitra**, O **Rama**, forcibly drove **Shabala** away. And, O **Rama**, as she was carried off by that noble-minded king, **Shabala**, overcome with grief and stricken with sorrow, thought to herself while weeping—

"Have I been abandoned by the noble-minded **Vashistha** that the King’s servants carry me away in such distress? What wrong have I done to that mighty hermit of focused spirit, that he, knowing me to be blameless, permits the one who was devoted to him to be taken away?"

<summary>King Vishwamitra offers immense wealth for the divine cow Shabala, but Vashistha refuses, explaining that she is the essential source of his religious life. Consequently, the King resorts to force, leading to the cow's abduction and her deep distress at being seemingly abandoned by her master.</summary>

Revolving this in her mind and sighing again and again, she darted unto where the pre-eminently energetic **Vasishtha** was; and defeating those servants (of the king), she with the speed of the wind, appeared at the feet of that high-souled one. And weeping **Savalā** <note>original: Savalā; commonly known as Shabala, the divine wish-fulfilling cow.</note> having the voice of clouds, standing before **Vasishtha**, spoke in distressful accents,—'O **Brahma's son**, wherefore have I been forsaken by thee,—that the servants of the king were carrying me from off thy presence?' 

Thus addressed the **Brahmarshi** <gloss>A "Brahmin-sage," the highest class of sage who has attained the status of a Brahmin.</gloss> said these words unto that one aggrieved, and of heart afflicted with sorrow, and like unto a sister,—'O **Savalā**, not that I forsake thee; nor hast thou done me any wrong. Thy power is immeasurable; and the exceedingly energetic **Viçwāmitra** <note>original: Viçwāmitra; typically written as Vishwamitra.</note> is not as powerful as thyself. Thy energy is unequalled. O highly energetic one, do thou command me bursting with **Brāhma** <note>Referring to the spiritual power or "soul-force" inherent to a Brahmin, which is said to overcome physical weapons.</note> forces: the pride, power and endeavours of that wicked one will I bring down.'

“AND beholding them sore harassed, and overwhelmed by **Vishwamitra’s** weapons, **Vasishtha** directed (**Shabala**) saying,—‘O cow of plenty, do thou create (fresh troops), through thy **spiritual power** <term>Yoga power<gloss>Refers to *Siddhis*, the supernatural abilities or perfections attained through intense meditation and asceticism.</gloss></term>.’ And from her roar, there came into being **Kambojas**, resembling the Sun. [Vishwa]mitra, overwhelmed by shame, was plunged into deep thought. Like a tideless ocean or a snake whose fangs have been removed, his brilliance suddenly vanished—resembling the sun when it is eclipsed by the **shadow-demon** <note>original: Rāhu; the celestial entity in Hindu mythology responsible for eclipses.</note>.* Deprived of his army and his sons, he appeared like a bird stripped of its wings. Having lost his entire host and the high spirits they had once inspired in him, his heart died within him. Then, entrusting his one remaining son with the kingdom, saying—"Rule the earth according to the duties of the **warrior class** <term>Kshatriya<gloss>The social class of kings, warriors, and administrators.</gloss></term>," he departed for the forest.

Traveling to the side of the **Himalayas** <term>Himavat<gloss>The personification of the Himalayan mountain range.</gloss></term> inhabited by **celestial musicians** <note>original: Kinnaras</note> and **serpents** <note>original: Nagas</note>, that king of mighty asceticism began to perform penances to appease **the Great God** <term>Mahādeva<gloss>A primary title for Shiva, meaning 'Great God'.</gloss></term>. Eventually, that lord of the gods, **the Bull-Bannered One** <note>original: Vrishadwaja; an epithet of Shiva.</note>,† intending to grant a wish, appeared before the powerful ascetic **Vishwamitra**—"For what reason, O king, are you performing these penances? Speak what you have to say. I am here to grant a boon; name what you wish to obtain."

Addressed by the god, that performer of mighty austerities, **Vishwamitra**, bowed down to him and said—"If, O mighty god, you are pleased with me, grant me the science of archery with all its mysteries and **sacred chants** <note>original: mantras</note>, as well as its powers to strike from both far and near. And, O sinless one, may all those weapons belonging to the **gods** <note>original: celestials</note>, the **titans** <note>original: Dānavas</note>, the **great sages** <note>original: Maharshis</note>, the **celestial spirits** <note>original: Gandharbas</note>, the **nature spirits** <note>original: Yakshas</note>, and the **demons** <note>original: Rakshas</note>, manifest within me! May this desire be granted through your grace, O god of gods!"

Thereupon, saying—"So be it"—the lord of the gods vanished. Having obtained these weapons from the lord of the gods, the naturally proud **Vishwamitra** became filled with—

Today I will destroy the son of Gādhi <note>Vishwamitra.</note>, just as the sun dissolves the mist.” Having said this, that master of **sacred chants** <term>mantra<gloss>Vedic hymns or sounds believed to have spiritual and physical power when recited correctly.</gloss></term>, Vasishtha, in a great rage, addressed Vishwamitra, saying: “Since you, O fool, have destroyed this hermitage which has flourished for so long, you, who follow such wicked ways, shall not live much longer.” Saying this, he was overcome with wrath, appearing like a smoking flame at the **end of the world** <note>original: universal dissolution or Pralaya; the periodic destruction of the universe in Hindu cosmology.</note>, and quickly raised a staff that looked like the very club of **Death** <term>Yama<gloss>The god of death and justice who maintains the balance of the universe.</gloss></term>.

### SECTION LVI.

<image-desc>decorative horizontal divider line with a central diamond-shaped ornament</image-desc>

“Thus addressed by Vasishtha, the mighty Vishwamitra, aiming a fiery weapon, cried out—‘Stay! Stay!’ The venerable Vasishtha, for his part, raised a **Staff of Brahma** <term>Brāhma staff<gloss>The Brahma-danda; a wooden staff belonging to a Brahmin that contains the spiritual power of the creator god, capable of neutralizing any physical or divine weapon.</gloss></term> which resembled the staff of **Time and Death** <term>Kāla<gloss>A personification of Time as the ultimate destroyer of all things.</gloss></term> itself, and shouted in anger: ‘You miserable **warrior** <term>Kshatriya<gloss>The social class of kings and soldiers, whose power is based on physical force and weapons.</gloss></term>! Here I am—show me what strength you possess. Today, O son of Gādhi, I will humble your pride in your weapons. You disgrace to your lineage, what is your warrior might compared to my high **priestly power** <note>original: Brāhma energy; the spiritual power attained through meditation and holy living.</note>? Behold my supernatural spiritual energy!’ 

And just as water puts out the fury of a flame, the **Staff of Brahma** quenched the power of the divine weapons.”

<summary>King Vishwamitra attacks Vasishtha's hermitage with divine weapons granted by Shiva, but the sage Vasishtha easily neutralizes the assault using his spiritual staff. This scene highlights the classic Indian epic theme of the superiority of spiritual power (Brahma-tejas) over mere physical or military might (Kshatra-tejas).</summary>

When these weapons were resisted, the son of Gādhi hurled the ultimate **Brahma-weapon** <note>The Brahmastra, the most destructive force in Hindu mythology.</note>. Once the high-souled Vasishtha had defeated the Brahma-weapon, his form became fierce and terrible, capable of striking terror into all three worlds. From the pores of his body, which looked like a smoking flame, sparks of fire shot out. Resembling another staff of the god of death <note>original: Yama.</note>, the **Brahma-staff** raised by Vasishtha's arm flamed like the smokeless fire that consumes the universe at the **universal dissolution** <note>The periodic destruction of the world in Hindu cosmology.</note>. 

Then the company of ascetics fell to praising that best of **sacred-chant** reciters, Vasishtha, saying: "Your power, O **Priest** <term>Brāhmana<gloss>A member of the highest priestly and scholarly class.</gloss></term>, is infallible. Please, restrain..."

### SECTION LVII.

---

THEN with his heart burning from the memory of his humiliation, and having made an enemy of that high-souled sage, Vishwamitra—now a man of great asceticism—sighing repeatedly, went towards the south in the company of his queen. There he became engaged in dreadful austerities, O Rama <note>original: "Rāghava"; a title for the hero Rama, descendant of Raghu</note>. 

And living only on fruits and roots and restraining his senses, he performed the most rigid penances. And four sons devoted to truth and duty—Havishpanda, Madhushpanda, Dridhanetra, and Maharatha <note>Some versions of the text name the fourth son "Mahodara."</note>—were born to him. 

And when a thousand years had been completed, the Creator and Grandfather of all, Brahma, addressed the ascetic Vishwamitra in sweet words, saying: "O descendant of Kushika <note>original: "son of Kuçika"</note>, the rank of a Royal Sage <note>original: "Rājarshi"</note> has been won by you through your austerities. Because of your penance, we recognize you as a Royal Sage." Having said this, the highly energetic Supreme Lord of all creatures went to the celestial regions in the company of the gods.

<summary>Vishwamitra realizes that spiritual power is superior to military might and begins a thousand-year penance, eventually earning the title of Royal Sage (Rajarshi) from the god Brahma.</summary>

# BALAKANDAM
### (The Book of Childhood)

<page-num>133</page-num>

Hearing this, Vishwamitra <note>The king who is attempting to become a high-ranking sage</note> lowered his head in shame. Overwhelmed by deep sorrow, he spoke in mournful tones:
> "I have practiced intense self-discipline <term>austerities; rigorous spiritual practices like fasting and meditation</term>—yet the gods and the saints recognize me only as a Royal Sage <term>Rājarshi; a sage who was formerly a king, considered a lower rank than a Priestly Sage or Brahmarshi</term>. I do not feel I have truly earned the rewards of my spiritual efforts."

Having decided this in his heart, that righteous and composed man of great spiritual power, O Rama <note>original: "Kākuststha"; a title for the hero Rama as a descendant of King Kakutstha</note>, again devoted himself to penances. And, O descendant of Raghu <note>original: "Rāghava"</note>, it happened at this time that the famous and truthful King Trishanku—an ancestor of your own line who had mastered his senses—made a bold resolution, saying:
> "I will perform a sacred ritual <term>sacrifice</term>, and while still in my physical body, I shall enter the highest realm of the gods."

He summoned the sage Vasishtha and revealed his desire. But when the great-souled Vasishtha said:
> "I am unable to perform such a feat,"

and was thus dismissed by the sage, the King traveled toward the southern regions. To ensure the success of his goal, the King went to the place where Vasishtha’s sons had been practicing their spiritual disciplines for a long time. The powerful Trishanku saw the hundred radiant sons of Vasishtha, all focused deeply on their meditations. Approaching these holy sons of his own spiritual guide and showing them great respect, he bowed his head in shame and, with hands joined in prayer, addressed those mighty spirits:
> "I seek your protection; I take refuge in you, for you are capable of granting it. I have been turned away—may you be blessed—by the great-souled Vasishtha. I have set my heart upon performing a great ritual; I ask for your command. To please you, I bow my head low and beg the sons of my teacher—priests <term>Brāhmanas</term> who live by spiritual practice—to lead this ritual with focused minds. Help me succeed so that I may reach the realms of the gods in this physical body. Having been rejected by the sage Vasishtha, I find no other way, O holy men, except through the sons of my teacher. For those of the Ikshvaku dynasty, their teacher is their ultimate path."

<summary>King Trishanku seeks to enter heaven in his physical body, a feat deemed impossible by the sage Vasishtha. After being rejected by the father, the King turns to Vasishtha's hundred sons to lead the ritual.</summary>

"Therefore, after my priest Vasishtha, even you are the objects of my worship."

### SECTION LVIII.

---

“Hearing Trishanku's speech, the hundred sons of the saint, O Rama, were filled with anger and said these words to the king:
'You have already been rejected, O man of foolish understanding, by our truth-telling father. Why then, having been dismissed by him, do you seek help from others? For the kings of the Ikshwaku line <term>Ikshwaku; the royal dynasty of King Rama</term>, their spiritual guide is their ultimate authority; you are not capable of casting aside the words of that truthful sage. That worshipful saint already said that he was unable to accomplish this—how then can we undertake such a sacrifice <term>Yajna; a sacred ritual involving offerings to the gods</term>? You are being ignorant, O leader of men. You must quickly retrace your steps. Furthermore, O king, that adorable sage is competent to lead the rituals for the three worlds <note>Heaven, Earth, and the Underworld</note>—how could we possibly contribute to his dishonor by overstepping him?'

Hearing those words of theirs, the king, with his voice trembling with passion, addressed them again, saying:
'Since I have been rejected by that worshipful one as well as by the sons of my spiritual guide, I will seek out another path—so may peace be with you, ascetics.'

The saint's sons, hearing this speech which suggested a defiant intent, cursed him in an exceeding rage, saying:
'You shall become a Chandala <term>outcast; historically a person of the lowest social status, often associated with death and cremation grounds</term>.'

Having said this, those high-souled ones each went back into his own dwelling. And when the night had passed, the king was transformed into a Chandala. He was dressed in blue clothes; his skin became dark and rough; he had a short, coarse head of hair; he wore a garland made of flowers gathered from a cemetery; his body was smeared with ashes from the cremation grounds; and he was weighed down by iron ornaments. 

And, O Rama, seeing him in the form of an outcast, his advisors and his followers abandoned him and fled."

<meta>The text marks the transition of Trishanku from a glorious king to a social pariah as a result of a curse. This sets the stage for his eventual meeting with Vishwamitra.</meta>

<summary>King Trishanku is cursed by the sons of Vasishtha for his pride and defiance, transforming overnight into a social outcast (Chandala). Abandoned by his court, he is left in a wretched physical state, having failed to find a priest to help him reach heaven alive.</summary>

# BALAKANDAM
### [The Book of Youth]

...in his physical body. And, O Rama <term>Kakutstha; a title for Rama, meaning "descendant of Kakutstha"</term>, maintaining his patience despite his suffering, the king—who felt as if he were burning day and night with grief—went all alone to the ascetic Vishwamitra. 

When Vishwamitra saw the disappointed king in the form of an outcast <term>Chandala; historically a person of the lowest social status, often associated with death and cremation grounds</term>, the sage was moved to pity. Out of compassion, that deeply pious and powerful man spoke to the king, whose appearance was now frightening to behold, saying:

"May you be blessed, O heroic lord of Ayodhya <term>Ayodhya; the capital city of the Kosala kingdom and Rama's home</term>. You have fallen into the state of an outcast because of a curse. What is the reason for your visit, O powerful prince?"

Upon hearing this, the king, who remained skilled in speech despite his transformation, folded his hands in respect and said to the sage:

"I have been rejected by my spiritual guide <note>The high priest Vasishtha</note> as well as his sons. Instead of achieving my heart's desire, I have met with this calamity. O peaceful sage, my wish was to ascend to heaven in my physical body. I have performed a hundred sacrifices, yet I have not received the rewards of my devotion. I have never told a lie in my life; and I swear by my honor as a warrior <term>Kshatriya; the social class of rulers and warriors</term> that even in these dark times, I will never do so in the future. I have performed many sacrifices, ruled my people with justice, and pleased my teachers through my good character. But now, O best of ascetics, while trying to fulfill my duty and perform a ritual, I have failed to gain the blessing of my spiritual guides. Therefore, I believe that Destiny is supreme and human action is nothing. Destiny overtakes everyone; Destiny is the ultimate path. It is for this reason that I ask for your favor. I am in deep distress, and my efforts have been ruined by Fate. I have no other path to take, nor any other refuge. It is up to you to overcome this Destiny through your own spiritual exertion."

## SECTION 59

---

**W**hen the king had finished speaking, the sage Vishwamitra <note>original: "Kuçika's son"; Vishwamitra is the son of Gadhi and grandson of Kusha (Kuçika)</note>, moved by compassion, spoke these kind words to the king who had been transformed into an outcast <term>Chandāla; historically a person of the lowest social status, often associated with impurity or the handling of the dead</term>: 

"O descendant of Ikshwāku <note>The ancient solar dynasty to which King Trishanku and later Rama belong</note>, was your journey here a good one? I recognize your great virtue. I will grant you refuge, so do not be afraid, O best of kings. I shall call upon all the Great Sages <term>Maharshis; 'Great Sages,' the highest class of seers and saints</term> to assist with the sacrifice. Then, O king, you will be able to fulfill your purpose easily. Even if this physical form, which you received through your teacher’s curse, stays with you, you shall still go to heaven in this body. Because you have come to me for protection, O lord of men, I consider heaven to be already within your grasp."

After saying this, the energetic sage ordered his virtuous and wise sons to prepare everything necessary for the sacrifice. He then summoned his disciples and said: 

"By my command, go and bring all the holy men together, including the sons of Vasishtha <note>Vishwamitra’s rival priest</note>. Bring our friends, their students, and the family priests who are experts in sacred knowledge. If anyone who is summoned should say anything against this, report their exact words of disrespect back to me."

Hearing his command, the disciples set out in different directions. Soon, many scholars of the Spirit <term>Brahmavādis; those who maintain that all things are Spirit or Brahman</term> began to arrive from various lands. 

When the disciples returned, they spoke to the sage—who was glowing with the energy of his penance—and reported the words of the scholars: 

"Hearing your message, the holy men from every region are coming, and some have already arrived. However, Mahodaya and the hundred sons of Vasishtha have refused. O foremost of ascetics, listen to the words they spoke with voices trembling with emotion: 'How can the gods and sages...'"

"...and how can gods and saints partake of offerings in the court of a man who, in addition to being an outcast <term>Chandāla; historically a person of the lowest social status, often associated with handling the dead</term>, has a member of the warrior class <term>Kshatriya; the ruling and military caste, here referring to Vishwamitra's origins</term> as his priest? And how can high-souled priests <term>Brāhmánas; the priestly and scholarly class</term>, patronized by Vishwamitra, attain heaven after eating the food of an outcast?"

These cruel words, "O powerful ascetic," did the sons of Vasishtha, together with Mahodaya <note>A son of Vasishtha or a king who joined in the mockery of Vishwamitra</note>, utter with eyes reddened by anger.

Hearing those words, that foremost of ascetics, with eyes reddened in fury, wrathfully cried:

"Since I am blameless, yet those wicked-minded ones insult me while I am practicing fierce austerities, they shall, without doubt, be reduced to ashes. And this very day, bound by the noose of Time <term>Kāla; the personification of Time and Fate</term>, meeting their destruction at the hands of the God of Death <note>original: "Vivāswata's son"; referring to Yama, the god of the dead and son of the sun-god Vivasvat</note>, they shall wander these worlds for seven hundred births. They shall be forced to scavenge for the clothes of the dead, always feeding on the flesh of dogs, and be known as *Mushtikas* <note>A group considered to be outside the social order, associated with the most impure tasks</note>; they will be devoid of any sense of decency, possessing frightening, distorted forms and practicing foul habits. And because the wicked Mahodaya also blamed me, though I do not deserve it, he shall be despised by all and live as a hunter <term>Nishāda; a person from a tribal community of hunters, traditionally viewed as outside the settled social hierarchy</term>. Becoming cruel and dedicated to taking life, he shall, through my anger, live wretchedly for a very long time."

Having uttered this in the assembly of saints, that mighty ascetic, the highly powerful Vishwamitra of fierce spiritual discipline, fell silent.

Hearing Vishwamitra's words, the pious <term>Great Sages; highly advanced spiritual practitioners known as Maharshis</term> readily spoke in accordance with their duty, saying—"This descendant of Kushika is an extremely short-tempered ascetic; therefore, what he says should undoubtedly be carried out. The worshipful one is like a fire, and if angered, he might curse us. Let us begin this ceremony." 

Saying this, the sages entered upon the ceremony; and in that sacrifice, the highly energetic Vishwamitra acted as the head priest. The <term>Sacrificial Priests; those who perform the Vedic rituals, known as Ritwijas</term> who were skilled in <term>Sacred Chants; phonetic formulas used in ritual, known as Mantras</term> performed every part of the ritual in order, following the scriptures and prescriptions. 

Then, after a long time, Vishwamitra of great spiritual power invoked all the gods to receive their designated portions of the offerings; but the deities did not appear to receive them. Thereupon, falling into a rage, the mighty ascetic Vishwamitra, lifting up a ritual ladle, spoke wrathfully to Trishanku—"O lord of men, witness now the power of the spiritual merit I have earned through my own austerities. By the strength of my asceticism, I shall take you bodily to heaven. O King, go now in your physical form to that heaven which is so difficult to reach. Some power still remains in me from my hard-earned spiritual fruits; through the energy of that asceticism, O King, travel to heaven in person."

As the ascetic said this, O <note>original: "Kākutstha"; a title for Rama meaning "descendant of Kakutstha"</note> Rama, that king ascended toward heaven in the sight of all the hermits. But beholding Trishanku rising to heaven, the <note>original: "subduer of Pāka"; an epithet for Indra</note> King of the Gods, together with all the other celestials, said—"O Trishanku, turn back. You have not earned a place in heaven. O fool, you have been ruined by the curse of your spiritual teacher. Therefore, fall back down headlong!" 

The great Indra having spoken thus, Trishanku fell toward the earth, crying out to the ascetic Vishwamitra—

Hearing those words of theirs, that greatest of hermits, Kaushika, in the company of the celestials, spoke these meaningful words: "May good fortune attend you. Let Trishanku dwell in heaven in person forever, and let these stars I have created endure as long as the worlds themselves. It is right for you, O gods, to ordain this."

Thus addressed, the deities answered that best of ascetics, saying, "So be it! May prosperity be yours! ### SECTION LXI.

AND, O powerful one, seeing those saints gone, the highly energetic <term>Vishwamitra; a celebrated sage and former king</term> addressed those inhabitants of the forest, saying—"A mighty disturbance has happened regarding the southern quarter; let us therefore repair to another region to carry on our <term>austerities; intense spiritual penances or meditation</term>. You high-souled ones, in the west there are extensive tracts of land; and there in <term>Pushkara; a holy lake and pilgrimage site</term> we will peaceably carry on our spiritual practices. That asylum is really pleasant." Having said this, that exceedingly energetic and mighty <term>Muni; a silent sage or holy man</term> <note>original footnote: Ascetic.</note> began to perform terrible penances, subsisting only on fruits and roots. 

And it came to pass that at this time that mighty lord of <term>Ayodhya; the capital city of the Kosala Kingdom</term>, <term>Ambarisha; a righteous king of the Solar Dynasty</term>, prepared for celebrating a sacrifice. And as he was sacrificing, <term>Indra; the king of the gods</term> stole away his sacrificial beast. And on the beast being stolen, the priest said unto the king—"O king, the beast has been stolen away; and it has been lost through your own neglect. And, O lord of men, his own fault destroys the king who fails to protect his subjects. And, O best of men, even this is the expiation: do you, while the ceremony lasts, speedily bring back the beast, or bring a man in its stead as a substitute." 

Hearing the priest's words, that highly intelligent king began to range through various countries and provinces, cities, forests, and holy asylums, searching for the beast, offering a thousand <term>kine; archaic term for cows or cattle</term> as the price thereof. He beheld the sage Richika seated there in the company of his wife and sons, O descendant of Raghu. And bowing before that <term>God-like Sage; a Brahmarshi, the highest class of sage who has attained divine knowledge</term> who was glowing with the power of his <term>asceticism; intense spiritual discipline</term>, and honoring him, the exceedingly energetic royal sage <note>Referring to King Ambarisha</note> of unparalleled radiance, having inquired after the sage's welfare, addressed Richika, saying—"O highly pious one, O son of Bhrigu, if you will sell your son to me to serve as a substitute for my sacrificial animal, I shall attain my desire. I have traveled through every country, but I cannot find a suitable animal. Therefore, it is right for you to give up one of your sons for a price." Thus addressed, the exceedingly energetic Richika replied—"O best of men, I will in no way part with my first-born son."

Hearing the words of the high-souled Richika, the mother of the children spoke to that foremost of men, Ambarisha, saying—"The venerable son of Bhrigu has said that his first-born cannot be sold; do you also know, O lord, that the youngest, Sunaka, is my favorite. Therefore, I will not give you my youngest son. O foremost of men, eldest sons are generally the best-beloved of their fathers, and the youngest are the favorites of their mothers; therefore, I choose to keep the youngest."

And when the ascetic and his wife had spoken thus, the second son, Sunasepha, O Rama, spoke up himself, saying—"My father will not sell the eldest, nor will my mother sell the youngest; therefore, I consider the middle son to be the one available. You, O prince, may take me." When that youth, who was well-versed in the <term>Sacred Scriptures; the Vedas, the foundational holy texts of Hindu tradition</term>, had finished speaking, that lord of men, O mighty-armed descendant of Raghu, took possession of Sunasepha. By paying <term>tens of millions; kotis, a unit of ten million</term> of <term>cows; kine, which served as a primary form of currency and wealth</term>, along with heaps of jewels and gold and silver by the hundreds and thousands, the king went away exceedingly delighted. And that royal sage, the exceedingly energetic and highly famous Ambarisha, placed Sunasepha upon his chariot and speedily set out.

"[To protect] your own sons, do you desire to surrender the son of another? This we consider a heinous act, even like eating one’s own flesh."

Hearing this speech from his sons, that greatest of hermits—his eyes reddened with anger—said:

Therefore, becoming <term>Mushtikas; a social class traditionally considered outcastes who lived outside the established Vedic social order</term> and living on the meat of dogs, may you all—just like the sons of the sage Vasishtha—inhabit the earth for a thousand years."

O son of a hermit, chant these two verses during that sacrifice of King Ambarisha. Then you will achieve success."

Thereupon, having memorized those two verses with a focused mind, Sunasepha quickly presented himself before that lion-like monarch, saying:

"O lion of a king, you who are endowed with high intelligence, let us depart from here without delay. O foremost of monarchs, engage in the sacrifice and begin the initiation ceremony."

Hearing these words from the hermit’s son, the king was filled with delight and immediately went to the sacrificial ground. With the consent of his royal court, the king bound Sunasepha with a rope made of holy grass and, dressing him in crimson clothing, tied him to the stake as the sacrificial animal. 

Once bound to the stake, the hermit’s son first correctly sang hymns to the Fire God, and then to the deities <term>Indra; the King of the Gods and ruler of the heavens</term> and his younger brother <note>original: 'Indra and his younger brother'; a reference to Upendra or Vishnu</note>.

### SECTION 63.

And when the thousand years had been completed and the mighty ascetic had accomplished his vow, the celestials in a body, desirous of conferring upon him the fruit thereof, appeared before him. And the exceedingly effulgent **Brahma** <note>The creator god in the Hindu trinity</note> addressed him in soothing words, saying,—"Thou art henceforth a <term>Rishi; a sage or saintly 'seer' who has attained a high level of spiritual truth</term>, good unto thee—and this eminence thou hast attained through thy own laudable exertions." Having spoken thus unto him, the lord of celestials returned to heaven. 

And **Vishwamitra** of mighty energy became again engaged in rigid austerities. And, O foremost of men, it came to pass that after a long lapse of time that prime of <term>Apsaras; celestial nymphs of the clouds and waters, often sent by gods to test the meditation of sages</term>, **Menaka**, was at that time performing her ablutions in **Pushkara**, and she was observed by <note>original: "Kuçika's son," an epithet for Vishwamitra</note> the son of Kushika, appearing like lightning among clouds. 

And coming under the control of <note>original: "Kandarpa," one of the many names for Kamadeva, the god of love and desire</note> the God of Love, the hermit spoke unto her, saying,—"O **Apsara**, has your journey been a pleasant one? Do you abide in my hermitage. Do me a favor; for, may good fortune attend you, I have been rendered senseless by <note>original: "Madana," another name for the god of love, meaning 'he who intoxicates'</note> the God of Desire." 

Thus addressed, that woman of shapely hips began to dwell there. And mighty was the hindrance that befell **Vishwamitra** regarding his asceticism, as she, O <term>Raghava; a title for Rama meaning 'descendant of King Raghu'</term>, staying in that hermitage of his, pleasantly spent five and five years <note>ten years</note>, O gentle one. 

And after this period had gone by, overwhelmed with shame and afflicted with anxiety and grief, the mighty ascetic **Vishwamitra** impatiently thought, O son of **Raghu**, that all this mighty loss of austerities was the work of the gods. And deprived of his senses by lust, the decade had passed away imperceptibly as if it were one day and night; and this impediment stood in—
