# BALA KANDA
### SECTION 63 (CONTINUED)

...the way of his austerities. And heaving a sigh, that best of ascetics burned in repentance. And with sweet words, renouncing the terrified and trembling **Menaka** <note>The celestial nymph who had been sent to distract him</note> standing with clasped hands, <note>original: "Kuçika’s son"</note> **Kushika’s** son, **Vishwamitra**, O **Rama**, went to the northern mountains. And practising the <term>Brahmacharya; a life of strict celibacy and spiritual study</term> mode of life with the intention of subduing lust, that highly famous one engaged in arduous austerities on the banks of the **Kaushiki** <note>A river in modern-day Bihar and Nepal, named after Vishwamitra’s own lineage</note>. 

And as he was thus engaged in profound austerities on the northern mountain, a thousand years, O **Rama** passed away. Then taking counsel together, the celestials and the saints appeared before **Brahma**, saying,—"Let **Kushika's** son obtain the title of <term>Maharshi; 'Great Seer,' a rank of high spiritual accomplishment</term>." 

Hearing the words of the celestials, the Grand-sire of all addressed the ascetic **Vishwamitra**, in these sweet words,—"O mighty saint, hast thou had a pleasant journey? Satisfied with thy fierce austerities, O **Kaushika**, I confer upon thee the eminence of the foremost saintship." 

Hearing **Brahma's** speech, the anchorite **Vishwamitra** bowing down thus answered the Grand-sire with clasped hands,—"The incomparable title of <term>Brahmarshi; the highest possible rank of sage, possessing divine knowledge and power equal to the priestly class</term> is to be won by one by performing sterling works. And since thou hast not addressed me by that title, it appears that I have not yet succeeded in subduing my senses." <note>The translator notes that the original Sanskrit text here is somewhat confused or "faulty" in its logic, as a literal reading suggests Vishwamitra claims to have subdued his senses while simultaneously acknowledging he hasn't earned the title yet.</note> 

Thereupon **Brahma** said unto him,—"Do thou exert thyself until thou conquer thy senses." Saying this, **Brahma** went to heaven. 

And when the celestials had gone, the mighty ascetic, **Vishwamitra**, with upraised arms, and without any support, and subsisting on air <note>A high stage of asceticism where the practitioner is said to live only on the breath of life</note>, began to perform penances. And in summer, the ascetic surrounded himself with five fires <note>A practice known as *Panchagni*, where the sage sits amid four fires with the sun as the fifth</note>, and in rains remained in an uncovered place, and in winter day and night stood submerged in water. Thus passed by a thousand years of terrible penances. And on the mighty...

# RAMAYANA

While the ascetic **Vishwamitra** was engaged in his intense spiritual practices, great was the alarm felt by the celestial beings and **Indra** <note>The King of the Gods, referred to in the text as Vasav</note> in particular. And **Indra** <note>original: "Sakra"</note>, together with the **Maruts** <note>Storm deities who serve as Indra's companions</note>, spoke these words to the nymph **Rambha**, seeking his own welfare and the downfall of **Vishwamitra** <note>original: "Kauçika"</note>.

### SECTION LXIV.

---

"O **Rambha**, you must perform this great service in the interest of the gods: you must entice **Vishwamitra** <note>original: "Kauçika"</note> with the sorcery of love." Thus addressed by the intelligent, thousand-eyed deity, the <term>Apsara; a celestial nymph of exquisite beauty</term>, O **Rama**, answered the leader of the gods with joined palms and great bashfulness:

"O Lord of the gods, this mighty ascetic, **Vishwamitra**, is a terrible person; without a doubt, O Divine One, he will curse me in his rising anger. O God, this is my great fear, and therefore it is right that you should show me favor."

To her, as she spoke apprehensively in her fear, the thousand-eyed one answered that trembling damsel who stood with joined hands: "Fear not, **Rambha**, may all be well with you! Do my bidding. I will assume the form of a **Koel** <note>An Indian cuckoo, whose song is a traditional symbol of longing and love</note> to captivate the heart, and I shall stay by your side in this springtime forest of graceful trees, accompanied by **Kandarpa** <note>The God of Love, also known as Kamadeva</note>. Do you, adding to your beauty with various charms, bewitch this ascetic, the son of **Kushika**, O gentle one?"

Hearing **Indra's** words, that beautiful woman of radiant smiles, heightening her charms to an extraordinary degree, inspired **Vishwamitra** with desire. He listened to the sweet melodies of the cuckoo and, with a delighted heart, he gazed upon the beautiful woman. 

Soon, however, as he listened to the warbling of the bird and the woman's own incomparable singing, and as he looked upon **Rambha**, the ascetic began to have doubts. Realizing for certain that it was the thousand-eyed deity **Indra** who had planned this whole deception, that greatest of hermits, the son of **Kushika**, was overwhelmed with anger and cursed **Rambha**.

<summary>Fearing Vishwamitra's growing spiritual power, Indra sends the nymph Rambha to seduce him. Although Indra assists by appearing as a songbird and bringing the god of love, Vishwamitra recognizes the trap and reacts with fury.</summary>

<keywords>Vishwamitra, Indra, Rambha, seduction, curse, Apsara, Koel, spiritual austerities, Hindu mythology</keywords>

--- Page 163 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Vishwamitra’s spiritual trials. On the previous page, Indra, the King of the Gods, sent the celestial nymph Rambha to distract Vishwamitra from his meditations, fearing the sage's growing power.</meta>

->**BALA KANDA**<-

saying,—"Since, O **Rambha**, you have tried to seduce me while I am striving to conquer my anger and desire, you shall, O unlucky one, remain as a stone for ten thousand years. And a highly energetic **Brahmana** <term>Brahmana; a member of the priestly class possessing supreme spiritual authority</term> equipped with spiritual power will, O **Rambha**, eventually deliver you from this state caused by my anger." Thus spoke the immensely powerful ascetic **Vishwamitra**, but he was immediately filled with regret, unable to control the lingering anger in his heart. 

And as a result of his powerful curse, **Rambha** was transformed into a stone. Upon hearing the saint's curse, both **Kandarpa** <note>The God of Love</note> and **Indra** fled the scene. And, O **Rama**, because he had given in to his anger, his senses remained untamed, and he suffered a loss of his accumulated spiritual merit <note>In Vedic tradition, 'tapas' or merit is a finite spiritual energy earned through discipline that can be depleted by outbursts of ego or anger</note>. 

Finding his spiritual power diminished, he thought to himself: "I shall no longer allow anger to control me, nor shall I speak a word to anyone. For a hundred years, I shall not even breathe; I will master my senses and wither my body through discipline. Until I reach the rank of **Brahmana** earned through my own efforts, I shall remain without food or breath for a great length of time. My body will not fail as I remain devoted to these practices." Thus, that greatest of ascetics committed himself to this supreme vow of self-denial.

### SECTION LXV.

---

"Leaving the north, the great **Muni** <term>Muni; a sage, often one who has taken a vow of silence</term>, O **Rama**, traveled to the East and began performing even more terrible austerities. Taking a vow of absolute silence for a thousand years, he began the most difficult and significant spiritual tasks. Even when those thousand years were finished, many obstacles tried to distract the great **Muni**, who stood as still as a tree trunk. Yet, he did not allow anger to enter his heart, for he was firmly determined..."

<summary>Vishwamitra curses the nymph Rambha to become a stone but regrets his anger, which cost him his spiritual merit. He then undertakes a new, even more rigorous thousand-year vow of silence and breath control to attain the status of a Brahmana.</summary>

--- Page 164 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the sage Vishwamitra’s final and most difficult trials of asceticism. After failing previous tests due to anger, he has now committed to a vow of total silence and breath control to achieve the status of a Brahmana.</meta>

->**RAMAYANA**<-

By shutting out anger, O **Rama**, he kept his asceticism from deteriorating. And, O foremost of the **Raghus** <note>The lineage or dynasty to which Rama belongs</note>, when his vow of a thousand years had been observed, that one of mighty vows became desirous of feeding on boiled rice. And it came to pass, O best of the **Raghus**, that at this time **Indra** <note>The King of the Gods</note>, assuming the guise of a holy man <term>Vipra; a member of the priestly class</term>, asked for the rice. 

Thereupon he gave it away unto the **Vipra**; and when the rice had been thus exhausted, that worshipful one of mighty austerities went without food. Nor, abiding by the vow of reticence, did he say anything unto the **Vipra**. And he then again resumed his silent guise, restraining his breath at the same time. And that puissant ascetic did not breathe for a thousand years. 

And as he restrained his breath, vapours began to issue out of his head. And, at this, the three worlds being on fire became as if afflicted with fear. And bewildered on account of the energy of his asceticism, and shorn of their brightness, and afflicted with anguish, the **Devarshis** <term>Divine Sages</term> and the **Gandharvas** <term>Celestial Musicians</term> and the **Pannagas** and the **Uragas** <note>Both terms refer to celestial serpent-beings or Nagas</note> and the **Rakshasas** <term>Demons or powerful non-human entities</term> in a body addressed the **Pitamaha** <note>original: "Pitamaha"; literally "Grandfather," a title for Brahma, the Creator, as the great father of all beings</note>, saying,—

'O divine one, various were the means by which we endeavoured to affect the mighty **Muni** <term>Muni; a sage, particularly one who has taken a vow of silence</term> **Vishwamitra** with covetousness and lust; but for all that he increases in asceticism. Nor do we perceive in him ever so little of anger or lust. And if thou do not confer upon him what his mind desires to have, he will annihilate the three worlds with all that is mobile and immobile in them. And the ten cardinal points are disconsolate: and nothing can be discovered therein. And the seas are vexed, and the mountains riven. And the earth shakes, and the winds keep steadily blowing. And, O **Brahmana**, we do not know how to remedy this. And every one is inactive like an infidel. And the three worlds look as if stupified, with their minds exceedingly exercised. And by virtue of that mighty saint's energy, the sun itself has been deprived of his splendour.'

<summary>Vishwamitra successfully overcomes a final test of hunger and anger by giving his food to a disguised Indra and maintaining a vow of silence. His subsequent thousand-year breath-holding creates such cosmic heat and energy that the gods fear the destruction of the universe and plead with Brahma to intervene.</summary>
<keywords>Vishwamitra, Indra, Brahma, asceticism, tapas, breath control, cosmic destruction, spiritual energy, Rama</keywords>

--- Page 165 ---
<meta>This page concludes the story of Vishwamitra’s legendary transformation from a warrior-king to a Brahmin-sage. Following the previous page, where his intense asceticism threatened the stability of the universe, the gods now intervene to grant him the status he has sought for thousands of years.</meta>

### BALAKANDAM
<note>The Book of Youth; the first volume of the Ramayana</note>

"Therefore, O god, against the mighty **Sage** <term>Muni; a silent holy man</term> who has set his mind upon destruction, and who is consuming the three entire worlds like the fire that rages at the end of an age, that exalted one of exceeding radiance, who resembles a flame, should be pacified. Even should he desire the dominion of the celestial regions, do thou grant him his wish."

Then the celestials, with **Brahma** <note>original: "Pitamaha"; literally "Grandfather," a title for the creator god</note> at their head, addressed the high-souled **Vishwamitra** in sweet words, saying: 

"Welcome, O **Brahmin-Sage** <term>Brahmarshi; the highest class of sage</term>! We have been well pleased with thy penances. And, O son of **Kushika**, in consideration of thy fiery asceticism, thou hast obtained the **status of a Brahmin** <term>Brahmana-hood; the priestly and most spiritually advanced social class</term>. And, O **Brahmin**, I will, in company with the **Storm Gods** <term>Maruts; deities of the wind and rain</term>, confer on thee long life. Hail unto thee! Do thou accept this; may prosperity attend thee. Go thou, O gentle one, as thou likest."

Hearing the speech of **Brahma**, the mighty ascetic, bowing down unto the celestials, said in delight: 

"If the **status of a Brahmin** has really been obtained by me together with length of days, let the **sacred syllable Om** <term>Omkāra</term>, the **ritual exclamations** <term>Vashatkāra; specialized mantras used during sacrifices</term>, and the **Sacred Scriptures** <term>Vedas</term> crown me. And let, O gods, that foremost of those versed in the **Science of the Warrior** <term>Kshatra Veda</term> as well as those cognizant of the **Science of the Spirit** <term>Brahmaveda</term>—even **Brahma’s** own son, **Vashistha**—recognize me. Having granted this prime desire of mine, do ye depart, O gods."

Then, pacified by the celestials, that best of reciters, the **Brahmin-Sage Vashistha**, made friends with **Vishwamitra**, saying, "So be it. Thou art a **Brahmin-Sage**. There is no doubt about this. Everything has been accomplished on thy behalf." Having said this, the deities went to their respective regions. 

And that **Brahmin-Sage**, the righteous **Vishwamitra** also, having attained the excellent **status of a Brahmin**, paid his homage unto that best of reciters, **Vashistha**. Having secured his end, he began to travel throughout the entire world, remaining steadfast in his asceticism. In this way, O **Rama**, was **Brahmin-hood** actually obtained by that high-souled one. This, O **Rama**, is the foremost of ascetics—this one is Asceticism incarnate."

# RAMAYANA.

"This one ever abideth by duty; and he is the stay of ascetic energy."

Having said this, that **best of the twice-born** <note>original: "regenerate persons"; referring to Brahmins who have undergone spiritual rebirth through initiation</note> paused. Hearing **Satananda's** <note>The high priest of Mithila and son of the sage Gautama</note> narration delivered in the presence of **Rama** and **Lakshmana**, **Janaka** with clasped hands addressed the son of **Kushika** <note>Vishwamitra</note>, saying,—

> "Blessed and favored am I, that thou, O **Kaushika**, accompanied by **Rama** <note>original: "Kakutstha"; a title for Rama as a descendant of King Kakutstha</note>, hast arrived at my sacrifice, O puissant anchoret. Purified am I, O **Brahmin** <term>Brahmana; a member of the priestly and scholarly class</term>, by thy very sight, O mighty **Sage** <term>Muni; a silent holy man or inspired saint</term>. And from thy sight have I received various qualities. O **Brahmin**, thy mighty austerities have been related in detail; and myself as well as the high-souled **Rama** have listened to the narration relative to thy formidable ascetic energy; and the assembled courtiers have heard of thy various perfections. Immeasurable is thy asceticism; and immeasurable thy power; and ever immeasurable thy qualities, O **Kushika’s** son. I never, O lord, am tired of listening to that wonderful narration. Now, O foremost of ascetics, the hour for performing the daily devotions hath arrived, and the sun is setting <note>original: "the solar disc hangeth aslope"</note>. To-morrow morning, O highly energetic one, thou wilt see me again. Welcome, best of reciters. It behoveth thee to favor me."

Thus addressed, that best of ascetics, extolling that powerful one, well pleased, dismissed the delighted **Janaka**. Thus accosted, **Mithila’s** lord, the **King of Videha** <note>original: "Vaideha"; a title for Janaka based on his kingdom</note>, in company with his priests and friends, went round <note>original: "went round that foremost of ascetics"; the practice of Pradakshina, or walking clockwise around a holy person as a sign of respect</note> that foremost of ascetics. And the righteous **Vishwamitra** also together with **Rama** and **Lakshmana**, having been honored by the high-souled ones, took up their quarters there.

Formerly, with the view of destroying **Daksha’s sacrifice** <note>A famous mythological event where the god Shiva was insulted by being excluded from a grand ritual, leading to his divine wrath</note>, the puissant **Shiva** <note>original: "Siva"</note>, drawing this bow, sportively spoke unto the celestials in ire, saying,—

> ‘Since, ye gods, ye deny me the shares of this sacrifice which I lay claim to, I will with my bow even sever those heads of yours.’

Thereat, O powerful ascetic, with agitated hearts, the deities fell to propitiating that lord of the celestials—and **Bhava** <note>Another name for Shiva, meaning "the Source" or "Existence"</note> was pleased with them. And well-pleased with them, he conferred this bow upon those high-souled ones. And even this is that jewel of a bow belonging to the high-souled god of gods, and which was ultimately, O lord, consigned as a trust unto our ancestor.

And unsprung from the usual source <note>meaning she was not born from a womb, but was of divine or miraculous origin</note>, she was then established here as my daughter, whose hand must be obtained by bending the bow. And those potent princes, conceiving themselves as frustrated by me, began to harass the city of **Mithilā**, waxing wondrous wroth. 

And when a year had been completed, my defensive resources were entirely exhausted—and at this, I was exceedingly aggrieved. Then I sought to propitiate the deities; and well-pleased, the celestials granted me a **four-fold army** <term>Chaturanga; a traditional military formation consisting of four divisions: chariots, elephants, cavalry, and infantry</term>. At length those wicked kings, meeting with slaughter, broke and fled in all directions, together with their adherents, bereft of vigor and confidence in their strength. 

And, O puissant ascetic, this highly effulgent bow will I show unto **Rāma** and **Lakshmana**, O thou of excellent vows. And, if, O ascetic, **Rāma** succeeds in fixing the string to it, I will confer upon **Dasharatha’s** <note>original: "Daçarātha"</note> son my daughter **Sītā**, unsprung from the usual source <note>original: "ayonijā"; meaning "not born from a womb," emphasizing her divine and miraculous origin from the earth</note>.

### SECTION LXVII.

---

Upon hearing Janaka’s words, the mighty **Sage** <term>Muni; a silent holy man or inspired saint</term> Vishwamitra said to the king, “I pray you, show the bow to Rama.” Thereupon, King Janaka commanded his ministers, saying, “Bring forth the wonderful bow, adorned with scented ointments and floral garlands.”

Obeying Janaka’s command, the ministers entered the city. Leading the way for the bow, these men—possessing immeasurable energy—emerged. The bow was kept in an iron case resting upon a cart with eight wheels; it was so massive that it required five thousand strong, well-built men to struggle to pull it along. Having brought the iron chest containing the bow, the royal counselors spoke to Janaka, who appeared like an immortal god, saying, “Here is the greatest of bows, O King, worshipped by all sovereigns. O foremost of kings, O Lord of **Mithila**, if you deem it fit to be shown, then show it.”

Hearing their words, the King, with palms pressed together in respect, spoke to the high-souled Vishwamitra as well as to Rama and Lakshmana:

“This, the best of bows, O **Priest** <term>Brahmana; a member of the social class of priests and scholars</term>, has always been worshipped by the line of Janakas, as well as by many mighty kings who were nonetheless unable to wield or string it. Neither the Gods, nor the **Demons** <term>Asuras; powerful beings often in opposition to the gods</term>, nor the **Ogres** <term>Rakshasas; shapeshifting spirits or man-eaters</term>, nor the **Celestial Musicians** <term>Gandharvas; heavenly singers and musicians</term>, nor the **Nature Spirits** <term>Yakshas; guardians of natural treasures</term>, nor the **Heavenly Beings** <term>Kinnaras; celestial creatures often depicted as half-human</term>, nor the **Mighty Serpents** <term>Uragas; divine serpent-beings or Nagas</term>—how then shall mere men fare? None have succeeded in stringing it, stretching it, fixing an arrow to it, pulling its string, or even holding it up. This, the greatest of bows, has been brought here, O chief of ascetics. Do you, O exalted one, show it to these sons of the King.”

Hearing Janaka’s words, the righteous Vishwamitra said to Rama <note>original: "Raghava"; an epithet for Rama meaning 'descendant of King Raghu'</note>, “O Rama, my child, look upon the bow.”

At the words of the **Great Sage** <term>Maharshi; a title for the highest rank of Hindu sages</term>, Rama opened the case where the bow lay. After looking at it, he said, “I shall touch this divine bow with my hand—and shall I also attempt to lift and draw it?”

To this, both the...

# RĀMĀYANA

The king and the **ascetic** <term>Vishwamitra; here referred to as an anchorite or hermit</term> both said, “Excellent, let it be so.” At the words of the sage, and before the eyes of countless thousands of spectators, the righteous **son of Raghu** <note>Rama, descendant of the solar dynasty founder Raghu</note> with great ease took hold of the bow by its middle. He fixed the string upon it and, having secured it, drew the bow back. That foremost of men, who enjoyed high fame, snapped the bow in the middle. 

A mighty sound was heard at that moment, like the bursting of a thunder-clap; the earth trembled terribly, as it does near a mountain that is splitting apart. Overwhelmed by the noise, everyone present fell head over heels, with the exception of that best of sages, the king, and the two **Rāghavas** <note>The brothers Rama and Lakshmana</note>. 

When the people had recovered and were reassured, the king—who was a master of speech and whose anxieties were now removed—addressed the powerful ascetic with folded hands, saying:

“O worshipful one, I have witnessed the prowess of **Dasharatha’s** son. This is truly wonderful and inconceivable; I did not believe such a thing was possible. My daughter, **Sita**, being united with her lord, Rama, will bring great glory to the line of Janaka. My promise—that I would give Sita to whoever could bend the bow—has been fulfilled, O **son of Kushika** <note>An epithet for the sage Vishwamitra</note>. 

This Sita, my daughter who is dearer to me than life itself, I shall confer upon Rama. Therefore, O **Brahmana** <term>A member of the priestly and scholarly class</term>, with your permission, let my counselors travel quickly from here to **Ayodhya** <note>The capital city of Rama’s father, King Dasharatha</note> in chariots. With humble words, let them bring the King to my capital. Let them inform him of the bestowal of Sita upon Rama following the bending of the bow. Let them acquaint the monarch with the well-being of the **Kakutsthas** <note>Another name for the descendants of King Kakutstha, referring here to Rama and Lakshmana</note> who are under your protection; let them hurry and bring the delighted King here.”

Thereupon, the son of Kushika replied, “So be it.” The righteous king, summoning his counselors, dispatched them to Ayodhya with a letter to communicate everything properly to the King and bring him back to the city of Mithila.

# RĀMĀYANA

### SECTION LXVIII.

---

Thus commissioned by **Janaka** <note>The King of Mithila and father of Sita</note>, the envoys, having spent three nights on the way, entered the city of **Ayodhya**, with their horses and chariots exhausted by the journey. In accordance with the royal command, they entered the king’s residence and saw the aged king **Dasharatha**, who appeared as majestic as a **celestial** <term>A god-like or divine being</term>. Freed from any anxiety, the envoys addressed the monarch with hands clasped in respect, speaking in sweet and humble tones:

“O mighty monarch, **Mithila’s** lord, King Janaka, together with his priests, sends sweet and affectionate words to repeatedly inquire after the welfare of yourself, your priests, and your servants. After inquiring about your well-being, the King of the **Vaidehas** <note>Vaideha is a title for Janaka, meaning 'Lord of the Videha kingdom'</note>, with the permission of **Kaushika** <note>An epithet for the sage Vishwamitra, referring to his ancestry from King Kusha</note>, addresses you thus:

‘Thou knowest the vow I made formerly—*namely*, to confer my daughter upon him who could bend the bow. Many kings, lacking sufficient **prowess** <term>Valor or extraordinary heroic strength</term> and being thus defeated, have come to hold a grudge against me. But that daughter of mine, O King, has been won by your son, who arrived here of his own will led by **Vishwamitra**. O mighty-armed one, that divine, jewel-encrusted bow was snapped in the middle by the high-souled **Rama** in the midst of a great assembly. 

To that high-souled one I must confer **Sita**, for she has **prowess assigned as her dower** <note>original: "vīrya-shulkā"; a bride-price paid not in wealth, but in a display of heroic strength</note>. In this way, I shall free myself from my vow; and this you should permit. O mighty King, may good fortune attend you; come quickly, led by your priests. It is right that you should see the **Raghavas** <note>The descendants of Raghu; here referring to Rama and Lakshmana</note>, and, O foremost of kings, see me delivered from my vow. May you also experience the joy of the nuptials of both your sons.’ 

Thus spoke sweetly the lord of **Videha**, with the permission of Vishwamitra and following the counsel of **Shatananda** <note>The head priest of King Janaka’s court</note>.”

Upon hearing the report from the envoys, the king, exceedingly rejoiced, addressed **Vasishtha** and **Vāmadeva** <note>King Dasharatha’s primary spiritual advisors and royal priests</note>, as well as his other counsellors, saying: “Protected by **Kuçika’s son** <note>The sage Vishwamitra, who is a descendant of King Kusha</note>, that **enhancer of Kauçalyā’s joy** <note>A poetic reference to Rama, the son of Queen Kausalya</note> stayeth in **Videha** <note>The kingdom ruled by Janaka; its capital is Mithila</note> in company with his brother **Lakshmana**. And the high-souled **Janaka** hath witnessed the prowess of **Kākutstha** <note>An epithet for Rama, meaning "descendant of Kakutstha"</note>; and he wisheth to bestow his daughter upon **Rāghava** <note>An epithet for Rama, meaning "descendant of Raghu"</note>. If this alliance with the high-souled Janaka is relished by ye, we shall speedily repair to his capital. Let there be no waste of time.” 

Thereupon, the counsellors along with the **Maharshis** <term>Great Sages</term> said, “Excellent!” And the king, highly delighted, said unto the counsellors, “Our journey commenceth on the morrow.” And excellently ministered unto, the counsellors of that **foremost of monarchs (Janaka)** <note>This refers to Janaka’s messengers, who were treated as honored guests</note>, endowed with every excellent quality, spent that night in joy.

---

### SECTION LXIX.

Then, when the night had passed, King **Daçaratha**, accompanied by his priests and adherents, spoke well-pleased unto **Sumantra** <note>The king’s chief charioteer and trusted minister</note>, saying: “To-day, let the officers in charge of the treasury, taking plenty of excellent wealth and furnished with various gems, go in advance under proper escort. And let the **four-fold forces** <term>The traditional divisions of an ancient Indian army: infantry, cavalry, chariots, and elephants</term> sally out with speed. And at my command, let horses and conveyances and elegant vehicles march out. 

And let **Vasishtha** and **Vāmadeva** and **Jāvāli** and **Kāçyapa** and **Mārkandeya**—endowed with long life—and the saint **Kātyāyana**; let these **regenerate ones** <term>Literally "twice-born," referring here to the high-ranking sages and priests</term> go forward. And do thou also yoke my car. Let not the proper time pass away, for the envoys urge speed upon me.” At these words of the king, the four-fold forces together with the saints went in the wake of the monarch. And after having passed four days on the way, they arrived at **Videha**.

-># THE BOOK OF YOUTH #<-

->### SECTION LXX ###<-

Upon hearing of **Dasharatha’s** arrival, the auspicious King **Janaka** experienced great delight. Having received the aged King Dasharatha, he honored him with due ceremony. <note>The text notes that upon hearing of the arrival, Janaka honored Dasharatha duly; having obtained the aged monarch, he was filled with an excess of joy.</note> 

That best of men, Janaka, then spoke words to the delighted chief of men: “Was your journey pleasant, O best of men? By good fortune have I received you, O descendant of **Raghu** <term>Raghu: An illustrious ancestor of Rama; his descendants are called the Raghavas</term>. May you now experience the joy earned by the heroic prowess of your sons. By good fortune, I have also received the highly energetic and venerable sage **Vasishtha**, accompanied by the foremost among the **twice-born** <note>original: "regenerate ones"—referring to the Brahmins or sages who have undergone a spiritual rebirth</note>, just as the **King of the Gods** <note>original: "him of an hundred sacrifices," an epithet for Indra</note> is accompanied by the **deities** <note>original: "celestials"</note>. 

“By good fortune, I have overcome all obstacles; by good fortune, my lineage has attained great renown through this alliance with the exceedingly powerful **Raghavas** <note>The descendants of King Raghu, referring here to Rama and his brothers</note>. O Lord of Men, tomorrow morning, after the completion of the sacrifice, please perform the weddings in the company of the foremost of the sages.”

Hearing this speech in the midst of the sages, that king—the best among those eloquent in speech—replied to the monarch, saying: “A gift should be accepted—this is a truth I heard long ago. Therefore, whatever you say, O you who are cognizant of duty, we shall accomplish.”

Hearing these words from the truthful King Dasharatha—words which aligned with morality and were conducive to fame—the Lord of **Videha** <note>The kingdom ruled by Janaka</note> was filled with admiration. Then the ascetics, experiencing great delight, passed the night happily in each other's company. 

The King, overjoyed at seeing his sons—the **Raghavas**—spent the night in exceeding delight, having been splendidly entertained by Janaka. And the exceedingly energetic Janaka as well, being well-versed in the nature of men and things, completed the sacrifice according to the sacred ordinances. Having performed all the preliminary rites for the weddings of his daughters, he also reposed for the night.

->### SECTION LXX ###<-
---

**THEN** the next morning, **Janaka**, who was skilled in speech, having performed his daily religious duties in the company of the **Maharshis** <term>Maharshis: 'Great Sages'—the highest class of seers and saints in the Vedic tradition</term>, addressed his priest **Satānanda**, saying:

"My highly energetic, powerful, and eminently righteous brother, known by the name of **Kuçadhwaja**, dwells in the auspicious city of **Sānkāçyā**. Its ramparts are lined with pointed weapons, it is laved by the river **Ikshumati**, and it resembles the celestial regions or the **Pushpaka** <note>The Pushpaka was a divine, self-moving aerial chariot or palace belonging to the gods, symbolizing great wealth and splendor</note>. I wish to see him, for he is in charge of my sacrifice. That highly energetic one shall partake with me in the joy of this occasion."

After he spoke to Satānanda, several competent messengers presented themselves, and Janaka commanded them to set out for Sānkāçyā. Following the monarch's command, they departed on fleet horses to bring that best of men—who was like **Vishnu** <note>The supreme protector deity in the Hindu trinity</note> at the mandate of **Indra** <note>The King of the Heavens and the gods</note>.

Arriving at Sānkāçyā, they appeared before Kuçadhwaja and faithfully delivered Janaka's message. Upon hearing the news from those swift envoys, Kuçadhwaja set out at the king's command. When he arrived in **Videha** <note>The kingdom ruled by Janaka, famous for its spiritual learning</note>, he appeared before the high-souled and righteous Janaka. After saluting Satānanda and his virtuous brother Janaka, he sat upon an excellent, superb seat worthy of a king.

Once they were both seated, the two heroic brothers of immeasurable splendor addressed the foremost counselor, **Sudāmana**, saying: "Go, chief of counselors, and quickly bring here the irrepressible **Ikshwāku** <note>A title for King Dasharatha, referring to his descent from the solar dynasty of King Ikshwāku</note> of immeasurable splendor, along with his sons and his ministers."

Thereupon, repairing to the camp, Sudāmana saw that enhancer of the race of the **Raghus** <note>The lineage of King Raghu, to which Rama belongs</note>. Saluting him with a bowed head, he addressed him: "O heroic lord of **Ayodhyā**, the King of **Videha** <note>original: "Vaideha"—referring to Janaka</note>..."

From Kashyapa sprang **Vivaswat** <note>The Sun god</note>; and **Manu** is the son of Vivaswat. <note>original footnote: The Bengal version of the text reads: From Marichi sprang Angiras; and his son was Prachetas; and Manu is Prachetas' son.</note> This Manu is otherwise called **Prajapati** <term>Prajapati: 'Lord of Creatures,' a title for the progenitors of humankind</term>; and **Ikshvaku** is Manu's son. And Ikshvaku's son, as is well known, was the graceful **Kukshi**. Taking refuge in the **Himalayas** <note>original: "Himavat"</note> with his two wives, the weakened **Asita** eventually passed away <note>literally: "paid his debt to nature"</note>. 

The story is told that both his wives were pregnant at the time. With the intention of destroying the other's unborn child, one of the wives administered poison to the other by mixing it into her food. It so happened that at this time, the son of **Bhrigu**, the ascetic **Chyavana**, was living in the beautiful **Himalayas**—the greatest of all mountains. There, one of these noble ladies, whose eyes were as beautiful as lotus petals, saluted the son of **Bhrigu**, who shone with celestial radiance, and begged him for an excellent son. 

Approaching the sage, **Kalindi** paid her respects. That <term>Vipra: A wise man or learned priest</term> said to her, as she longed for a child: "In your womb, O noble lady, an excellent son will soon be born. He will be mixed with poison, yet highly powerful, exceedingly energetic, possessed of mighty strength, and graceful. Therefore, do not grieve, O lotus-eyed one." 

Having shown reverence to **Chyavana**, that chaste and worshipful princess—though she had lost her husband—gave birth to a son. Because her co-wife had administered poison to her intending to destroy the fetus, the child was named **Sagara** <note>original: "Sagara." From the Sanskrit *Sa* (with) and *Gara* (poison), meaning "one born with poison."</note>.

### SECTION LXXI

---

When **Vasishtha** had spoken thus, **Janaka** with clasped hands answered him, saying: "It is only right that you listen to our genealogy as related by myself. And his son was named **Mithi**, and **Mithi's** son was **Janaka** <note>This is an earlier ancestor also named Janaka, not the current speaker.</note>. And from this...

-># RAMAYANA #<-

...who belong to noble families. From the first King **Janaka** <note>original: "Janaka"; while it is his personal name, it became a dynastic title for all the kings of Mithila. The name literally means "producer" or "father."</note> we have derived that title which is now applied to every one of us. And from **Janaka** was born **Udavasu**; and **Udavasu's** son was the pious-souled **Nandivardhana**. And **Nandivardhana's** son was the heroic **Suketu**. And **Sudhriti's** son was the pious-spirited and eminently righteous **Dhritaketu**. And the <term>Rajarshi</term> **Dhritaketu's** son was **Haryyashya**. And **Haryyashya's** son was **Maru**; and **Maru's** son was **Pratindhaka**. And **Pratindhaka's** son was the righteous king **Kirtiratha**. And **Kirtiratha's** son was **Devamirha**, and **Devamirha's** was **Vibudha**, and **Vibudha's** was **Mahidhraka**. And **Mahidhraka's** son was king **Kirtiratha** <note>Note that this name repeats in the lineage; he is also referred to as Kirtirata II</note> who was endowed with great strength. And the <term>Rajarshi</term> **Kirtiratha** had **Maharoma** born unto him; and from **Maharoma** came the virtuous **Swarnaroma**. And the <term>Rajarshi</term> **Swarnaroma** had **Hraswaroma** born unto him. 

And this high-souled king, who was well-acquainted with morality, had two sons: the elder was myself, and the younger was my brother, the heroic **Kushadhwaja**. And after installing me, the elder son, upon the throne and entrusting **Kushadhwaja** to my care, our father retired to the forest <note>In ancient Indian tradition, this refers to the 'Vanaprastha' stage of life, where a king leaves his worldly duties to focus on spiritual meditation.</note>. And when my aged father ascended to heaven <note>A respectful way of saying he passed away.</note>, I ruled the kingdom righteously and looked after my brother **Kushadhwaja**, who resembles a celestial being, with the eye of deep affection. 

And it came to pass that on one occasion, a certain powerful king named **Sudhanwa** came from the city of **Sankasya** to the gates of **Mithila** <note>The capital city of King Janaka's kingdom, Videha.</note> intending to lay siege to it. And he sent word to me, saying: "You must give me the all-excellent bow of **Shiva** <note>The mighty celestial bow which is the centerpiece of the challenge to win Sita's hand; it was given to the ancestors of Janaka by the gods for safekeeping.</note>, as well as your daughter, the lotus-eyed **Sita**." And because I would not grant him either, king **Sudhanwa**, O <term>Brahmarshi: A "Brahman-Sage," the highest class of sage who possesses divine knowledge</term>, went to war with me; but he was both defeated and slain by me in that encounter. And, O foremost of ascetics, after slaying king **Sudhanwa**, I installed my heroic brother in the city of **Sankasya**...

<summary>King Janaka recites his royal lineage, tracing his descent from the original King Janaka down to himself and his brother Kushadhwaja. He also recounts his victory over King Sudhanwa, who had unsuccessfully demanded the Bow of Shiva and the hand of Sita.</summary>

-># THE YOUTHFUL BOOK #<-
->### (Bālakāṇḍam) ###<-

**Kushadhwaja** <note>King Janaka's younger brother, who ruled the city of Sankasya.</note> is here; this man, O mighty hermit, is my younger brother, and I am his elder. O powerful ascetic, it is with great joy that I confer these two upon you as your daughters-in-law—**Sita** to **Rama**, may blessings attend you, and **Urmila** to **Lakshmana**. I take an oath three times that, without any doubt and with a glad heart, I will grant to you, O potent ascetic, as your daughters-in-law my second daughter **Urmila** and also **Sita**, who resembles a daughter of the gods and whose dowry was the trial of strength <note>original: "prowess assigned for her dower"; this refers to Janaka's vow to marry Sita only to the man who could lift and string the massive Bow of Shiva.</note>. 

Do you now, O King, perform the ceremony of <term>Godana: A rite of passage marking the transition to adulthood, involving the first ritual shaving of the hair or beard and the gifting of cows to priests</term> for the nuptials of **Rama** and **Lakshmana**. Perform also their ancestral rites—may it be well with you—and then complete the marriage ceremony. Today the star <term>Magha: An auspicious lunar mansion (Nakshatra) associated with ancestors and royal power</term> is in the ascendant. On the third day from now, my master, when the <term>Phalguna: Specifically the Uttara-Phalguni constellation, which is traditionally considered highly favorable for marriage ceremonies</term> is to the north, do you, O Monarch, perform the marriage ceremony. For now, however, distribute gifts to invoke blessings upon **Rama** and **Lakshmana**.”

### SECTION 72

---

When **Vaideha** <note>A title for King Janaka, meaning "the King of Videha."</note> had spoken thus, the mighty ascetic **Vishwamitra**, in the company of **Vasishtha**, addressed that heroic king, saying: 

“O powerful one, the lineages of the **Ikshvakus** <note>The solar dynasty to which King Dasharatha and Rama belong.</note> and the **Vaidehas** are exceedingly noble and beyond compare. No other race can match them in any way. O Monarch, this union of the youthful **Rama** and **Lakshmana** with **Sita** and **Urmila** is entirely fitting; it is worthy of their great beauty and grace. 

Now, O foremost of men, listen to my words. This younger brother of yours, King **Kushadhwaja**, who is well-versed in morality and pious of soul—he, O King, has a pair of daughters whose beauty is unparalleled on this earth. We would ask for them to become the wives of Prince **Bharata** and the intelligent **Shatrughna** <note>The two younger brothers of Rama and Lakshmana.</note>, just as we have asked for your own daughters in the interest of those who belong to such noble families.”

...the high-souled ones <note>referring to Rama and Lakshmana</note>. Having spoken these kind words, King **Janaka** stood up and, with hands clasped, addressed both of the leading sages, saying: "I have gained great spiritual merit through these weddings, and I consider myself your disciple. Please, you holy men, take these royal thrones belonging to us. And just as this kingdom belongs to **Dasharatha**, so too is **Ayodhya** <note>The capital city of the Ikshwaku kings</note> my own home. Do not have any doubt that you hold authority here. Proceed, therefore, as is proper."

As **Janaka**, the King of the **Videhas**, was speaking in this way, **Raghu’s** descendant, King **Dasharatha**, replied to that monarch with great pleasure, saying: "Countless are the virtues that belong to you two brothers, the lords of **Mithila**; holy men and kings are always honored by you,* may prosperity be yours. May you be blessed; I will now return to my own quarters to perform..."

* <note>The original translator, Manmatha Nath Dutt, notes: The traditional commentator seems to be mistaken here. He interprets the text as "By you have your royal ancestors been honored," but I disagree. The use of the word *cha* (and) makes it clear that Janaka is the one currently showing honor to the visiting sages and kings.</note>

### SECTION LXXII.

---

And it came to pass that on the day the king made these excellent presents of cows, the heroic **Yudhajit**—son of the lord of the **Kekayas** and the maternal uncle of **Bharata**—presented himself before **Dasharatha**. Having seen the king and inquired after his well-being, he said to him:

"The lord of the **Kekayas** has, out of affection, inquired after your welfare, saying, 'Those for whose peace you are anxious are currently well.' And, O foremost of kings, desiring to see my nephew **Bharata**, that lord of the earth traveled to **Ayodhya**, O descendant of **Raghu**. Learning at **Ayodhya** that your sons had come to **Mithila** with yourself for the purpose of marriage, O monarch, I have quickly hurried here with the intention of seeing my sister's son."

Then at an auspicious moment called **Vijaya** <term>Vijaya: A specific period or 'muhurta' in the Hindu calendar believed to ensure victory and the successful completion of any task begun within it.</term>, **Rama** <note>original: "Rāma"</note> with **Vashistha** <note>original: "Vasishtha"</note> as well as other **Maharshis** <term>Maharshis: 'Great Sages' or seers who have attained the highest spiritual realization.</term> at his head, and accompanied by his brothers adorned with various ornaments, who had all performed the rites relative to their nuptials, approached the entrance of the sacrificial ground. Do thou therefore maintain thy merit by accomplishing this excellent nuptial ceremony.”

Thus addressed by the high-souled **Vashistha**, that exceedingly generous and energetic one versed in morality <note>King Janaka</note> answered, saying—

Do thou perform everything without let <note>Without hindrance or delay.</note>. What need of delaying further?”

->**BALAKANDAM**<-

With these gifts, that king, the lord of **Mithila** <note>Janaka</note>, bidding adieu to the monarch **Dasharatha** <note>original: "Daçaratha"</note>, re-entered his own capital. And the master of **Ayodhya**, accompanied by his high-souled sons and led by the company of saints, started on the journey, followed by his army and attendants. And as that tiger-like king was on his way, in the company of **Rama** <note>original: "Rāghava," a title meaning 'descendant of Raghu'</note> and the saints, the birds began to utter frightful cries all around, and the beasts on the earth began to move in a Southern direction <note>In ancient Indian tradition, animals moving south was often considered an omen of impending death or danger</note>. And beholding them, that tiger-like monarch asked **Vashistha** <note>original: "Vasishtha"</note>, saying:—

> “Those birds of fierce aspect emit frightful cries and the beasts move in a Southerly direction. What is this? My heart trembles and my mind is not at ease.”

Hearing the speech of King Dasharatha, that mighty saint spoke sweetly, saying:—

> “Hear what the result of this shall be. These birds of the air, by their cries, presage some dreadful impending evil; but these beasts betoken peace restored. Therefore, do thou renounce thy anxiety.”

And as they were thus conversing, there blew a strong wind, shaking all the earth and breaking down the trees. And a deep gloom enveloped the sun, so that no direction could be discerned. Enveloped in ashes, that entire host became stunned. And at that dreadful hour, Vashistha and the other saints, along with the king and his sons, alone retained their senses; all others were deprived of their senses as the army was covered in clouds of dust. 

Then the king saw that repressor of kings, the descendant of **Bhrigu** <note>a famous lineage of sages</note>, the son of **Jamadagni** <note>this refers to Parashurama, a warrior-sage and avatar of Vishnu</note>, who was dreadful to behold. He wore his hair in matted locks and appeared as unyielding as Mount **Kailasa** <note>original: "Kailāça"; the sacred mountain home of the god Shiva</note>. He was as unbearable as the fire at the time of the universal dissolution, flaming with energy and impossible for the unrighteous to look upon. With his axe on his shoulder and equipped with a bow like a bolt of lightning and fierce arrows, he looked like the god **Shiva** <note>original: "Çiva"</note>, the slayer of **Tripura** <note>a mythological city of three fortresses destroyed by Shiva</note>. And beholding him of such dreadful appearance, like a flaming fire, the **Brahmins** <term>Vipras: members of the priestly caste</term> headed by **Vishwamitra** <note>original: "Viçvāmitra"</note>—who were engaged in reciting **sacred chants** <term>mantras: Vedic hymns or prayers</term> and offering oblations into the fire—those assembled saints began to converse with one another:—

In the past, having slaughtered the warriors, he pacified his anger and mental turmoil—therefore, to annihilate the warriors once again can never be his goal.” Having said this, the saints offered the ritual guest-offering <term>Arghya <gloss>a traditional reception offering of water and respect</gloss></term> unto the son of Bhrigu <note>This refers to Parashurama, a powerful immortal sage who famously eradicated the warrior class 21 times to avenge his father's murder</note> who possessed a dreadful appearance; and they addressed him in soothing words, saying, “O Rama! O Rama!” Accepting that homage rendered unto him by the saints, that powerful one, the son of Jamadagni, addressed Rama, the son of Dasharatha <note>The protagonist of our story</note>.

### SECTION LXXV.
---

“O Rama, son of Dasharatha, I have, O hero, heard of your wonderful prowess; and I have also heard all about your breaking of the bow <note>The great bow of Shiva which Rama broke at King Janaka's court</note>. And having heard of that wonderful and inconceivable breaking of the bow, I have made my way here, bringing another auspicious bow. Do you stretch it and fix an arrow upon this mighty and dreadful bow, which belonged to Jamadagni <note>Parashurama’s father</note>; and in this way display your prowess. Then, having witnessed your might in stretching the bow, I shall offer you combat, where we shall both use our utmost strength.” 

Hearing his words, King Dasharatha with a pale face and hands clasped in prayer, said, “You have already quenched your anger against the warriors; and, moreover, you are a priest <term>Brahmana <gloss>the scholarly and priestly social class</gloss></term> boasting of high spiritual austerities. It therefore is right for you to ease the fears of my sons, who are still boys. “Mahendra hill is your home. > “These two foremost of bows, extraordinary, and worshipped by all the worlds, and stout, and powerful, surpassingly excellent, were constructed with care by <term>Vishwakarma <gloss>the divine architect and craftsman of the gods</gloss></term>. >

“Richika made over the divine bow unto his son of immeasurable prowess, my sire the high-souled son of Jamadagni. And once on a time, while my sire was surcharged with ascetic energy <note>referring to the spiritual power gained through intense meditation and self-discipline</note> and had renounced the bow, Arjuna <note>This is Kartavirya Arjuna, a thousand-armed king, not the Arjuna from the Mahabharata</note>, under the influence of unrighteous sentiment, brought about the death of my father. 

Thereupon, learning of the lamentable and untoward slaughter of my sire, I, from ire, annihilated the <term>Kshatriyas <gloss>the warrior and ruling caste</gloss></term>, who kept springing up afresh in great numbers. Then, bringing under my sway the whole earth, I, O Rāma, when the sacrifice was over, conferred the land upon the righteous Kaçyapa as *Dakshina* <gloss>a ritual gift or fee given to a priest</gloss>. Having made this gift, I was dwelling on the Mahendra hill equipped with ascetic energy, when, hearing of thy snapping of the bow, I have speedily come hither. 

Do thou now, O Rāma, agreeably to the laws of <term>Kshatriya morality <gloss>the code of conduct for warriors, known as Kshatriya Dharma</gloss></term>, take this excellent and mighty bow of Vishnu, which had belonged to my father and grandfather. And do thou set upon this best of bows an arrow capable of conquering hostile cities. And, O Kakutstha <note>An epithet for Rama, meaning 'descendant of Kakutstha'</note>, if thou succeed, I shall then offer thee combat.”

Jamadagni’s son, Rāma <note>This refers to Parashurama</note>, saying,—“Thou art a <term>Brahmana <gloss>a member of the priestly and scholarly caste</gloss></term> and through Viçwāmitra <note>The sage who acted as Rama's mentor</note>, art worthy of my homage.
